PRACTICAL

POULTRY FARMING.

[BY REDCAP].

THE ILLUSTRATIONS.

This week I purpose giving you two illustrations. Next week will be my last for this season. I shall have a good deal to say in next week's column. Therefore even if I do re-produce one of the blocks it will only be as a relief to the column. At any rate I shall not be able to recopy any of the space with anything beyond the mere picture.

===== 0002601_19120622_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
JAPANESE SILKIES.

The above picture is a fine illustration of a Japanese Silkie cock. Now these beautiful little fowls are not commonly known in the Northern counties; princi- pally because they do not come under the heading of general utility. Nevertheless they are very useful indeed. They are not at all bad layers and their flesh, although covered with a very daik skin (somewhat like a Ptarmigan) is of very good quality. Nearly every individual breed of poultry have some special quality. Silkies are noted fcr their usefulness as careful sitters. They are very docile and make very careful mothers, and as a consequence are much sought after by those who incubate eggs by natural methods. They are used chiefly for sitting valuable bantam and pheasant eggs. The silkie is rather a quaint looking little fowl yet very beautiful. Their plumage is not like the ordinary fowl. The -body is covered with what looks like one mass of swan's down, and because of this they must undoubtedly be very useful as sitters. They are able because of this natural" dress" to keep a more even temperature on the eggs than a ben not so well garbed. I have purposely written at length on the above variety because they are not half so well known as they ought to be. They are a very favourite breed with the writer and owing to lack f better accom- modation they are the only breed I can keep at the present time (I have however none to sell). Those who live in the suburbs and who have none bit) much space may well give the Silkie a trial. When once kept I feel sure they would never be giveri up. Their precociousness endears one to them, and with children they become great -

PAIR OF DUCRIVING YOKOITANIAS.

Quite a picture gallery this week! The above again, will be something new to many readers. It is not often that you see any Yokohamas for sale in Warrington market. These towls are noted chiefly for their v.onderful plumage. A more common name than Yokohamas is Japanese Longtails taken of course from the male bird's very long tail feathers. These fowls can only be kept where the accommodation is suitable. 'When running on a green lawn they look very beautiful indeed. I have heard very good reports as to the laying qualities of the Yokohama and I am told that they carry a fair amount of good quality flesh. I can hardly zecommend my readers to cater for these fowls. If you want something near you can get a replica of them in the Yokohama Bantam.

===== 0002601_19120622_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
SOME COMMON DISEASES IN PuULTIIY.

lam not an advocate of tampering too much with diseased fowls. If they are very bad it is far better to destroy them. Of course if they are valuable exhibition specimens then it is obvious.that all that can be done to save them should be done. There are, however, some simple remedies for certain curable diseases and if I give a few of these it will no doubt prove serviceable.

===== 0002601_19120622_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
COLDS.

Like human beings fowls ;re subject to colds and bronchitis. When taken in the early stage these diseases is easily cured. Treatment :—Take a dish of warm water to which has been added some Condy's Fluid. Dip the affected bird's head right into the water. While doing so the fowl's mouth•should be held open. A few applications will generally prove sufficient to cure a bad cold. When suffering from bronchitis the treatment should be the same ; but to give relief to the fowl you should administer a teaspoonful of salad oil at night. Keep the affected ones in a warm room separate from the rest of the stock.

===== 0002601_19120622_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
SCALY LEG.

This :very common disease is caused through a parasite which lodges underneath the scale of the leg. It is on a par with what is khown as Elephantiasis in the human species. A fowl thus affected must therefore not only look unsightly, but also feel very uncomfort- able as well.

Treatment :—Talte a bowl full of hot water and soap and well soak the fowl's leg. Do this several times and then apply powdered sulphur and paraffin oil mixed together. A good way of applying this mixture is with a small nail brush, so that you are sure to get the mixture well under the scales of the leg. After this give the legs a good rubbing with pure Vaseline. Eventually the scales will be soft enough to remove with a pen knife.

===== 0002601_19120622_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====

SHEPHERDS' MAIN.

THE GILBERT GREENALL " CHALLENGE CUP.

The annual main for the handsome trophy presented by Sir Gilbert Greenall took place at the Sankey Chapel House on Saturday and proved a complete success. Bro. Ben Higham and that veteran bowler Bro John Parr, provided a splendid struggle in the final stage. Hignam went away with a commanding lead at the fifth end, leading 9-4. Parr effected a fine rally, eventually reaching 11-12 with jack in hand, but he was short on a tricky mark and Higham became bolder of the cup by 13-11, The prizes 'will be presented on a date to be decided upon. The Examiner, Saturday, June; 22, 1912.

MESSRS. R. A. NAYLOR'S MAIN AT BOLLINGTON. The employees of Messrs R A Naylor, Ltd, held' their annual bowling man on Saturday, The party about fifty in number, left Warrington by 'bus, atter a most enjoyable drive arrived at the'r destination, the Swan Hotel, Bollington, al-ont half past two. Bowling commenced at once, and on the conclusion of the third round halt-will called for tqt. This was partaken of in the commodious dining-room adjoining the green, and was admirably served by the host, Mr Cowen, and his staff. Tea was followed by a little speech-making, in which the weighty problems of life were not touched upon. After this pleasant interlude, the players, like Sir Francis Drake, of immortal memory, returned to finish their bowling. The finalista were C Ward and T Carter, the former winning easily by 11-3. The various prizes, which were presented to the winners in the works dining-room on Thursday evening by Mr Shake- shaft, were distributed as follows :—l, C Ward ; 2, T Carter ; 3, C Phillips • 4. J Smith ; 5, G Houghton ; 6, E Lunt ; 7, Taylor; 8, G Stout; 9, Alf Smart; 10, L Dillon ; 11, F Howard ' • 12, Block; 13, Thomas; 14, Pritchard ; 15, Whittaker; 16, Dunne.

CRICKET NOTES,

There was a debacle complete and distressing at Arpley on Saturday, when the Manchetwer eleven came down to display their prowess at the expense of the cals. Wariington may, however, justly plead extenuating circumstances, for the visitors brought an exceptionally strong eleven, and two of their players —W Tyldesley and Fairclough—have been doing envy for the county this week. Going ia first, Wariington made a very bad start, four wickets being down for about thirteen. F. G. Cooper, however, played splendid cricket for hie 39, bitting all round the wicket, and was a length out to a magnificent catch at point. Fisher showed a sAid (Mecca and played a correct game, which realised 32. Silcock played carefully for 8 and was at the wicket much longer than his score would indicate. All the other batsmen were tumbled out with melancholy regularity aid the innings closed for 95. Feirclough was the chief destructive agent, eaptu. ing 6 wickets for $1 runs. The leatuie of the Manchester innings was the brilliant display of A E Lawton, the ex-Derbyshire County man who has recently teturned from America. lie bit merrily to all rarts of the ground and his driving off the liev II Gray was very fine. Ile bit one six off Silcock right over Garton's offices a. d when the Rev fl Gray at length bowled him lie had made 59. W Tyldesley disingnished himself with an excellent defensive game, although there was not much to appeal to the average spectator. He was missed by Silcock in the early stages at mid-on and the bowling of the Bev. 11. Gray and W. Bennett failed ta make any impression en him. He carried out his bat r ith 51(0 his credit. When play ceased the visitcts had amassed 150 for the loss of only two wickets.

WESLEYAN METHODISM.

Quarterly Meeting.

The quarlerly meeting of the Warrington Circuit was held at Penketh'on Thursday last week. Tea was provided by the ladies of the Penketh congregation The Rev. W. A. Beckett presided at the meeting, and all the ministers, together with a large number of representatives from all parts of the Circuit were present.—The Rev. E. Morgan voiced the sympathy of the meeting with Mrs. Garnett, and also with the Rev. Jos. Howard, who had met with an accident. Mr. Morgan also made appreciative reference to the work of the Rev. John Yonge, who was retiring from the pastorate of the Wycliffe Church. Mr. Morgan paid a high tribute to the beneficent influence of Mr. Yonge in the town and district, to his great interest in and friendship towards other denominations, and expressed the great appreciation of the Wesleyan Churches for many services rendered by him. The Chairman reported an increase of seven on the membership for the quarter, with a very large number on trial for membership. The Circuit stewards showed a favourable balance sheet with a small balance in hand. A long discussion took place on the Sunday Schools. Progress and development along modern lines were generally reported, with an increase of numbers in all schools but two. These were referred back for further explanation and report. The Cifatintax reported an increase in every Connexional C Heetion with one exception, the total increase being over twenty pounds and the totat amount sent to the headquarters of the Methodist Church, as distinct from the amount raised for Circuit, purposes is nearly £5OO. Intere9ting arrangements have been made for the approaching Conference in Liverpool. Twenty-one ministerial representatives will be housed in the. Circuit and all the pulpits will be occupied by Confer- ence representatives on July 21st and 28th. Two large Conference meetings will be held in Warrington one at Bold-street, an Ordination Service, and one at Bewsey-road. Distinguished and leading ministers Ival take part. The meeting accepted the invitation of Padgate to bold its next assembly there on September 19th.

===== 0002601_19120622_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
INDEPENDENT METHODISM. ----- Annual Conference at Warrington.

Those Independent Methodist Churches which refused to accept the incorporation of the denomination a few years ago held their annual conference at Warrington in the early part of this week. The delegates, representing 22 churches, arrived on Saturday evening and were welcomed at a reception at Friars' Green Church. After wards a fellowship meeting was conducted by Mr J 'Judson, president of Friars Green Church. On Sunday special services were held, the preacher at the morning service being Alderman James Evans, and at the evening service Mr W Brimelow, of Bolton. A young people's service, conducted by the Superintendent, was held in the afternoon, at which an address was given by Mr It Brimelow of Southport. On Monday morning a Communion service was con- ducted by Alderman James Evans (president of the con- nexion), an address being given by Mr J Isterling. It was followed by a business meeting, presided over by Alderman Evans.

THE NEED FOR AGGRESSIVE WORK. The report of the General Committee, stated that generally, they might be raid to be about holding their ono, so far as the work or Gad in their churches is concerned. They rhould not, however, be satisfied with it. The committee pleaded for greater effort and a fuller consecration on the part of all the the members. Let their motto in the coming year be, "The utmost for the Highest." The CHAIRMAN, in ni_iving the adoption of the report, put in a strong plea for evargelicil effort, and said it was not sufficient to feel that they were merely holding their own. If any churches in connection with Noncomformity needed to be aggressive, it was the Indeprndent Methodists. One of the distinctive characteristics of their body was independence of action end government, and that entailed tremendous responsi- bilities.

Mr. DIGGLE seconded and the report was adopted. The various financial reports were also submitted and apjroved. At the afternoon session the Conference, on the proposition ef Alderman EVANS, seconded by Mr. eHEPIIERD, adopted the following resolution : That we record w:th gratitude our thanks to Almighty God for the measure of success attained during th 3 past year, and we pledge ourselves to increased seal and sell- sacrifice in promoting the Kingdom of our Lord Jesus Cbrpit."

===== 0002601_19120622_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
PUBLIC MEETING.

A public meeting which was well attended was held in the church in the evening and the Mayor (Dr, Joseph) presided. He was supported by Alderman James Evena, Mr W Brimelow ([loiters), and Mr T Worthington (Wigaa)._ In his opening remarks the Mayor said that all through his life in Warrington he had been more or less associated with that body--(applause)—probably more so thou any other religious sect in Warrington. lie ln.d formed friendships amongst many .:mennbers of the chtrch, some of whom had passed away, but he was glad to see some or their descendants present that night and some whom he still revered as old friends, tie was glad to see some of his old friends remaining, ainengst which were Aldermen Evans and Pluton, with whom he was Still rssociated in doing good work for the town. lie knew they did good work for that church and they were fellow-workers of his for the good of the community. (Applause.) God was • good and they should try to imitate Him as far as they could, and then they would less often fail to with stand the temptations of the world. It was a matter of congratulation that there was so much freedom in religious worship. Legislation had done a great deal to bring that about,but its powers were somewhat slow, unless they were well administered and followed up. There was now more broad-minded freedom and charity between various sects than existed in his younger days and that was a great advantage, not only to the religious bodies collectively, but individually ; because instead of spending their energies in arguidg which was the best way to worship God, they used their energies in worshipping God and in furthering their own particular cause which was all for the good of humankind, and what, was more it all led to the future welfare of the souls of the people who worshipped God. (Applause ) Mr W Brimelo J.P. (Hilton) said they were all delighted to find that Dr. Joseph was still Mayor of Warrington and delighted that the municipality of Warrington had exercised the wisdom of keeping in office a Christian gentleman to be at the head of municipal affairs. (Applause.) They were all grateful to the Mayor for the practical sympathy he had shown from time to time with their body. (Hear, hear.) They were also delighted with the broad sentiment he had given expression to. (II ar, hear.) They were all agreed that there did exist a larger-hearted charity and warmer sympathy between the various denominations than was the case many. years ag r. (Hear, hear.) The churches were coming into closer co-opetation and the people were hastening themselves nearer the time when the churches would all unite togcther more than in the past for the great work that lay before them. (Hear, hear.) Continuing Mr Brimelow dealt with the ques- tion of personal touch in Christian work and said they all knew of a man at Friars Green who by his personal touch had done much useful work for the cause. (Hear, hear). Ile did not know why it was that we were so reluctant to speak to each other on the sub- ject of wo-ship. Was it not strange he asked, that they talked to one another about everything connected with their earthly life, bu'. nothing was said about the spiritual life. It was an unreal and untrue state of things. The subject of the spiritii al life should be the first and predominating subject. They should all,by their personal touch and influence get people into the house of God and if every one of them would eanh bring another then the world would be won over for Christ. (Applause). Mr. WORTHINGTON (Wigan) said he suppose] they were the smallest denomination in the country, but few people were responsible for their size and he was not ashamed of the fact that they were the smallest denomination. Peopls might say what were the uses of small denominations? They could be a beacon if they got high enough up and if they were low enough down they could be an example. Both things were extremely useful. (Hear, hear.) They were desirous of taking a hand in the righting of man, because the characteristics of the age really clematis:eel it. What were the characteristics of the age ? Drink- ing was not quite so bad now as in the old days but we had got a pleasure stage, a materialistic stage. The craving for pleasure had taken the place of drunkenness in a large way. (Hear, hear). Then there was the great spirit of unrest which prevailed everywhere. If the people who were responsible for the uarest were asked whit was the reason for so much unrest, they said "things were wrong." They did not know properly what the matter was. The thing: they said were wrong were inanimate things, such as money in the wrong pockets, property belonging to the wrong person or land was in the wrong hauls. Twat however was not altogether untrue, for when a man only got .£1 per week for wages and hat a rent to pay, money was then in the wrong pockets. However " things" did not put themselves wrong because they had note breath of life in them. " Things " were wrong became [ilea we-e wrong—(hear, hear)—and they should try to put them- selves right and all things would come right afterwards (Applause). Mr. NATHAN COOK rendered the solo " Abide with Me " in a very acceptable manner, and at the close a hearty vote of thanks was accorded those who had taken part in the proceedings.

TUESDAY'S PROCEEDINGS.

Prior to the business session on Tuesday morning, a devotional service was conducted by Mr. J. 'sterling at which an interesting and instructive addresi was delivered by Mr. Joseph Trickett. Alderman Evans presided over the business meeting, and the SECRErAItY, in presenting the summary of reports of the churches, said that speaking generally the reports were very encouraging, and they seemed to be well on the way to better things and brighter days. (Applause). The report was then adopted. Votes Of thanks having been accorded to the retiring officers and committees the retiring president, Alder- man Evans said he was feeling pretty confident that what ever might be said or left unsaid he believed they were all, along with himself doing their best in the interests of that worthy denomination. (Hear, hear.) When they had elected him as president twelve months before he had promised that he as ou'd do his best and he was of the opinion that he had faithfully kept that promise and given of his very hest. (Hear' hear.) If he had not on some occasions been unable to attend the meetings they should all remember that he was like each one of them, a creature of circumstances. So far as the desire was concerned, he had remarked at the last annual meeting that he would like to visit all the churches in the connexion and have personal interviews and personal co-operation with them. However, that had been out ot the ques- tion, but be looked upon it as the particular duty of the president and whoever they elected as his successor he would like him as far as was possible to get into personal touch with the other churches in the denomina- tion. He was pleased to believe that although they might not be showing great numerical improvement and advancement,they were in a far better spiritual position than twelve months ago. (Hear, hear.) He would like to exprees his gratitude and indebtedness to all those men who had rendered yeoman service for the benefit of the churches. They must all work hard and do their utmost for the good cause and then the churches would be as good as it was possible for them to be. (Applause). The election of officers was then proceeded with. Mr T Worthington, of Wigan, was elected president, Mr W Beasley, of Prescott, vice-president ; Mr H Brimelow, secretary • Mr W Mercer, treasurer ; Messrs W Burton and W 'Mort, editors of the magazine; Messrs James Hashim and James Trickett, auditors ; Mr P Gerrard, financial secretary ; Mr 13, Heaton, Evangelistic secretary and GE. and Band of Hope , secretary ; Mr G Heath, Ministers' Assistance Fund Committee secretary ; Mr J hterling was appointed to select texts for members' tickets ; Mr W Brimelow was appointed to write the address to the churches. THE NEXT CONFERENCE.

SHORTAGE OF TEACHERS.

"NO BETTER OPENING" FOR CHILDREN THAN THE TEACHING

PROFESSION.

The shortage of teachers which threatens to came anxiety to education authorities was the chief subject of interest referred to at the annual prize-giving in con- nection with the Warrington Training College, on Saturday afternoon. As usual, the occasion was lull of interest for the students, their parents and friends, of whom there was a large gathering. Ihe chair was taken by the Rev J B Lancelot (Principal of Liverpool college) and on the platform were the Rev Canon Morley Stevenson (Priucipal of the College), the following members of the College Council : the Ven. Archdeacon Spooner (lion secretary), Rev Canon Pehrhyn, Rev C.non Willis, and Miss Ferrimen and the students of both years. The following accepted the invitation to be present :—The Revs W Brac.,camp, A J Brett, J 51 Buckmaster, W H Cock (vice-principal of the college), Hon C F Cross, El B Firth, J H Garnett, 11 Gray, E Gunner, E E Peters, II V Pigot, G Vale Owen, J T Owen, and J Yonge, Mrs Stevens)°, Mrs Peurhyu, Mrs Willis, Mr and Mrs W B Atkin, M. and Mad ime Bazin, Mrs Bracncamp, Mr T W Brownell, Mr C B Baker, Miss Barker, Miss Brown, Mr and Miss Brooks, Mr. Mrs and Miss Burgess, Miss Onion, Mr F M Darby, Mrs and the Misses Davies, Mr and Mrs Vere B Davies, Miss Dorraus, Mr 11 Forbes, Miss Fox, Mrs an Miss Gorman, Miss Gorse, Mrs Gray, Mrs Gunner, Mrs Hopkins, Mr and Mrs it Johnson, Mr G Jones, Mr and Mrs Lane, Mr and Mrs W Mason, Miss Massy, Mr and Mrs Milling, Mr and Mrs Murray, Mrs G V Owen, Mrs J T Owen, Miss G M Perry, Mr s Peters, Mrs Pigot, Mr and Mrs Robson, Mrs Reid, Miss Reynolds, Mrs Bylands, Miss Shaw, Mrs Shepherd, Miss Taylor, Mr Taylor, the Misses Watkins, Mr and Mrs H Woods, Mr and Mrs J Young, the resident members of the college s.aff and about 100 friends of the second year students. The PRINCIPAL said the work of the college had been carried on efficiently and successfully during the past year, and the reports of the Government inspector, as well as of the Archbishops' inspector, who examined the college in religious knowledge, were excellent. At the present time, he continuei, we were face to face throughout the country with a very considerable shortage of teachers. The country had not yet begun to realise it to its fullest extent,as it would do probably in a year or two, but we had to look ahead, and the number of candidates who were coming forward for the teaching professsion and seeking to enter training colleges had very largely diminished of recent years. In the year 1908 the pupil teachers, bursar!, student teachers and other candidates for the profession numbered 6,191. This sear there were only 2,850. We required for the service of the country a supply each year of 7,800 teachers to make good the wastage, and when only 2,850 were coming forward the position was a very serious on?. It was serious for the schools which, before long, would find it difficult to got teachers ; it was serious for the country because the shortage would probably send up talaries to a great extent and cause the iratepayers more trouble—(laughter)—and it was serious for the colleges because it wrs not improbable that they would be tart with an inadequate supply of students. As the colleges were mainly dependent upon the Government grriat and the fees of the student for their maintenance, a diminution in the number of students made a serious financial situation for them. D..aling with the cause of the shortage, the Principal s aid that two or three years ago there was a great scare. It was said that there were not enough posts for teachers, and no doubt there was truth in that at the time. That influenced many patents against allowing their children to enter a profession in which they might not be able to gain a livelihood. That position of things had now entirely passed away, and the swing of the pendulum was in the other direction. He believed there was

===== 0002601_19120622_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
NO BETTER OPENING

H

for children at the present moment than the teaching profession. It would secure them certainty of employ- ment at a better remuneration ttan could probably be obtained in other ways. The Principal commented upon the change from the old pupil teacher system to that of bursars and student teachers, pointing out that under the former system there was a larger annual allowance for the maintenance of the student. It was rather curious that in these so-called democratic dajs, we should have had a change introduced which made it more instead of less difficult for the child of the person who was not in such good circumstances, to enter the profession than it was a few years ago. Ile greatly regretted that we were losing many of the country people who used to come into the profession. .Under present conditions candidates in the rural dist!icts must attend a secondary rchool, but it sometimes happened that there was no such school within reason- able distance, and they were, therefore, debarred from taking up the work. Under the old pupil teacher system the candidates were taught by the head to cher of the elementary school. He hoped that point would receive careful consideration, because he was a great believer in the country people, who introduced new blood into our towns, and were really the salt of the earth. The Principal concluded his speech with a high tribute to the memory of the late Air. Charles White who for many years was associated with the college as its medical officer. The Rev. J. B. LANCELOT, who distributed the prizes, addressed his remarks to the students and gave them much homely and practical advice, illumined with many shafts of wit and humour. He said there was a joy in all honest and intelligent wore which was surely ore of the best gifts that Providence hid placed within their reach. Ile urged them to keep up a real, living interest in the subjects they had to teacb,edding to their knowledge, pruning their opinions,and temper- ing their judgments. They must acquire that practical wisdom which experience alone could give, and realise that the humdrum commonplace virtues were also the most important, that it was not work that killed, hut arrears of work. If they wore to deal successfully with young people they needed a very large gift of cheerful- ness and good humour and that could only last while physical freshness was preserved. Finally he impressed upon the students the spiritual side of their avocation and asked them always to cultivate their own inner life. Archdeacon Sri °NKR, in proposing a vote of thanks to the Rev J B Lancelot, said the Warrington Training College occupied a very proud and recognised position among the training colleges cf the country. Canon PENItitYN seconded and the vote was heartily passed. During the afternoon the Territorial Band played selections in the grounds and the students gave some fancy dances in pleasing style. The visitors had also the opportunity of inspecting an exhibition of drawing, handwork and needlework by the students. Tea was was served and the day's proceedings concluded with service in the chapel. The Cheshire hay harvest gives promise of being a particularly good one this year, and the grass and clover crops are in fine condition. The Bishop said he had beard that men were not taking so much drink as they used to do. A chaplain of the Guards had told him that the recruits of the present day did not frequent the canteen as often es the recruits of twenty years ago. He was, however, afraid that drunkenness among women was increasing alarmingly. It was not only that the wemen of the poorer classes who were at fauP. There were, be laelieved, not a 107, C 3905 of intemperance among women of the well-to-do class in such respectable suburbs as Sefton Park and Mossley-bill. When travelling he had been shocked to see ladies, or apparently ladies drinking wine at the bar. The housing question had a tot to do with intemperance, and Liverpool owed a very great debt to its Corporation for having spent some- thing like £1,000,000 on rehousing the poor. The Examiner, Saturday, June, 22, 1912. YOU MUST GET INSURED!

ADVICE to MALE & FEMALE WORKERS.

Jo less than a month's time the National Insurance Act will be in actual operation. July 15th is the date fixed for the inauguration of this, the most far-reaching scheme of serial reform ever passed by a Britisit Government. Discreditable attempts are being made in some quarters to foster the impression that the working of tho Act is necessarily complex, but the great majmity of the 14,000,000 MEN, WOMEN, BOYS AND G1121,E1 who are brought under the 9ct will speedily find that getting inseired and keeping insured is the sempleat of orerations. Dow is it to be done? The foundation principle of the Act is that, with certain exceptions, every person in the Fervice ( f an employer, whose earnings are less than :£,3 a week (.£.160) a year, must be insured. Above all, the worker should bear in mind that the full benefits of insurance can only be obtained 1 y

JOINING AN APPROVED SOCIETY. All kinds of societies, such as Fiiendly Societies, Trade Ur ions, Provident Societies, Dividing S cietieq, Collecting Friendly Societies and Industrial Assurance Comranies are brcoming approved see:eties them- selves or forming sections for the administration of the In-urar co Act, and new societies may be formed for the same purp se. If a person who must be insured under the Act does not join an approved society he will become aPost Office contributor and this will mean that he will lose very considerable advan- toges. Deposit contributors are not really insured persors. 'they only draw benefits so leg as there is money stsndirig to their credit, whereas, as members of approved societies, they go on drawing benefits as long as they nei d them. Having srlected his society, the worker should obtain from it

A FORM OF APPLICATION FOR MEMBERSRIP, and fill it up. Approved Foci ties generally are not Msisting 'upon a medical examination, and rge up to 65 is no bar to the ordinary insurance, for the Act staits each man with an i surance leserve fund accerding to his age. The Insurance Caid plays an all-important part in the woikii gof the Act. Every person eligible for insurance must

GLT A CAI3I) BEFoItE itl either from the approved society of which he or she is a member, or from any Post Office, and produce it to his or her emplo;er before July 15th to ba stamped on the first day.

EVERY EMI I.OIER must require contribution carols from every person in his empinyment (unless excluded under the Act) ; or, if the employed person fails to produce a card, use an emergency card which may be btained from any Post Office. Be must also obtain Health Insurance stamps at the Post Office and stamp the cards before paying wages. On the card there are thirteen spaces for stamps to he affixed. The spaces are dated thus: "First week;commencing Monday, July 15, 1912." "Second week, commencing Monday, July 22, 1912." end so on to "Week commencing Monday, October 7th, 1912." On each of these spaces the

EMPLOYER MUST FIX AN INSURANCE STAMP amounting in normal cases to 7d. a week for a man and 6d. a week for a wom in. These stamps are not ordinary post-tge stamps, and they cannot be used for letters. Nor can letter btamps be used for the insur- ance cards. But the special stamps may be purchased at the Post Office just like ordinary stamrs, iu strips and sheets. It is the duty of the employer so to buy them. But rart of the cost (Id. for men and 3d. for women each week) is deducted from the wages of the insured person. Although the workman presents the card at intervals for stamping by the employer he retains possession of it at other times, unless he wishes the employer to take care of it for him THE WORKER'S SAFEGUARD OF SECRECY. The insuraf ce card contains a space for the age of the insured person and a space for the name of the approved society. But there is a warning that "no entry must be made in this space until after the return of the card to the approved society or Post Office.' In other welds, the particulars are only filled in after the card is completely stamped and changed for a new one. The employer cannot therefore find out from the card how old the contributor is, nor whether, for reasoos of health, he or she has been refused by the best societies and driven back on the class of Post Office contributors. In the contributot's interest these personal particulars are treated as strictly con- fidential. .

The Act, of course, protects each insured ,person by insisting that each approved society must maintain itself in solvency.

EXCEPTIONS -TO INSURANCE. There are eleven groups if empl, yed persons who are not required t) b., insured. They include all those employed at the rate of m re than £l6O a year (but th se employed in manual labour must be insured whatever their earnings) and persons already provided for sui h as those in the employ of the Crown, if 1 cil and pubic authorities, and clerks and salaried officials of railway and other statutory companies entitled to rights in a superannuation fund established by Parliament, and teachers who have accepted the Elementary School Teachers' Superannuation Act ; apprentices and learners without wages ; persons employed by the 'coupler of an agricultural holding without wages ; children employed by their parents without wages, and perw-ms who are maintained by their employer without wages ; wivrs employ d by their husbands a= d husbands employed by their wives; outworkers who are the wives of insured persons, and are not wholly or mainly dependent f, r their livelihood en their earrings as outw, rkers ; persons casually employed (hut persons have to be insured who are casually employed for purposes of the employer's trade, and those engaged or paid through a club for the purposes of a game or recrea- tion must a'sa be insured) ; an agent paid by commis- sion or fees or by share in profits, unless mainly dependent upon one employer : and employment of a subsidiary character. LONG PASTORATES.

Personally, I have known only three Nonconformist ministers whose pastorates of their respective churches have exceeded in length that of the Rev. John Yong°, formally closed last Sunday after 30 strenuous years of usefulness in Warringtoi. Curiously, enough, two of.the three were brothers—Baptists with whom the Rev. 11. Ogden, of the Golborne-street Church, must be intimate. Both are still living ; indeed, one of them, after an apparently hopeless breakdown in health has made a t 'tally unexpected and astonithingly good recovery, and has under- taken another charge. The third, likewise a Baptist, has retired afterupwards of 40 years' work in con- nection with one Church ; but is wonderfully fresh and alert, and contitmes to take an active biter( at in the every-day life of the community. May the Rev. J, Yonge long be spared to do lit muse. A BIG 'UN.

===== 0002601_19120622_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
SPECIAL TEMPERANCE EFFORT.

In view of the United Temperance campaign now proceeding in Warringt3/1, I may here reproduce a few appropriate facts of figures from ari article wtitten not very long ago by Mr. George B. Wilson, recretary of the United Kingdom Alliance. It appears that in 1909 no fewer than 5,200 men and women died in England and Wales from alcoholism and cirrhosis, and the last ten years have cost us the lives of over 64,000 persons from these causes, of whom over 28,000 were women. These figures, moreover, far from adequately rep client the drink-caused mortality of this country. Ihey do not, for example include the heavy annual mortality from diseases of various organs of the body which are peculiarly susceptible to the toxic efftct of alcohol. IN or do they include the large number of accidental dtaths which would not have taken place if the deceased had been perfectly sober. They pass by the 1,400 chathe cf infants under a year old who were suffocated by their mothers in bed—a mortality almost entirely due to Ilia deadening of the senses caused by alcoholic indulgence ; and they do not include the very heavy infant mortality which is due to drink-caused poverty and parental neglect. A GRAVE SOCIAL SCANDAL.

Nor can we ignore the social misery which lies behind the 310,000 cases of public drunkenness which, in 1909, were dealt with by the police, and the 91,000 cases of assault There were in England and Wales in that year 3,554 suicides and 2,356 attempts to coin- Mit suicide, of which a very large proportion were due to alcoholic excess. Nearly evety trade which has a high alcoholic mortality has a high suicide mon tality. Nor must we forget the breaking up of family life by wife desertions and separation orders after aysaults, the latter numbering over 8,000 c..ses, largely drink- caused. The principal sufferers are the childien, and it is an appalling fact that during the past ten years the proved Cases of cruelty to children investigated by the Natioanl Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Childreh affected no fewer than 1,135,890 children, of whom 0,728 died ; and the offenders numbered 527,653. In 90 per cent, of these cases one or both offenders were in the habit of taking drink to excess. , URGENT NEED AND REFORII.

By means of a truly appalling list compiled from Press reports of only 15 days' doings, Mr. Wilson showed that no fewer than 2,228 violent deaths and crimes of various degrees occurred in the infatuation for of the fortnight as the result of the for alcohol. As he concluded, perus tl of the details of these cases makes it quite clear that drunkenness and drink-caused crime and disorder centre round the public-house. In view of such facts as those mentioned it seems difficult to resist the conclusion that our present licensing system has utterly failed to cope with what even "The Trade" adwjt to bo " a gigantic evil which still remains to be remedied and most social reformers will agree that the ex-Lord Chancellor de,cribed the true remedy when he said : " You must give people a free hand in their own locality to say whether they will have fewer of these public-houses, or whether they will have them at all. In my opinion," he added," it is, I will not say useless, but of comparatively little value to try anything else unless you give the power to the people who suffer to save themselves. A NUISANCE AND ANNOYANCE. bile the temperance workers are doing their utmost to reform the drunkards, and better still, to prevent the further increase and multiplication of the wretched class who are often a grievous burden to themselves as well as a constant social menace to others—like " 5.8.", the writer of the letter headed "Tram Terrors,"— are with timely editorial backing, directing the attention of the Tramways management to the intolerable official laxity which allows foul- mouthed drunken buttes to habitually use the cars and even grossly insult respectable passengers. The railway people have a short and simple way with such. The bpokiog clerks are not supposed to issue tickets to them, nor the platform staff to suffer them to travel or to remain on the station premises; but if these rules are evaded or disregarded, and an intoxicated person joins a train and annoys his fellow, passengers, the aggrieved ones have it in their power to apply a speedy and effectual remedy. A while aga, while travelling in Yorkshire, I had an experience of this. A drunken fellow who euteted the compartment was guilty of most unseemly misconduct toward some ladies, so I made complaint to the guard, who hauled him out, took his and my names, and in due course the sharp lesson made possible by " the statute (or bylaw) in such cases made acd provided" was admioistered after a fashion calculated to impress the memory and curb the impetuosity of the delinquent. I am glad to be able to add that I have known many electric tram and motor bus conductors who would stand no nonsense from disorderly passengers, even though, as not infrequently happened, a charge of assault had to be added tl the original complaint. Warrington must look to her laurels. By-the-bye, I am told there are no "swear words" in the language of 'happy Japan."

===== 0002601_19120622_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
_ _ TO. PECULIAR DISCUSSION.

Mrs. SI111:-SOVI;;Wit forward a proposal that a lecturer of the Lancashire and Cheshire Band of Hope Union be allowed to give a scientific temperance lecture to the children of the Padgate Cottage Homes At the last meeting the Board was equally divided on the question. Mrs. Simpson pointed out that there were five fully qualified lecturers for Lancashire, Cheshire and North-West Derbyshire, and the lectures were based on the Board of Education's syllabus and graded according to the ages and circumstances of the children. Essays were afterwards written by the children and thus helped them to retain in their memories what they had heard. The Rev A M MITCHELL seconded. He hoped the advocates of the lectures were going to achieve a victory that day. Ile was thoroughly mystified as to why any Board of Guardians or a Board of any kind should refuse such an application. Boards of Guardians especially ought to be most keen on looking out for such invitations and accepting them most cordially. They had as their vice-chairman a leading educationist, but he (Mr Mitchell) could not quite reconcile Mr Jolley as they had him at the Board of Guardians with Mr Jolley as they had him at the Education Committee. (Laughter.) It always seemed to him that there were two Mr Jolleys. (Laughter.) At any rate, he hoped they were going to have the right Mr Jolley there that morning and that he would give his entire and unqualified support in carrying that matter through. It was a dis- grace to a Board like Warrington that they did not try to set an example to other districts and take the lead. Instead of that, they lagged behind and year after year when that proposal was made, they all with one consent began to manufacture excuses. He did not think any- body could reasonably oppose that motion, except those whom he would call antiquated old fossils or those who were directly interest,-d in "the trade." Mr HOUGHTON supported the proposition with the proviso that the members of the Board should receive an intimation of the date of the lecture.

oldMr KNOWLES sii he was not one of the fossils— (laughter)—but on the temperance question he was not a believer in interfering with young children or in forc- ing down their throats certain doctrines,and holding out to them some great fear of the terrible effects of drink by dictation to them. His personal opinion was that such methods were an inducement to the young as soon as they got the opportunity, to try the thing which was said to have such terrible effects, to see what it MIS like. (Toe Rev. A. M. 111iTcHELL : "Nonsense.") Where they found most temperance lectures, in many cases they also fund most drinking. Take their worthy friend the Rev. A. M. Mitchell. He (the speaker) was told he frequently, from his pulpit went for " the drink trade. (The Rev. A. AI. MITCHELL: "1 do.") On the other hand they were told that the rev. gentlem6n's district was a very capable district so far as what they in Lanca- shire called "ale-shifting "was concerned. (Laughter.) Such lectures were,he ventured to thina , to some extent an incitement to drink. If they were to have lectures on one side in the schools, let them have lectures on the other. In the business world they found that Scots- men came over to this country and took up positions of trust in financial matters ; yet the Scotch were a whisky-drinking nation. Irishmen were noted for brilli- ancy and wit—yet, as a rule, they were not teetotallers. Of course, different people looked at the question from different points of view but he did not believe in hammering into children that they must not do this, that and the other. Persuasion was far better.. ..

Mrs. COLLING WOOD, in supporting the resolution,said that if they declined to grant permission it would be very short-sighted policy and give the impression that they were afraid to allow the lecturer to visit their schools. What was there to be afraid of ? Could the man do any harm to the children ? It was a simple, pure, clean, lecture and if the Guardians took the trouble to hear it they would say it could not do any harm. They knew very well that it would do good. With regard to the fanatical view that it was thrusting things down children's throats, it was necessary to educate children in the right way from early years. She had never heard of men going about the country to lecture on alcohol from the opposite standpoint, but if there were any they would probably be glad to give them permission to lecture as well. She asked the Board to be open-hearted and open-minded and to welcome people to their institutions. She did not think the text books would do a bit of good ; it was just a funny little idea of Mr. Mather's. The Rev T R 'MATTHEWS said he was not going to follow the example of their "intemperate" friend Mr. Mitchell and talk about the disgrace attaching to the refusal of the invitation. He would, however, ask : Why should they be afraid of allowing the lecture to be given, when other people gave permission? He should regard it as an injustice to himself if the lecture was , not permitted, for he had attended such lectures for a long time and had enjoyed them immensely. Mr BACKHOCS E said Mr Knowles had told one side of the story but had forgotten to mention the other. He said whisky drinkers often attained good positions, but he forgot to tell them that they often occupied very bad ones. They helped to fill the lunatic asylums and gaols. Mr. FLANAGAN announced himself a convert to the lectures since the last meeting, and mentioned that his own son bad recently attended one and had gained much useful knowledge thereby. Mr. RC SHWOBTH said that if the lecturers would confine themselves to the evils of the abuse of alcohol, they would meet with little objection in any Union. He would be with them heart and soul on those lines, but be did not see why they should lecture against the use of alcohol, when there was nothing wrong in it. Mrs. COLLI NGWOOD : The lectures are against abuse. Mr. JOLLE Y said that as a stupid old fossil—(laughter) —he proposed to be at least a consistent fossil and to vote against the resolution. His feeling was that they wanted to make the boys and girls at Padgate,Christian boys and girls, to grow up into Christian men and women. They did not want to make them into sancti- monious, smug sort of people. (" Oh, Oh.") They did not want them to grow up into pharasaical prigs and to go home and lecture their fathers and mothers. Mr. FLANAG AN : My little boy is not a pharrisaical prig and he does not come home and lecture me. I resent that remark. Miss BROADBENT said she respected a man who could say be had changed his mind. The Guardians were responsible for the children and they had no right to refuse them the advantage of hearing the lecture. She had heard it herself and it certainly could not do any harm. It was a refreshment to the children to have someone coming in from outside to lecture to them. The Guardians knew how many people were in the Workhouse through drink. The CHAIRMAN said he did not wish to influence the Board in any way. He had always been in sympathy with the object of the lectures and he only objected to the illustrations on canvass which were introduced. On a vote being taken the resolution granting permission for the lecture to be given, was carried by 12 votes to 7. MR. AND MRS. AWBERY'S SUPERANNUATION. The Local Government Board wrote consenting to the proposal of the Board to add three years and two years respectively to the actual period of service of Mr and Mrs Awbery, late master and matron, for the purpose of computing their superannuation allowances. The Rev A M MITCHELL : May I ask if the Local Government Board has been informed as to what has I taken place since Mr Awbery's departure and the dis- cussions which have been held in connection with the Board? - -

The CHA.IRM AN : They have not been informed of this private matter. Mr HOUGHTON : I take it that when Mr Awbery left the institution we had finished with him. What have we to communicate further to the Local Govern- ment Board ? Mr W C BROOKS ?I submit there has been no instruc- tion from this Board to notify any change or any circumstances. The CHAIRMAN : I don't think it is any business of ours to notify the Local Government Board what has taken place since Mr Awbery left here. Mr HOUGHTON : There is nothing to prevent any member of the Board writing to the Local Government Board on any grievance he may have—iranginary or otherwise. The matter then dropped:

THE DUKE AND DUCHESS OF CONNAUGHT IN CANADA.

The influenct.exercise.d by the Dube and Duchess of Connaught during their present stay in Canada is admitted on all hands to be having the happiest results There has been no attempt to impress. The Duke and Duchess have gone about their duties with a quiet simplicity and dignity which have brought them as close to tbe masses of the people is to the social groups which constitute society. Their, manner of living gives no sanction to display and lavish expenditure. They have no other desire than to be of service and to per- form their public duties in such a fashion as will stimulate a wholesale Canadian patriotism. The general feeling in Canada in regard to the Duke and his consort is reflected in the following words of the "Toronto News " :—" We are finding that the whole attitude of the Duke and Duchess towards life and its problems and duties is to encourage simplicity in private living; to exalt character and culture rather than possessions, not to accentuate class feeling ; to suegest that in books and music and pictures there is inspiration and elevation ; and finally, to emphasise very delicately and unobtrusively the eternal truth that the supreme thing is to do service ; that mere profession and insincerity and self-advertisement are unlovely and contrary to true gentility, and consititute blemishes as much in the individual as in the national character."

GOING! GOING! GONE! .£9B STOLEN IN AN AUCIION ROONI.

Attending an auction sale at Cheadle, a Cheadle Hulme gentleman was robbed of a leather wallet, con- taining a £5O note and about £4B in gold. The wallet had been taken out of his pocket, and although informa- tion was at once given to the auctioneer, and the police called in immediately, no trace of the money or the thief could be found. The gentleman who lost the money is a retired officer, and at the time was accom- panied by his daughter. The money had been paid over o him by the bank.

ASSAULT ON A BAILIFF.

At tbe Warrington Police Court on Saturday, before Mr. R. T. Faircloush (in the chair), and other magistrates, David Walsh, formerly of Glazebrook- street, Warrington, was charged with haying, on the 18th May, assaulted Henry Harman. a bailiff, 3, Prince- street, whilst in the execution of his duty.—Mr H Woods appeared on behalf of the prosecutor and in presenting his case said it was a serious matter to assault a person whilst in the execution of his duty. The as.ault was a very serious one and the prosecutor had suffered considerably. On the day in question, the bailiff was left in charge of the premises, 34, Glaze- brook-street. At about 6 40 the defendant came in the house and after a few words bad been exchanged he picked up a metal tea pot and was going to throw it at the prosecutor but his sister prevented him. After that, he threw a chair which Harman warded off and was making for the door when Walsh picked up an earthenware tea pot and struck Harman on the bead with it, causing his fore- head to bleed very profusely. Defendant then kicked the prosecutor in the stomach and on the fade, cutting his lip and cau.ing several bruises. The defendant was making use of th,e most filthy language all the time. Harman gave corroborative evidence and Walsh's sister said she thought her brother had had a drop of drink.—Defendant said he was sorry; he was intoxicated —A One of LI 15s. including costs was imposed.

Borough Police Court.

SATURDAY.—Before Mr R T Fairclough (in the chair). Alderman Tinnion and Meseta A J Jolley and r Pemberton. DRUNK IN CHARGE OF A CRlLD.—Arthar Taylor, fireman on the" Campania" was charged with having been drunk in charge of a seven years old child inKnuts- ford-road on Friday.—P.C. MacDonald stated that at 3.15 the previous day he saw the prisoner in Knutsford road in a drunken condition and wandering from side to side of the path. lie (the officer) noticed the child s few yards behind the prisoner and he thereupon asked Taylor if he was in charge of the child and prisoner answered " I am, what's the matter with that." The officer told him be was not in a fit state to Le in charge and prisoner said there was nothing wrong with him. The child was very frightened and at first the prisoner would not give his narue.—Taylor, who said he had only landed from his ship on the Wednesday and had just come out of his sister's house when arre.,ted, was fined 5s and costs.

WEDNESDAY.—Before Mr J W Hayward (in the chair) and Mr W T Williams.

- BICYCLE THEFT.—John McLeah, 'formerly of War- rington, was charged on remand with stealing a bicycle value £lO, the property of Samuel Woods, from the platform of Central Station.—Tae Chief Constable said that on March 23rd Mr Samuel Woods, clerk in the employ of the Cheshire Lines Committee, left his bicycle on the platform about 4 o'clock and on going for it a short time afterwards he could not find it. Inquiries were made and it was found that the same night about 11 o'clock the prisoner had ridden the bicycle to the house of Mrs Drury, 18 Ellen-street, left it there for the week-end underneath the stairs and gone away on it on the Monday morning. He had said that he had taken the bicycle to Manchester and left it in an entry.—The prisoner was sent to prison for two months.

Befors the Mayor (in the chair), Alderman Tinnion, Messrs J W Hayward, S Wright, W Roberts, A .1 Jolley, W. Furness. ASSAULT ON A BARMAID.—Thomas Molyneaux, 73, Dallam-lane, was charged with wounding Margaret Plinston, a barmaid at the Royal Oar Hotel, Bridge- street, on Wednesday.—lnspector Williamson said on the night in question the prisoner had a dispute with nis brother In the hotel over domestic affairs. They commenced to fight, and the barmaid went between them and separated them. Prisoner picked up a jug and threw it at his brother, and it caught the barmaid on the head, knocking her Insensible. She had been under medical treatment since the affair, and he (the Inspector) asked for a remand until July Ist, when the barmaid would probably be able to attend C(Jurt.—The application was granted. RAILWAY Ti] Errs.—Walt or Watts, 30, Winwick-road was charged with stealing six packets of chocolate valued at 6s, from the goods warehouse of the London- and North-Western Railway, Bank Quay Station and was father charged with stealing gents clothing to the value of 10s. Bd. the property o f the company—Prisoner pleaded guilty—Mr. Tait of London said prisoner was a goods porter, and on the morning of the 17th June Detective Goode was concealed in the warehouse and saw the prisoner go to a lurry loaded with a number of packing cases. He was seen to break open one end of the case and take out the chocolates. Goode arrested him, and on searching the house he found the articles mentioned in the second charge.- A fine of £2 was imposed, or in default 28 days' imprisonment.—James Heny Green, a goods checker, 30, Cumberland-street, in the employ of the L. St, N.-W. Railway, was charged with stealing a suit of clothes valued at £2.— Mr Browne defended and pleaded guilty.—Mr Tait said the prisoner had been employed by the Fompany for the last 10 years, and from information received from the last prisoner, Watts, the police questionedtbe prisoner with regard to the suit of clothes. He replied "Yes I took it, I gave it to my mother-in-law." Al r.Browne pleaded for leniency 83 the prisoner was a married man with two children and bad never taken anything before that occasion. He had been tempted to do so by the other prisoner, Watts. He had no necessity to take the suit as he bad clothes at home. He asked that the prisoner should be bound over under the First Offenders Act.—A fine of 20s. inoluding costs was imposed. SCENE AT A STATION.—A well dressed man named William Bolton was charged with assaulting Robert Parr Knowles, a booking clerk in the employ of the L. and N.-W. Railway Company, at Arpley Station.— Mr. Browne defended and pleaded not guilty.—Mr Tait said that on Sunday the 20th May the defendant along , with a friend arrived at the station on the 7.15 train and on their attempting to cross the line at the level crossing they were warned by a porter named Allen. They took no notice of the warning and crossed to the other side. Allen stopped them near the door and told them they were doing wrong. The complainant was collecting tickets near the'door when the defendant contented that the boards formed a public footpath from the platforms. The defendant struck Knowles in the face twice after words had been exchanged. Severalwitnesses corrobor- ated this statement and Mr. Browne said it was a trumpery charge and what the defendant did was under great provocation. The defendant had expressed his regret for what had taken place and admitted he had lost his temper.—A fine of 5s and costs, 13s 6d in all was imposed.

ONE MAN, ONE VOTE.

THE GREAT REFORM BILL.

PLORAL VOTING ABOLISHED.

===== 0002601_19120622_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
Vote for Every Man of 21 Years of Age.

The Government's Franchise Reform Bill was introduced in the House of Commons on Monday by Mr. J. A. Pease. By its provision manhood suffrage will be established—with one man one vote and one vote one value. The essential features of the Bill are as follow :

A man becomes eligible for a vote at the age of 21. The qualifying period may occur during the last six months of his minority. An elector will be qualified by six 'months' residence or occupation, and in no other way. The value of property as an element of qualification will cease. A voter moving from one house to another in the same constituency will retain his vote. If he moves to another constituency he will retain his vote in his old constituency for six months while qualifying for the new one. Plural voting will be abolished, no elector being entitled to vote in more than one constituency. The obligation will be upon the plural voter to disclaim the vote he does not wish to exercise. If a person declines to disclose, at the request of the Registrar, whether he is qualified in another area, his name will be strtick off the list.

For plural voting a person will be liable to a fine of £2OO or a year's imprisonment, and also be incapacitated from voting for seven years. . .

Thee will be continuous registration for Parliamentary and local government purposes. Town clerks in the boroughs and clerks of county councils in the counties will be responsible for the preparation of the register. In addition to the annual publication of the register, a supplementary list will be issued monthly indicating all removals, deaths, and additions—thus keeping the voting lists up to date. Overseers must make a canvass of each district once a year, to see that all qualified persons are on the list. - _ _ -

Revision Courts are to be abolished, and disputed claims will be decided upon in the county courts. Viiiversity constituencies will be abolished, thus reducing the number of M.P.'s by nine. Under the Home Rule Bill the constituencies will.be reduced by 61, and by the operation of the two Bills the total membership of the House of Commons will be brought down to 600. Peers will be entitled to vote at Parlia- mentary elections, but. this, of course, does not carry the right to sit in the Commons. For the first time a large number of male domestic servants will get the vote. A pledge is given by the Government to introduce a redistribution of seats Bill when the effect of the present reform on the size of the constiteencies is ascertained.

It is provided that the Bill is to come in force on June Ist, 1914, or such earlier date as may be fixel by an Order in Council.

A WAYWARD BOY.

At the Warrington Children's Court on Thursday before Mr J Rymer Young (in the chair), Messrs %V Roberts, A J Jolley, E Woods and Dr Naden, William Norman, aged 12, sou of Edward Norman of 12, Porter street, was charged with stealing the sum of £1 4s 2d on the 10th June, the monies of hie father. The Chief Constable said that about 8 o'clock on the morning of Monday 1011 June, the mother sent a woman with a parcel of clothing to pledge at a pawnshop and the amount of money she had to obtain on the goods was about £1 4. The boy left home about 8.40 and went to the woman who had been sent with the clothing—Annie Lee—and told her that his mother had sent him for 2d. for the purpose of purchasing bread.—The woman however, banded the lad the whole of the money and told hirn to take it home to his mother. The parents had thought the lad bad gone to school. As the woman who had pawned the clotlus failed to turn up the mother made inquiries and it was found that the money bad been given to the boy who had not been to school since the sth inst. He was found to have spent Ss. and the remaining £1 Is. was found on him. Continuing the Chief Constable said the boy bad stolen money before and he thought the best thing to do would be to send him to the Birkenhead reformatory. The] father of the prisoner said he had done his best but he had no more control over the lad whatever. The Chairman : That shows your unfitness to be a parent. You are quite as much to blame as he is. The boy will be sent to the Birkenhead Industrial School and you will have to pay 2s. per week towards his maintenance.

WALEING-DAY WEEK-END EXCURSIONS. An advertisement on another page announces a fine assortment of cheap excursions by the L. & NAY. Railway Company from Warrington on June 27th, 28th, and 29th. Local holiday-makers should have no diffi- culty in finding a suitable outlet for the holiday spirit engendered by the annual Walking Day.

A SIMPLE PRESCRIPTION

SAID TO CURE RHEUMATISM, KIDNEY TROUBLE AND BLADDER TROUBLES.

At this time of the year many people suffer terribly from various forms of Rheumatic and Kidney trouble. Probably the most popular and effective form of medicine for these ailments is that known to chemists all over the world as the Barkola Prescription, which has been supplied many thousands of times since it became known to the general public. It is very simple, and is composed of Infusion of Gentian (8.P.) 1 oz., Syrup of Rhubarb 1 oz., Liquid Barkola Com- pound 1 oz., gni Syrup of Ginger 5 oz. You can have your chemist mix these ingredients, or mix them at home yourself. Take one teaspoonful after each meal and one at bedtime..

Ti;ose who suffer from back-ache, kidney troubles. swollen limbs and joints, rheumatism, lumbago, and similar ailments will find this a blessing for even a very few doses will show a marked improvement and give great relief.

Child Burned to Death.

At the Warrington Coroner's Court, Mr. 13righouse, coroner, held an inquest on Tuesday morning on the body of William Henry Critchley, one year and eight months old, the son of William Critchley, a locomotive fireman, residing at 357, Wellfield-street.—The 'mother stated that on Friday, the 14th inst., at 10 30 a.m., she placed her son in the cot in the front bed- room. About half-an-hour afterwards she went upstairs to see if he was all right and found that he was asleep. She then went downstairs to do some cleaning in the front part of the house, and she had been away about a quarter of an hour when she heard screams. She rushed upgtairs to see what was the matter and found the deceased in the cot with his clothes in flames. She took him downstairs and rolled him in a rug and dressed the burns, after which he was remcved to the Infirmary for treatment. He died on Sunday. She had examined the cot and found the charred re-mains of a matchbox and a few match stalks. She could not say how the deceased got hold of the matches unless he had raised himself on the cot and had taken them from the washstand. A verdic of "Accidental death" was returned.

British Protectionists UNFULFILLED now see the folly of AMBITIONS, pretending any longer that theirs is the sovereign medicine for all the ills the flesh is heir to. Sad though it be to remember it, strikes here have been matched by strikes abroad, labour unrest here by greater unrest there, until nothing is left of the preposter- ous claim that Protection would stop it all. The "Evening Standard," which is a Pro- tectionist paper, accordingly threw up the sponge the other day by declaring: "Protectionist countries and Free Trade countries alike suffer from the restlessness, the unfulfilled ambitions, of Labour. If Free Trade England has its devastating strikes, so has Protectionist France, Belgium, Italy and America." It is too much to hope that all our Tariff Reformers will imitate the candour of the "Evening Standard." Without its constant appeal to ignorance and blindness, Protection would be shorn of its force. There will still be many going about painting America and Germany as paradises of peace and concord. But the game is played out, The British voter is not so ignorant as Tariff Reformers would have him be.

LNOTES AND NOTIONS.

After years of obstinate refusal the War- rington Guardians have at last sanctioned scientific lectures on alcohol to the children at Padgate schools. But to some of the members it was like eating the leek.

** 0 - On page 4 we publish a special article designed to instruct the working man and woman in the best and simplest ways of fulfilling the requirements of state insurance.

* - Neither children nor grown-ups will need any reminder that next Friday is Walking Day. The arrangements for the processions and treats follow the well-established lines and all that is needed to ensure success is more genial weather than we have been experiencing of late.

One of the leading Witnesses at the Titanic inquiry stated the other day that the recent disaster notwithstanding, a voyage by a modern liner was the safest form of travel. His view is largely borne out by the record of casualties in London streets where, last year, 410 persons were killed and 14,254 injured.

r.; * Victoria Park bowlers will be delighted to learn that the Corporation are advertising facilities for the sale of mineral waters and refreshments in the Park during the summer season. Time was when the Committee were unsympathetic as adamant to the wail coming from the parched throats of the bowlers, though as individuals they would have been the last men in the world to bowl under similar conditions. Public opinion has, as usual, triumphed. la * * After agreeing that a full dress debate on the Medical Officer's annual report is a necessary thing, the Town Council proceeded last night to defeat their own good object by holding the meeting at a time when the press was not in a position to give the discussion the publicity the importance of the question deserved. !After all, a Town Council meeting is not of much account unless the public have an opportunity of hearing or reading what takes place. _ .

"One man one vote" is a dream nigh upon fulfilment. The Government's Franchise Reform Bill is a far-seeing measure destined to have great effect on the administration of these islands. The abolition of the plural voter and the addition of so many new names to the register will need to be taken into account at the next general election. It is difficult to estimate where party advantage will lie, but the path of duty lies plain and unmistakeably before the eyes of every Liberal and Progressive. It is : Educate, educate, educate—so that young men who receive the franchise in 1914, or earlier, may have a clear understanding the way true progress lies. * *

Holmfirth election is a decided triumph for Liberalism, and for the social policy of the Government. Mr. Arnold (L.) has secured the fine majority of 1,370 in a three-cornered contest in which the Labour poll has increased by 50 per cent. Conservative and Labour are on almost equal terms, and the only satisfaction which the Opposition can derive from the poll is the fact that the Con- servative vote is increased by 336. But Liberal and Labour combined have polled 7,944 votes, against 3,379 for the Conserva- tive. As their's is a community of interest in regard to Free Trade and most social legislation, the Opposition programme (?) Idoes not seem to have counted for much in the West Riding. * * The Warrington Guardians have at last come to a plain commonsense decision on a plain commonsense question. For some years past they have refused permission for the delivery of scientific temperance lectures to the children at Padgate Cottage Homes, without any rhyme or reason except the dictates of prejudice. This year, after twice voting on the question, the Board has, by a majority, sanctioned the lectures. There was plenty of opposition,however, of the type indicated by Mr. Knowles' remark that the Scotch were a whisky-drinking nation and yet Scotsmen frequently occupied positions of trust in financial matters; or by the remark of Mr. A. J. Jolley that they did not wish to make the children into pharasaical prigs, or sanctimonious, smug sort of people. Mr. Jolley commenced with an apologetic descrip- tion of himself as a " stupid old fossil," to which nobody said "question." On this lecture project Mr. Jolley and other mem- bers have both acted and spoken in a manner which detracts from one's best opinion of their intelligence. The Warrington Guardians seem to fear only one thing—and that is any personal responsibility for the manufacture of teetotallers. Maybe they realise that with no drunkards there would be few workhouses and possibly no Guardians. We congratulate them on their well-doing, however reluctant it was. The poor kiddies at Padgate will now learn something about alcohol before getting soaked in it.

Marriage at Wycliffe Church.

A quiet but pretty wedding took place at Wycliffe Congregational Church on Tuesday morning when Mr Thomas Whitfield, eldest son of Mr Robert Whitfield, 121, Bewsev-road, was married to Miss Frances Ethel Davies, youngest daughter of Mr Llewellyn Davies, 142, Lovely-lane. A number of friends of the parties were present to witness the ceremony and express their good wishes. The bridegroom is an accountant with the Elder Dempqter Shipping Company on the Gold Coast, West Africa, and the bride's father is a prominent official in local friendly society circles. The Rev John Yonge officiated. Mr W H Payton, Mus. Bac., was at the organ and played choice selections The bride was attended by Miss Cissie Whitfield (sister of the bridegroom), and the duties of best man were discharged by Mr Frank Lindley. Later in the day the newly-married ceuple left, amid the hearty , congratulations of their friends, for their honeymoon tour to London and Torquay. They intend to take up their residence on the Gold Coast.

It appnars that the child, in company with another girl, visited some friends in Latchford on Tuesday. By some means the child wandered away, and on being missed the police were info,nied. Open-Air Musical Festival. I The annual festival, in aid of the funds of the Infirmary and Dispensary, will be held in Hank Park Ito-morrow (Sunday) commenling at 3.15 p.m. Crosfield's Band (conductor, Mr W Halliwell) and the Warrington Choral Union (conductor, Mr S Hassell) have again kindly undertaken the task of pro- viding the musical entertainment, and lovers of music will greatly appreciate the high-class I fare which will be given for their edification. I With the kind assistance of friends of the Institution, the expense entailed in holding the festival is very low, consequently contributors will have the pleasure of knowing that practically the whole of their offerings will go to the relief of the sick and suffering. The Workingmen's Infirmary Committee earnestly appeal to the residents of the town and district to attend the festival, and by their presence and financial support, help the committee to provide their share of the £lOO per week, required to keep the hospital going. There will be no charge for admission to the Park, but there will be a collection at the gates. Presentation to Mr. John Upson. At a largely attended Representative Assembly of the Lancashire County Association of Teachers, held at Southport on Saturday, Mr. J. Upson was presented by the President (Mr. W. H. Hughes, J.P.), with a gold and enamelled jewel as a memento of his successful presidency of the association. In making the presen- tation, Mr. Hughes said that Mr. Upson was well- known not only to the Representative Assembly but to all the teachers of Lancashire for his earnest and successful work in the cause of education and for his staunch unionism. He was a good and convincing speaker, eloquent on the platform and, from what he (Mr. Hughes) had beard, equally good in the pulpit. His presidential address was one of the ablest he (the speaker) had ever listened to. In that address Mr. Upson clearly set forth the lines upon which the problem of the supply and demand for teachers might be solved. So highly did the officers think of the suggested solution that only a few months ago the address had been placed in the hands of the President of the Board of Education. Mr. Upson was much appreciated in his own district. He was the teachers' representative on the local Educa- tion Committee, and took an active part in many ways in the corporate life of the town. During his presi- dency of the Lancashire County Association he had been appointed a justice of the peace—a position he was pre-eminently fitted to fill. TENNIS, Croquet, Cricket and other outdoor gamer.— J. S. GARLAND, 51-55, Bridge at., Warrington.—Advt. Bluecoat School. "Old Blues" assembled in strong force on Sunday evening when the annual reunion service was held in the school chapel. Among those present were Alderman Forsbaw (president of the Blue Coat Brotherly Society), Mr F Short (secretary), Mr Thomas Armstrong (band instructor), and Messrs T Pilling, 3 Tilley, R C Essenhigh and J L Millett. The service commenced with the singing of Psalm I nail. as a prccessional, "I was glad when they said unto me, We will go into the house of the Lord." Then followed the hymn "To sing Jehovah's glorious praise, our tongues shall now unite," after which one of the pupils, George Roughley, who occupied the pulpit during the first part of the service, read several prayers. Then followed a portion of Scripture which was recited from memory by Richard Rigby one of the pupils, and afterwards the Gospel for the day was recited from memory by Annie Hodges. The hymn "Lord thy word abideth, ' followed, and after that the catechism, the subject being "Prayer the means of grace,"the catechist being George Roughley. The anthem "Thou wilt keep him in perfect peace," by Dr. Hastings, was sung in a very impressive manner, the soloist being Richard Rigby. The latter portion of the prayers was read by George Appleton, after which followed a musical rendering of the Lord's Prayer by A Watson, and the service, which always appeals very deeply to the Old Blues, was brought to a close by the singing of the hymns "Children of the Heavenly King " and " Lord dismiss us with thy blessing. " Mr J Appleton, one of the teachers, presided at the organ. BABY CARRIAGES, special value at 395. Gd. Go-carts from 10s. 6d.—J. S. GARLAND, 51-35, Bridge at., Warrington.—Advt.

Presentation to Mr. John Fairhurst. There was a very representative assembly of members of the Warrington Society at the Old Acadamy on Monday evening, when Mr John Fairhurst (the chairman of the Society), was the recipient of a handsome presentation in recognition of 1 his eight years valuable service as town treasurer. Mr I E J Hall presided, and said the presentation had been received with enthusiasm by the members of the Societyl who were desirious of showing their affectionate regard Ifor Mr Fairhurst, and to record on the annuals of I the society of the town, the fact that he had served them so faithfully and well. Alderman Bennett also testified to the honour and uprightness of Mr Fairhurst as a public servant, and remarked that it was largely due to his efforts that the Society was now an institution deeply rooted in the affections of the people of Warrington. Messrs Longland, L Greening, Lofthouse, Madeley, J Richardson, jun., Foster Jackson, J A Ransoms, and C D Parkinson also spoke in eulogistic terms of Mr Fairhurst's services to the Society. The Chairman then presented Mr Fairhurst with a solid silver centre piece, which bore the inscription: "Presented to John Fairhurst, Esq., by the members of the Warrington Society on his retirement Item the position of honorary treasurer, 29 Feb., 1912." Mr Fairhurst, in reply, said that what he had done for the society he had done very willingly and with the object of doing what he could to help what he thought was a good cause. When he became treasurer they owed the bank the very con- siderable sum of £l6O, but by tightening the strings a little they had now got it down to under £3O and he hoped his successor would see that wiped off in the next year. (Applause.) He highly appreciated the token of their esteem, and should never forget that day. When he took the present home someone else would appreciate the gift quite as much as he did. (Applause.) —An interesting feature of the presentation was the fact that every official of the society during Mr Fairhurst's term of office was present, and took part in the proceedings. igr Fairhurst afterwards entertained the 'company to light refreshments.
