SMOKE PREVENTION.

WHAT THE INDIVIDUAL MAY DO.

It is not a pleasant task for private individuals to feel obliged to undertake a duty which ought to be done by the authori- ties elected for the purpose. It is well, however, that it should be known that prosecutions for smoke nuisance can be put in force by private individuals under Section 91 of the Public Health Act, 1875. It is always open for any two householders to lay infor- mation of a nuisance and prosecute. Further, any 'ratepayer can appeal direct to the Local Government Board, when the local authori- ties as in Warrington are lethargic or timid. The action of Mrs. Hopwood of Middleton will no doubt be of interest to the readers of the "Examiner." This lady appointed a practical smoke inspector to take observa- tions in the boroughs of Middleton and Hey- wood, and had also photographs taken of the chimneys while smoking. As a result convictions were obtained against several of theeoffending manufacturers. By the end of 1903 this lady had summoned into Court and got convictions in 140 cases, only losing one. A number of magistrates conspired to upset this worthy lady's crusade by inflicting a small penalty and charging the prosecutrix with the costs. They further dismissed some flagrant cases of offence. This plucky lady reported the conduct of the magistrates to thg4 . Lord Chancellor of Lancashire, who caused an enquiry to be made, with the result that the magistrates were severely reprimanded. This had a very beneficial effect in the district in subsequent cases. Altogether, Mrs. Hopwood took over 1,000 cases into Couift. As a result, the average output of black smoke in the area under observation, which in 1892 wa§ 30 minutes per hour, in 1900 was reduced to two minutes per hour and the manufacturing firms benefited by the economies they were constrained to effect.

Health Appointments. At a meeting of the Health Committee yesterday, Mrs. Alcock, (Lrmerly of Warrington) was appointed a health visitor. The following candidates for the assistant medical office, ship were selected to appear by the committee on Tuescay, when the final choice will be made : —Dr Bullough, of Dudley : Dr Leigh, acting rceiical officer at Warrington: Dr. G. W. Nield Joseph, of Liverpool. New Football League. Another meeting was held in the Lion Hotel on Thursday evening with the intention of forming. a Warrington and District Football League and several representatives of various teams in the district were present. Nothing of a definite nature was done but it was decided to make the proposition known to all the teams in the district. Lecture On Poultry. The members of the B S.P. had an enjoyable treat on Monday evening when Mr. G. A. Leach (Redcap of the Examiner) delivered an interesting lecture on "Poultry keeping for the suburban and town dweller." Mr. Leach dealt at some length with the all-important subject of housing accommodation and the breeds most suitable for the suburban and town dweller. The lecture, which was illustrated with paintings from the late Harrison Weir F.R.H.S. was listened to with rapt attention and by means of the pictutes and black- board illustrations the difference between show and utility birds was shown. Many members ot the audience were surprised to learn that there were so many breeds and varieties in the poultry world. The practical side of poultry keeping was taro dealt with in a masterly manner and at the close questions were invited and satisfactorily answered by the lecturer who was accorded a hearty vote of thanks. Mr. Leach was given a cordial invitation to give a lantern lecture on some future occasion. DRESS BASKETS, 19a. Gd., 21s. Gd., 245. Gd., special value in Hand Bags Trunks, Suit cases, etc.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55:Bridge et., Warrington.—Advt.

Death of Miss Leigh. A highly respected and well known church worker has passed away this week in the person of Miss Ada Leigh, eldest daughter of Mrs Sarah Jane and the late Mr Peter Leigh, of 9, Mount-street. Miss Leigh, who was 40 years of age, had not enjoyed good health for some time and had been confined to her bed for five weeks prier to her death,which took place at her residence on Tuesday, from heart failure. A staunch Churchwoman the deceased was prominently identified with Holy Trinity, a parish for which she has done much useful work as a Sunday ' school teacher and general worker. She took an active part in the Girls' Friendly Society and had many friends in that organis- ation as well as being liked by the congregation generally—indeed all who knew her bad a great respect for her. She had shown her interest in the church in many practical ways and had, by her many acts of generosity, and benevolent nature, gained the appreciation and esteem of all. The funeral took place yesterday at the Warring- ton Cemetery amidst many manifestations of sorrow. The chief mourners were Messrs W W Leigh, J D Leigh, H Leigh (brothers), Richard Hornbv (cousin), Whittam (Chester), John Whitlow (Broornfields, Appleton), John Whitlow (Preston Brook), Alderman Pierpoint and Councillor Birtles. The Rev E C E Carleton, assisted by the Rev L Parry, conducted a service in the church and also officiated at the grave- side. The high esteem in which the deceased lady was held was evidenced by the fact that there were I no fewer than 63 wreaths on the grave.

:ORE NUISANCE CAUGHT BY THE CAMERA. trict taken from the south side of the river this week. The wind was blowing is the town.

Tos!

The result of the new methods upon the surrounding country as testified by farmers, fruit-growers, and residents, all went to show what an improvement had been effected; crops were increased and products grown which in the earlier period were impossible. Mrs. Hopwood, in her report, expresses the opinion that the country, as a whole, will never be able to deal satisfactorily with the nuisance until Parliament sees the necessity of putting the Black Smoke Act under the Alkali Act, and appointing Government In- spectors, and Stipendiary Magistrates to abjudicate.

Alderman BENNETT contended that if the manufacturers would not yield to persuasive methods, but persisted in that intolerable nuisance, the authorities should inflict fines, growing in intensity, until they obeyed the law. Alderman BOLTON resented Alderman Bennett's " libelling " of the town. He would have outsiders believe that we were living in an atmosphere of soot, but Warrington had as pure an atmosphere as they had anywhere else. For a low-lying town, Warrington was as healthy as any place in England. The more smoke we had, the better it was for Warrington. It meant more wages, and would help to keep the Insurance Act going. (Laughter). Councillor ROBERTS said a great deal of the agitation which was begotten of casual observations was not, he feared,founded upon such a basis as would permit the Health Committee to take punitive action. It was very rarely one saw black smoke coming from a non-exempted chimney for three con- secutive minutes. He suggested that Alder- man Bennett should notify the Health Com- mittee of the particular chimneys of which he had reason to complain. The MAYOR contended that arguments which had been advanced as to the effects of smoke on health were not borne out by the medical officer's report. He had taken the trouble to distribute the cases of diphtheria among the various wards and found that in Bewsey, which was a smoky ward, there was only one case. On the other hand in Latchford, the healthiest ward, there were 23 cases and he could not help thinkb g that the offensive smell from the canal and the river had a great deal to do with the preva- lence of that disease. The report showed that the smoky wards were particularly free from infectious disease—a fact which supported the argument he had used before, that the small amount of sulphuric acid in smoke acted as a disinfectant and the car- bonous part of the smoke was practically non-injurious to animal or vegetable life.

COUNCIL AND THE PROBLEM.

HEALTH IN SMOKY WARDS.

The Mayor's View.

In the course of the discussion on the Medical Officer's report at the Council meet- ing last night, Alderman Bennett alluded to the smoke nuisance, and while compliment- ing the Health Committee on increased vigilance, said he was not yet satisfied with their efforts. A larger number of chimneys were offenders than one would gather from the report and it gave one the impression that the officials put the telescope to the blind eye. (Laughter). The coal strike had one redeeming feature in that it enabled us to know what it was to live in comparatively pure air. The Alderman cordially acknow- ledged the help of the " Examiner " which, he said, had from first to last backed him up in that crusade with great pluck and courage. Councillor CUNNINGTON : It is ridiculous. We shall have smoke fever. I wish we had five times more smoke in Warrington. It would be better for the workingmen.

THE INSURANCE ACT. TOWN COUNCIL REPRESENTATIVES ON LOCAL COMMITTEE.

At a special meeting last evening, the Town Council appointed the following repre- sentatives on the Provisional National Insur- ance Committee :—The Mayor, Aldermen Bennett, James Evans and Tinnion, Coun- cillors Jenkins and Roberts, Mrs. George Venn and Mrs. E. Simpson. PROPOSED LOCAL SANATORIUM. Alderman JAMES EVANS, chairman of the Health Committee, informed the Council that the Health Committee was trying to evolve a scheme that would meet the I requirements of the Insurance Commis- sioners and would afford Warrington sole control of a sanatorium of its own. CORPORATION EMPLOYEES AND THE ACT.

The Town Clerk was authorized to make application on behalf of the salaried officers and clerks in the service of the Corporation for partial exemption under section 47 of the National Insurance Act.

LIBERAL WOMEN'S SEWING CLASS.

Entertained at Colwyn Bay.

An enjoyable day was spent by the above class at Colwyn Bay on Thursday, when some 110 members assembled under the leadership of Mrs Osborne and ten ladies of the committee. Mrs Crosfield had kindly invited the sewing class for the day, but in her unavoidable absence, the company was received by Miss Crosfield and Miss le Thicke. Races were run, and hopped, on the sands till the encroaching tide drove the party to the pebbles. The hostesses in vain lured members to come and bathe, so they eventually carried out some mild trick-swimming by themselves. One solitary wader was seen, however, and there may have been more. Tea was provided at Oartmell's restaurant at 3 o'clock, and then the party walked, and drove, up to. Bryn Erthin, Mrs Crosfield's residence. Good work was being done by several menibers, even on a holiday, to judge by snatches of argument on the great subject of Mr Lloyd George which took place between one or two of the drivers and our spirited Liberal Iwomen. The final heats for the races were now run off on the croquet lawn, and Mrs Halliwell and Mrs Crosier may be said to have proved themselves the athletes of the party. Miss le Thicke photographed the Committee, and then the assembled company. We hope to publish them in next week's " Examiner." The party then went over the house whilst tefresh. ments were served at the same time. In presenting the prizes for races, before the company broke up, Miss Crosfield said that she had a pleasant duty to per- form, the nature of which was no secret to the mem- bers present, but was quite unknown to the Corn mittee, on whose faces surprise was depicted. The sewing class, in recognition of the untiring efforts and devotion of Mrs. Osborne, as hon. secretary, and Miss Booth, as treasurer of the class, bad subscribed together and now presented these ladies with a hand- some salad bowl and a brown suede bag, respectively. Mrs Vose, having added a few words of appreciation on behalf of the class, Mrs Osborne and Miss Booth replied, and the proceedings closed with a hearty vote of thanks to Mrs Crosfield for her kind invitation. The weather was delightful, a west wind blowing and the views showing at their best.

MR A M LOADER APPOINTED MANAGER OF A LONDON THEATRE.

Mr. A. M Loader, who has been the general manager of the Royal Court Theatre, Warrington, since it was acquired by Sir Joseph Beecham, has just been appointed general manager of the Aldwych Theatres London. His many friends in Warrington, who highly appreciated his efficient, tactful and ever-courteous direction of local theatrical affairs, will congratulate him upon his selection for such an important position in the Metropolis. The Aldwych Theatre is, of course, the property of Sir Joseph Beecham, Mayor of St. Helens, and Mr. Loader will retain a general oversight of the three theatres belonging to Sir Joseph— the Aldwych,. Theatre, London, the Royal,Court Theatre, Warrington and the Theatre and Opera House, St. Helens. Mr. H. Warburton, the popular acting manager, will have special charge of the Warrington theatre. It is pleas- ing to know that Mr. Loader will still maintain a close connection with Warrington and, for some time at any rate, continue to reside hare.

STOCKTON HEATH WALKING DAY. --- Yesterday was the Annual Walking Day at Stockton Heath and the weather, although threatening, was kind enough to permit the children to spend a happy time. About 1,000 assembled at 2-30 and joined in a procession round the village headed by the Blue Coat School Band. Afterwards tea was served in the schools and the treat closed with sports and games in the field adjoining Wilderspool Brewery.

Royalty at St. Paul's Cathedral.—Their majesties attendance at the Hospital Sunday service at St. Paul'r Cathedral recalls the fact that the late King Edward, Queen Alexandra and other members of the Royal; , Family, attended a similar service at the Cathedral church of the metropolis in 1903. On that occasion royalty was present in State ; on last Sunday the King , and Queen attended as private worshippers, and there were no tickets, railed oft places, or ceremonial other than if their Majesties had not been present. It is not often that Londoners have an opportunity of seeing royalty in church, and as might have been expected, there was a very crowded congregation at the service. St. Paul's Cathedral however, never lacks a worthy con- gregation. There is something always impressive about a great cathedral, its architecture, embodied history and fine organ and choir, all combine to attract the public on week day and Sunday services alike. As a rule most of the city churches in the metropolis are all but empty on Sundays. There are very few resident parishioners compared with what there were in former times, when merchants and traders resided over or near to their business premises. But it is not of course, only in the city proper that the metropolitan churches are poorly attended, to which result two reasons mainly contribute—indifferent preaching and a religious service, which is considered too long for present day conditions of life.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

"The World and a Woman."

"The World and a Woman," presented twice nightly at the Royal Court Theatre this week, is a passable melodrama. The bills term it a comedy- drama, but the comic element might be still further cut without any loss of interest or flislocation of the story. There is plenty to thrill in the exploits of the adventuress who, having been cast asice by a duped and disappointed husband, devotes her efforts to wrecking -his life. Miss Augusta Tullock impersonates Judith Sartoris, the woman with a past, in a powerful style which grips the audience. There is also some clever characterisation by Mr Eric Norman as Jack Carrington, the long-suffering hero, and Mr R Cuthbert as the contemptible Eugene Darville. The other parts are in capable hands and the play is attractively mounted. NEXT WEEK.—" THE BONDMAN."

On Monday next and the five following nights twice nightly. Mr. Derwent Hall Caine will present at the above theatre his father's great play "The Bondman," supported by a strong London Company, with all new scenery and effects. It will also be doubly interesting seeing that Mr. Derwent Hall Caine plays the leading character " Jason." A short synopsis of what he has done will be interesting. He is the youngest of our leading actors and consequently of touring managers. He has been on the stage since leaving school and has played with great success such parts as " Oscar "in "The Prodigal Son," " Dan "in "The Bishop's Son " and " David Rossi " in " The Eternal Question " and has received both from Press and public warm praise. The story of " The Bondman " is as follows: It is a play dramatised from the novel, by the author, Mr. Hall Caine. The main theme is admirably preserved in the play, and is a splendid reflection of a story which has held many thousands of readers spellbound. It is admitted by the ablest critics to be one of the ' greatest plays of the present century, and deals in a very noble manner with great subjects. It also gives the actor abundant opportunities of portraying all the various passions the human heart is capable of feeling and teaches a rgreat moral lesson, forbearance, charity, love, atonement, resisting temptation, and to those that have fallen, forgiveness, and in particular, the working out of a nobler and better life. "The Bondman "is an easily understood domestic tale admirably adapted for dramatic produc- tion, and the fact that it is a simple story, whose main feature is magnificent character delineation, is one of the surest reasons why it will continue to have an uninterrupted run on the stage, and be witnessed by thousands who have read the gifted author's work. Dement Hall Caine will be supported by Miss Rita Tornkins, a well-known young Lcr_..ion actress who will play " Greeba," also Mr F Paigh Lawrence, who will play " Michael Sunlocks." This production should prove a great attraction and seats should be secured early.

Military Funeral. The funeral of ex-Chief-Sergeaut Tasker, late of the South Lancashire Regiment, took place on Sunday The &ceased had 19; sears Bervic3 and held the medal for Titah 1897-1899 also the long service and good conduct medals. The coffin was borne to the grave by members of the Serfeants Mess 8-40 R.D. A firing party composed of I\ .C.O.'s and men of the 8-40 R.D. and the Troops from Orford Barracks under Captain Cotton, South Lancashire Regiment, was in attendance. Besides the widow, mother and relations of deceased, many of his old comrades were present. Wreaths were sent fmm Sergeants Messes of the following : 8-40 RD., 2nd. The band of the 4th P.W.O.V. South Lancashire Regiment (Territorials) under Sergeant Qr. Clarke played the "Dead March " from deceased's house to the Cemetery.

St. Ann's Church.

One can easily perceive and appreciate the vest improvement which has taken place in the organ at SL Ann's Church, which has recently been renovated and undergone such additions as to make it practically a new instrument. It is now quite up-to-date and the parishioners are justly proud of it. The builder is Mr. Thomas Pendlebury, Leigh, Lancashire, who is recog- nized as a tone-specialist. The dedication took place at a special service on Wednesday evening when the .Venerable Archdeacon Spooner preached an eloquent sermon to a large con- gregation. Taking for his text "Praise ye the Lord " Psalm cl., 6, the Archdeacon said that no fewer than ten Psalms commenced with those words in the form of Alleluia and the Book of Psalms ended with those words. He esteemed it a great pleasure and privilege to be with them on that occasion in order that he might dedicate that gift to God and also to show his deep sympathy with the Vicar and his assistants in the work they were carrying on in that large and poor parish. Continuing the Archdeacon said there was a great distinction between praise and thanksgiving. They could take the word "Praise ye the Lord" as an appeal to nature and they could not understand nature without studying it. Praise was a very important part of worship and the instrument for leading such praise was the organ. However, the organ was only intended to accompany and not to take the place of the wonhip of the church. Sometimes the choir and organist took the place of the congregation but it should not be so. All persons should be able to join in the service and the choir and organ should just fulfil the parts allotted to them.

A special organ recital was given by Mr. W. Hull, organist of Leigh, Lancashire, at, the close aud was much appreciated.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

There was the usual meeting on Sunday, presided over by Mr J Himsworth. Mr T Hearn gave the address upon " Character," founded upon the words" And they took knowledge of them that they had been with Jesus." What a man was,and not what he said, proved character Mr Farrar read the lesson, Mr Himsworth led the prayer, Mr J Thomas sang" Evening song," which was much enjoyed. Mr Payton presided at the organ. The secretary read the following lettet from the Rev John Yonge _ . _

June 9th, 19,2. Dear Mr. Farrar,—l am afraid I did not, as tuliv as I ought, express my sehse of gratitude this afternoon f.,r the generous kindness of the Brethren. And I should be glad if next Sunday afternoon you would assure them, on my behalf, that I du most highly appreciate their goodness. The money which the purse contained, I am handing to the custody and appropriation of my wife, to be devoted to such objects in the domestic replenishment as may seem to her needful and desirable. They will be objects in our home which, as we look at them, we may recognize as procured through the kind gilt of ' the P.S.A., and so we shall be reminded or the ' kind contributors to the fund. The commendatory words of the Brethren who spoke were pleasant to hear, and I can only say that they will be thankfully remembered. The P.S.A. will always be regarded by me with feelings of pleasure and thankfulness that I had part in the initiation of the Brotherhood. It has been a good work, and I venture to think the com- mittee may be well advised to consider what new and further develOpments may be possible and desirable. A change of title from P.S.A. to " Brotherhood " might bring you more into line with the modern term. And now that your finances are so improved, you might be willing to launch out in fresh aggressiveness, and to make tresh attempts to gather in the large numbers of men still at large and lacking religious association upon Sundays. These are ideas I should greatly rejoice to see put into action, and earnestly trust that an early attempt should be made. Once more,l thank you, and Mrs. Yonge joins me in doing so, for the kind present of this afternoon, and for all the warm and cordial words I was privileged to hear. May God bless the Brotherhood, and vouchsafe its speedy extension, and its increased usefulness and success.—Very sincerely yours,

JOHN YONG&

Ellesmere-street Sunday School.

Anniversary services in connection with Ellesmere- street Wesleyan Methodist Sunday school were held on Sunday, when great interest centred in the visit of Mr and Mrs Gipsy Evens. The morning service was con- ducted in the Ellesmere-street Mission Room, when Mr Evens gave an interesting address to the children, and later in the service delivered a discourse from the words "Cast the net on the right aide of the boat and ye shall find," taking as the basis of his theme "Adaptation to the varying circumstances in our work." Mr J Hulse presided at the organ, and Miss Plinston accompanied Mrs Evens in her solo, "God will take care of you." In the afternoon a musical service was held in Bold-street Church under the presidency of Mrs. Dodds, and Mr. Evens gave an address on " The companionship of David and Jonathan." urging the young people to chooce good companions. Mrs. Evens greatly impressed the congregation by her singing of a Gospel song entitled "Do something for Jesus to-day " Mr. I McArthur sang very expressively the solo, " Lead, , kinaly light." and Miss Whitmore sang with I pleasing effect the solo entitled " Calvary." At ' the evening service the church was filled. , Mrs Arthur Booth sang with much feeling "0 God, our I help is ages past." Mrs Gipsy Evens was heard to advantage in the Gospel song "He keepetn His promise for ever." The text from which Mr Evens discoursed was taken from the 6th verse of the 19th chapter of St Luke," And he made haste and came down,and received Him joyfully." The singing of the children all through the day was excellent, and reflected-great credit alike on themselves and Mr E Ellison, the choirmaster. The following children sang solos in the hymns :—Helen Miller,Cissie Lamb,Lizzie Jonei,Jessie Miller,FredJones Mat Fogarty and Willie Livesey. Mr F Hickman offici- ated as organist of the afternoon and evening services. On Monday evening Mr. Evens lectured before a large audience in Bold-street schoolroom on " Hidden gems in city life." He narrated some splendid instances of characters reformed by the power of God in their mission work. Some of the gems in our cities he named were such noble characters as the [Rev. Charles Garrett, Rev. Father Nugent, Bishop Ryle, Rev. Canon Lester, Rev. Ilugh Price Hughes, Rev. Peter Thompson and Rev. Samuel F. Collier.—Mrs. Evens related some of her experiences in the mission field, and sang a Gospel song taken from Torrey-Alexander's book, entitled "Lean on His arm." Mr. J. IG. Hill Cliff e Baptist Anniversary.

On Sunday the annual summer sermons were preached by the Rev A Curtis (of Baintrse, Essex, brother of the pastor). There were large congregations and at the evening service the churcu was crowded. Tea was pro- vided for visitors by special ladies of the church. The interval between tea and service was pleasantly spent by the visitors in viewing the old burial ground, with its stones taking us back to Cromwell's days, and many of them to previous years. The Rev A Curtis gave an address to children in the morning. In the afternoon he took for his text, Dent. xxix, `2.9, "The secret thing belong unto the Lord our God: but those things which are revealed belong unto us and unto our children for e% er and ever that we may do all the words of this law." The preacher's main points were: 1, That secret things of ten become revealed things. This became so by study, and he instanced this point by the advances made in the various departments of science, and also instanced Bible light on history and prophesy. Prayer alsa was a key that unlocked the doors of the unknown; 2, Tnat , revealed' things have in them much of secrecy. In science for example, we know a great deal about , electricity, but what it really is no one can tell us. In natural history we are acquainted with various forms of life, but what life is no one can tell us. In the botanical realm we are acquainted with various plants but no one can inform us chat germination is. The chemist can describe various forms and combinations of matter, but what matter is he knows not. He further instanced some of the mysteries of Providence and said that in religion the cross was the centre of the known and unknown. 3. That there must be secret things, from the relation of the infinite to the finite. What does a child know of household management, or the deaf of music, or the blind of Colour? In divine Provi- dence how much cf mystery exists, and DO one can know all that salvation means. 4. That revelation is to promote obedience. Revealed things are for us to own, teach, and obey. 5. That the secret things of this life will become the revelations of eternity. The picture is not yet finished. The top of the hill is not reached—when it is and we stand above the clouds a glorious view will reward us for our life's climbing. The victory is not yet won, when it is, and the palm branch takes the place of the sword we shall know why the Captain placed us in that difficult spot,and gave us such arduous work to do. In the evening his text was Luke xv., 10, and the title of his sermon "Heaven's interest in the earth." The large congregations listened with wrapt attention to the eh,quent sermons which were most I heartily appreciated and the offerings during the day , were in excess of several previous years. Anthems were beautifully rendered by the choir at each service. In the afternoon the anthem " When the Lord shall build up Zion " was given, Mrs James Ken- worthy taking the solo with her usual ability. Miss Taylor sang most sweetly " 0 Divine Redeemer," accompanied by Miss Jones. At the evening service the choir gave the anthem " Rock of ages," Miss Taylor giving the solo. Mrs. James Kenworthy with great feeling sang " Abide with me," and was accom- panied by Miss Isherwood. Miss Ads Grounds was the organist and Mrs James Kenworthy the conduct)r.

St. Mary's Church. Annual School Sermons.

The annual school sermons in connection with the 11 St Mary's Roman Catholic Church. Buttermarket-street, were preached on Sunday by the Rev J S Vaughan (assistant Bishop of Salford). Good congregations assembled at both morning and evening services and the collections, which were in aid of the school fund, were highly satisfactory. Preaching at the High Mass, the Bishop said there were two ways of teaching, that of precept and that of example. Jesus Christ had chosen the hardest way—that of example. He was te example and model of every man for be possessed every possible virtue and excellence. If we studied the life of Christ and turned over, thoughtfully, the pages ofn the Scripture we should be struck with the fact that although,. He possessed every virtue in the highest possible degree there was one virtue He seemed desirous of manifesting, and which rendered it more con- spicuous in our eyes,l. and that was the virtue of I humility. As God He could not have exercised that virtue, because what, after all was humility ? It was 1 the practical recognition of our nothingness and our dependence on Almighty God, but as man He was able to give a good example of humility. Therefore we perceived on studying His life how wondrous and how great was his pro- found humanity. There was no virtue in His life which struck us more in its extraordinary perfection and there was no virtue in his character which filled us with os much astonishment. When he exercised other powers, virtues and excellences, they seemed so natural, con- sidering tie was God as well as man, and we were not astonished because He bad the uncreaied wisdom of the Father. When however, we saw Him practising humility and biding His infinite power and might then it was that we began to grow astonished. When, for

instance we saw Min, by whom kings reigned and princes of the earth exercised their power, when we saw Him, this great and infinite God going down to Nazareth in the form of a child and becoming subject to Mary and Joseph, we must wonder exceedingly. Christ had drunk to the dregs the chalice of humilia- tion.He had suffered as much ignomony and humiliation as it was possible for anyone to suffer. How were we to explain such extreme humiliation on the part of the Son of God ? The answer was that Jesus being God as well as man and being able to look throughout the world and into men's hearts he knew, because be, was infinite in knowledge, that pride was the very source of every spring of' our misfortune and that it was through pride that all that was sinful and hateful took its rise. The Bishop gave many illustrations of the downfall which came to those who were too proud. It had been the source of all our misfortune and the mainspring of every sin. If we would only resolve to learn this lesson thoroughly, and if we only succeeded in becoming really and truly humble, then our way to sanctity was easy and rapid, for humility was the source not of one but of every virtue. When we came to examine faith, we found that it was nothing else but humility of the intellect. It was the bowing down of our proud intellect to the obedience of God. Obedience without which it was impossible for us to see salvation, was humility of the will ; when we conquered our own will and brought it under subjection to the will of God. Then there was brotherly love which could only be found amongst those who were humble of heart. There was nothing like pride to destroy that brotherly love and intimate friend- ship. Also there was the duty of prayer. A proud man thought be would be alright without praying, that he needed not the help of any man, but the humble man was he who admitted his'own weaknesses and recognised the forces of temptation, and he knew that left to him- self he could not overcome his weaknesses and tempta- tions without the help of God. how could we obtain this prolific virtue of humility ? By asking for it of God and by realising its importance and how much good came with it, also by putting before us the example of Christ and learning of Him.

PARISH CHURCH UP FOR SALE.

The ancient Parish Church of St. Bar- tholomew, at Wanborough, near Guildford, was put under the auctioneer's hammer at Guildford on Tuesday. The church is supposed to have been built during the reign of William the Conqueror, but from 1642 to 1862 no services were held in it, and it was used as an outhouse for farm purposes. It was also used as a carpenter's shop and timber shed. The church contains only sixteen pews—eight on each side—and the ,haucel is quite as big as the nave.

===== 0002601_19120622_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN F.C.'S AMBITIOUS SCHEME.

--- The first report of the directors of the Runcorn Football Club, Limited, states that the performances of the local team last season were most disappointing, and it is questionable whether the club ever experienced a more disastrous season from a playing point of view. The profit and loss account shows a loss on the revenue account of 1:74 45., and on forma- tion and depreciation expenses of £l5 17s. 5d., a total loss of just over £9O. The liabilities of the old club, amounting to £489, have been cleared off. The report goes on to show that an ambitious scheme is to be attempted. The large Irwell-lane enclosure is to have a piece taken off it, upon which 120 to 150 cottages will be erected and new streets made. Sufficient ground will be left to provide a full-size Rift) leg pitch a cinder running track, and accommoda- tion for 9,000 to 10,000 spectators. It is proposed to erect dressing and bath rooms for both the home and visiting teams, a referee's and officials' room, a board room, and a large room available as a gymnasium and meeting room for players.

GREENORE (CO. LOUTH). Greenore nestles inside the southern shores of Cmrlingford Lough, and those who are contemplating a trip to the Emerald Isle should not fail to visit this haven of rest. It is easily accessible for English tourists by the short sea passage from Holyhead, and it is a convenient starting place for people desirous of visiting any part of Ireland, particularly the Donegal Highlands, Giants' Causeway, Portrush, Lough Erne, Lough Neagh, Antrim Coast, Belfast, Newcastle (Co. Down). A commodious Hotel which overlooks Carlin ford Lough and toe Mourne Mountains is under the management of the London and North Western Railway Company, where visitors will be sure of every comfort, and attention. There is splendid fishing in the vicinity. The golf links attached to the Hotel, which are free to visitors, have been immensely improved, and are now a really first-class eighteen-hole links. Visitors to the Greenors Hotel also enjoy free use of the comfortable Lough Steamer which plies regularly between Greenore and Greencastle, as well as Warrenpoint. The pretty bungalows at Greenore are also under the management of the London and North Western Company, and visitors may rent these for any period. Full particulars, and free guide (postage 3c1.) can he had on application to the Enquiry Office, Euston Station, N.W.

You wi I forgive the frankness of Buse counsels, from your old minister, as he lays down his work. And now, Christian brethren, the hour of separation has come. %% hen I leave this pulpit presently, I shall never again stand in it, in the rein ion which has been my privilege, and often my delight, for so lengthened a period. By your kindness, I may visit it. now and agaiti, for a passing hour. But the cc urse of my so-called " regular ministry " is now ending. Four years, in the beautiful Isle of Wight : ten years in the ruid-t of factory life at Heywood : 31, nearly 36 years in this growing arid thriving town of Warrington. Fifty years in all ! lam

Ot TIIE TJiRetIOLF OF MV 1 ÜBILEE and am, presently, to become a memory, rather than a persotiality ! Fur mytelf, and for my devoted wife, this is a rupretne event. Neither el e nor I can sever tbis long exiatt nt link with Wycliffe withtut unspeakable regret. And yet, how much cause have we f.r thankfulness to Hod that iie has suffered us to continue for so lengthened a period; and gratitude to our beloied Wycliffe people that they have borne with our diminishing powers w ith such fidelity and pa' ience. We believe that not one of our three churches on ever fade from loving memory •, and that we shall henceforth dwell like all other elder people, considerably in the 3 eara which have passed. Wycliffe, nem-warily, by reason of its great majority of pars, and its multitude of experi- ences, must hold the premier regard of our hearts, until the end may come. Indeed—Cowes, Heywood, War- rington, wherein we have together, w ife and 1, spent our lives—will ever fill our h,'arts with previous visions of faces, and echoes of familiar voices which have made lire an even frequent delight, despite occasional trials, difficulties, disappointments. And so, we pass to our eventide, pray ia,g, hoping, believing that "at eventide it will be light.' Brethren of Wycliffe, brethren of Warrington, we bid you a Christian farewell. " The Lord of Peace Himself give you peace always, by all means. The Lord be with you all." The choir sang very expressively the anthem "Abide with me " (Barony), the soloists being Miss E Hewitt, Messrs A Griffiths and H Hawthorn. The concluding hymn was " With the sweet word of peace we bid our brethren go. "

FAREWELL MEETING. Tributes from Many Churches. HANDSOME PRESENTATIONS.

A meeting which will ever rank as memorable in the history of Wycliffe Congregational Clurota, and indeed ot Nonconformity in Warrington, w..s held in the church on Tuesday evening. The gathering was fully representative of the religious and civic life of the town and there could have been no more impressive tribute to Mr. Yonge's life and work than the desire of representatives of so many sectiens of the community to express their affectionate regard for him. The chief feature of the meeting was the presentation to Mr Yonge of two handsome gifts—.£27o from the co agregation of Wyeiffe Church and £55 from other townspeople. Before the meeting there was a converaezione an the Memorial Buildings when p is and preront, mernhers of the %Wycliffe oongre;ation and many other friends had the opp ,rtnnity of meeting Mr one Mrs Yonge. e'rern 6.45 to 7.20 Mr W 11 Peyton gave a most enjoy- able organ recital in the church, the selections being well chosen and tastefully played. ho first part of the meeting was presided over by Mr G N Ford (ex-chairman of the Congregational Union of England and V% ales), who was supported on the pla form by the Rev J Yonge, Rev Pr %lessor Armitage (Yorkshire Amalgamated College, Bedford), his Worship the Mayor (Councillor Dr Joseph), the Rector (the Rev Canon Willis), the liev Ca, on 31 rley Stevenson (Principal of the Training College). the Rev William Bracecamp (vicar of St Paul's), Rev II T Epencer, the Rev W Cooper (representing the Free Church Miuisters' Fraternal), the Rev II Ogden (Gulborne-street Baptist Church), Mr E II DANVEIOO, LLB, Rev B Nightingale and Mr J B Leach (represent- ing the I..)ncashira Congregational Union), Mr C B Parker (Free Church Council), Mr W Brown (Sunday school suy erintendent), Messrs A Corney, B Wallii gten, B Farrar, C Ecclee, E S Garland, J Hawthorn, W Beswick and J Ball (deacons), Mr P T Vinsklll (church treasurer!, and Mr George Wallington. The Rev E C E Carleton wrote to Mr Eccles :—"Holy Trinity Vicarage, Warrington, 15th June, 1912.—My dear Sir, I sin sorry to say I cannot be present on Tuesday evening on the occasion of the Rev John Yonge's retiroment meeting. I had, as y .0 know, intimated my intention of being present in March last. My son, however, some hat unexpectedly arrived from Switzerland a few days ago for a short visit, and as the time I am able to devote to him is limited and as Tuesday evening especially is the evening before his departure again, I shall not be able to get away for that evening. I regret this the more as Mr Yonge and I have been assectated together in many works of various kinds, in which his co-operation for the future will he greatly missed. We have worked happily and pleasantly together without any kind of difficulty ever having arisen to my knowledge, Mr Yonge's fairness and courtesy at all times being as conspicuous as his cen- sisteney and honesty of conviction. He has my sincerest wishes for his weilfare on his retirement, and I greatly sympathise with the nook who are losing his services, and will be deprived of his oversight." The Rev. Silas Rixon, minister of the Heywood Church, wrote : T be Congregational Church, York-street, Heywood, of which, from the first Sunday in January, 1867, until Sunday, October 29th, 1876, the Rev. John Yonge was beloved completelovedpasr pastor, having beard that Mr. 51) years of service in the tyhoen faithful tihaf ualbaonudt t Christian ministry, and at the same time to retire from active, responsible pastoral work, desires heartily' to rejoice with him in the consummation of so unique and honour able a record, giving glory to God for kilt 3 grace and goodness thus manifested ; and also to express both for Mrs. Yonee and himself, loving goodwill, and the prayerful desire that the evening time of their life and service together may be full of serene glad rest, associ- ated with the joy of ' sheaves after sowing, sun after rain.'—Si gued on behalf of the church, &mks Rum, minister." Mr. F. Monks wrote :—" As a fellow townsman, I can speak with the fullest knowledge of Mr. one's steady, unselfish devotian to the public welfare, for I have been associated with him in all the various forma of educational, social, and philanthropic work of the past 35 years, and I wish to bear testimony to its useful- ness and value. Others will doubtless bear the same testimony and wish him a happy green old age to which he is entitled after so long and strenuous a ministry among you." Mr. S. Twelves (of Heywood) said he felt it one of the greatest honours of his life to be there and bear testimony to the worth of Mr. Yonge. When he came to them at Heywood in the year 1867 the young people, of whom he was then one, looked at him and wondered what sort of a help he would be to them. He bad held the view all his life that unless a minister was a great help to young people,as to the elder ones, he would be a complete failure. Mr. Yonge took the young people in hand and taught tbem things which had proved beneficial in their daily lives. He taught them to be straight and true in business life—to be, above

everything, their true selves. When the nieishers at Wycliffe sent Mr. Yonge the invitation to take up the pastorate at Warrington they at Heywood thought the Wycliffe people a terribly cruel lot. (Laughter aid applause) Tna Onaittmee said they had come to look at the completed work of Mr. Yong,. He thought they had a right to say" Better a long min'stry completed thaa a minis ry starting forth in an unknown place." (Hee., hear). One always felt teat while there were poten- tialities for great things, there were possibilities of immense failures. That evening they rejoiced over a ministry completed and crowned with success. , (Applause). Mr. Yonge had ministered to them faith- I fully and well and be had been a true friend of his (the , speaker's). He honoured him greatly and honoured his long pastorate. He (the speaker) liked those long pastorates ; he hated the whirligig business which seemed to he so much in vogue amongst them now-a- days. A man was no sooner settled than he was look- ing out for what possibilities there might be elsewhere. Ile knew the times were arduous and strenuous and he by no means said a man was bound to abide where he entered on his work, hut if, to the mutual happiness of himself and his people, a minister could see those to whom he had ministered as children growing up to he useful,in their day and generation, ant c uld feel that he had lad a shaping hand in their early years that was something like en ideal pastorate, and they had one there at WI ell ffe. (Hear, hear), lie hoped they would have in Mr. Yonge's successor one to whom they could give the tame confidence, support and affection. (Applause ) Mr G WALLIsw,TON,on behalf of Wycliffe Church, said the prevailing feeling that bad filled their hearts during Mr. Yonge's pas'orate had been one of profound thank- fulness to Alreighty God whoin His providence and grace sent him amongst them. Mr. Yonge had been to them a men with a message from the King. They bad come week by week to that church, after the toils and con- flicts of working days, realising more and more in their souls that man did not live by bread alone but by every word that proceedeth out of the mouth of God, and they had returned to their homes strengthened end invigorated and buoyant., having received help from the Sanctuary by Mr. Youge'e nuitaistratiors. They had EOM visions and dreamed dreams, as he had unfolded the oracles of God ; which they trusted bad materialized in leftier ideals nod efforts after higher life. In addition to hie public ministrations, Mr. Yongo had been a corufo: t in their homes, rejoicing with thme who rejoiced, and in their sadder seasoss, when their hearts h ,e 1 been bowed down, when they had seen the vacant chair, and sighed for " the touch of a vanished bend, the Bolted of a voice that is still," next to the Mast.. et 3 e Mpg/1y DO voice or handshake were flora welcome than Mr. Yonge's. (Applause.)

REV. JUN Y

Mr. W. BEsavicx,one of the senior deacons of Wycliffe Church, arid he thanked God be had been spared to give his bumble testimony to the thiity•five years' loving service Mr. and Mrs. Yonge had given to them as a church and pep 'o, guiding them safely along Royal pathways. Tho leading principle of Mr. Yonge's ministry had been to preach Christ and Him crucified, and lay the foundation of a silent worship of God and love of humanity. Mr W BROWN, on behalf of the Sunday school, said that in timm of difficulty and trouble in their homes they had all hal the benefit of Mr Yonge's kindly help and sympathy. (Hear, hear) When the time came, as it must come sooner or letter, when one lad to make room for younger folk, words were scarcely adequate to express one's feelings' but on behalf of the Smithy school, he would like to assure Mr Yonge that thr ir sympathies rnd love went with him in his retirement. Many old scholars and members of the Bible class scattered in all parts )f the country shared in their symp‘thy and appreciatlon. (Applause). PRESENTATION BY THE MAYOR.

The MAYOR said the task he had to discharge was a difficult one,i ut at the same time it me a pleasant one. It was his honorable duty to represent his fellow- townsmen in almost every way except in political and Parliamentary matters. In every-day life be was fre- quently called upon to take the lead end he did so very readily, hut never with greater pleasure than he &el on this occasion. Ile had beard a great deal about Mr Yonge, and he thoroughly endorsed what had been said about his ministerial work, Ile was old enough to ramember when such a platform as the present one would not have been filled in the way in which it was now filled. The barritrs of sectarianism and political rivalry had crumbled away before their estimation of a good man and a useful citizen. (Hear, tear and applause). He gave himself the pleasure and privilege of listening to their pastor's sermon on Sunday morning earl he was very pleased with it. Ile thought what an admirable sermon it was, and how very applicable to the occasion. (Applause.) He calculated that during his ministry at Wycl ffe Church, Mr. Yonge, making allowances for holidays must have preached about 5,000 sermons, and if every one was characterised by the same energy and the same amount of anxious thought, the mental and physical work involved must have bean altrost incalculable, and was worthy of the gratitude of all. His Worship next paid a tribute to the services of Mr. Yonge as a member end former Chairman of the Board of Management of the Infirmary, and referred to the interest which he took in the patients. Continuing, the Mayor said he did not think they appreciated their public men as they ought to appreciate them. He did not refer to the members of the Town Council—(laughter;—but to prominent citizens, men and women, who by their efforts in various directions for the good of the people deserved their highest appreciation. Mr. Yonge had been a true citizen, and bad done everything in his power for the good of the people and the improvement of the town—in fact no man in the ministry had done more in that direction than Mr. Yong°. His Worship then, on behalf of his fellow townsmen outside that church, presented Mr. Yonge with a cheque for £55, and expressed the hope that Mr. and Mrs. Yonge might be long spared. Ile wished Mr. Yonge every happincas —be would not say in his declining years, because his mental and physical vigour were such that really one did nct know whether he ought to have retired. (Laughter nod applause). , The Rev. JOHN TONGS, in acknowledging the gift, said he had not looked for anything of the kind, and he greatly appreciated the kindness and generosity of the friends who had contributed. He thanked the Mayor ' for the good character he had given him as a oitizen. Laughter.) He had certainly alwais been willing to do what was possible, outside his ordinary duties, for the welfare and happiness of his fellow townsmen, and he rejoiced to think that during that considerable period Warrington had developed and increased in many ways. lie believed Warrington warts better place than it was when he first knew it. He did not say he had had much to do with that, but he believed there had been a great advance in the popular idea of what a town should be. It was very pleasing to him, in retir- ing from active work, to feel that he had so lived as to commend himself to the good graces of his fellow- townsmen. (Applause). CONGREGATIONAL UNION'S ESTEEM.

Mr E B DAwsoN, on behalf of the Lancashire Con- gregational Union, spoke very highly from intimate knowledge of Mr Yonge's work for that organization. Mr Yonge had not been one of those who were most prominent in speaking at meetings and taking up valu- able thee—(laughter)—but whenevtr he had Opoken, he had been listened to with attention because the members knew be would only say that which was ncces- sary and which be felt would instruct them. He had been one of the most faithful officers the Union ever had. In 1890 the Union raised him to the chairmanship, and his presidential address, delivered at Blackburn,had never faded from his (the speaker's) memory. As a denomination they owed thanks to Mr Tongs for the way in which he had brought up his son to be head- master cf Silcoates—the training school of Congrega- tionalism in the North of England. (Applause). The Rev. 13. NIGHTINGALE (chairman of the Lanca- shire Congrega' lona! Union), bore personal testimony to more than one kindly act he had experienced at the hand 3 of Mr Yonge, and said be would to the end of his days cherish the most sacred memories of Mr Yonge's great kindness towards him. No address to a hich he (the speaker) had been privileged to listen, made a deeper impression upon him than the address which Mr Yonge delivered as chairman of the Lancashire Con- gregational Union. It was a bold utterance and distinctly prophetic. To-day everobody preached the gospel of social salvation, but it was comparatively new in those early d tas. Mr Yonge's speeches at the various meetings of the Union always indicated sanity of thought and real statesmanship, and his counsels bad almost invariably been acted upon, Mr.

Yonge's ministry was not really terminated, although officially it might be said to have concluded on Sunday. Although another voice might occupy the pulpit and another person move in and out among them, it was plrfectiv certain that his ministry which had been so long and so richly exercised would continue working itself out in high characters and good lives for many years to come. (Applause.) Mr. LEACH', of Huyton,also spooking on behalf of the Lincashire Congregational Union, said he had worked alongside Mr. Yonge in the Union and he had learned to respect sill esteem hir&for his sterling worth. There was a universal feeling of love toward Mr. Yonge Oroughout the Union and it was a great pleasure to him that their pastor had not retired from tho souretarial duties of the Union. (Applause). _ . . .

The preacher's work was not so much that of a speaker as of a seer—the man who saw and th, n declared to others that which he had seen. ANGLICAN APPRECIATION.

The RECTOR (Rev( anon Willis) said he had been asked in his official cepacity, in conjunction with the Vicar of St. Paul's, to say a few words on that interest- ing and important occasion on Lehalf of the clergy of the English Chureh. He could herne:tly say it was a very great pleasure to him to join in the expression of general regret at the departure, or resignation of Mr Yonge and the universal appreciation of his character and work. Ile bad always regarded him in three lights, (list as the acknowleiged and re-pscted Mad of the various Nonconformist religious bodies in the town. Ile was cure that al the ministers, if they had to be represented,would wish to be represented by Mr Yonge, lie had also looked upon him as a prominent citizen, whose words and opinions always carried weight. When he spoke upon social and m ,ral qeestions affecting the welfare of the town everybody listened with attention and respect. But he had looted urn' him thirdly and chiefly as a God-fearing and Ch mh ristian man, o had never been afraid to ere k his mind, and bad always referred with courtesy and kindness to those who might tier from him. Ile was sure Mr Yonge must feel very deeply the solemnity of that occasion when he vas giving up active work and had to I iok forward to renderieg his acccuet to Almighty God, as we all Inuit do, for his life's work. Ile (the Rector) trusted it would be a comforting reflection to him io Inn retire- ment to think that though the Aligner's clergy and !semen clergy and Nonconformist ministers thought so differently on many vital and most important questions they we e all at one in their warmest appreciation of his life and work. (Applause) The Rev W Bracecamp said he bad known Mr Yonge for thirteen years. For three years he knew him by ra pule, fcr he realzed ant whenever there was any great social or religioes work to be done in Warring- ton, Mr Yonge was al.vays in the forefront, ready to do his level beat fur the people with whom he Came in contact and for the town in which he lived. For the last ten leers he had known him more intimately, He supposed that, eecleeieetically, kir Yonge had been one of his sheep— (laughter)—and unfortunately some people might say he had not been a very good one— (liughtel)—but bad rather wandered from the fold. lie (the sp eke!) hoped he would always be broad-minded enough to recognize that, after such a life Mr Yonge would at the end be bound to form one of? that great ck of which the Or- at Shepherd Himself was the Heed. His people at St Paul's we'll(' b 3 most anxious that he should join with the congregation of Wyoliffe in wishing Mr. Yonge every blessing in his retirement. Mn'. Yonge had been a true helpmeet all the days of their married life, acd he hoped that they would both be spared for many years. All present, no matter what their pelitical or religions tenets might be, were full of gratitude to God for such a life as Mr. Yonge had lived The man in the street sometimes said he did not believe in religion because there were so many frauds in it. If such a man would only take the life of Mr. Yonge as an xarnple be would see that there was a reality in eligion, after all. (Applause). The Rev. CANON STEVENSON add he had known Mr. Yonge for several years, and he Ind known him better and better as time went on. He entertained for him not only-esteem, but affection, and be should have been. sorry if he had not been able to say that in the presence of that meeting. From the bottom of his heart he sympathized with the congregation, who were about to sever the ties which bad so long exist e I between them and their pastor. He also sympathized with the town and many of its institutions, which were losing a good friend and citizen, and with many a heart in Warring- ton that would be eeparated for a time from a true and loving friend. (Applause). FREE CHURCH COUNCIL AND MINISTERS. Mr C E PARKER, on behalf of tl es Free Church Council, raid he was proud to join in doing honour to one of Warrington's grand old men. lie spent an after- noon with another of Warring ou's trend old men—Mr F Monks —last week and a gr.( at deal of his talk was about Mr Yonge and the history and work of Wycliffe Church. Everything that Mr Yonge as3ociated himself with be had lifted and helped and he had placed every Free Church under a debt of gratitede to him. Notting was ever suggested in the way of special effort by the Free Churches which they cared to ptit into oner ition until it had had the sanction and approval of Mr Yonge. Canon Willis had rightly said that Mr Yonge bad been looked upon as the honoured and re pected representa- tive of Nonconformity in Warrington for many years. tie was president cf the Free Chuech Council for a period en i even when he was norninaEy not occupying that position, they had always looked upon him es the real president not cnly in the matter of advice Lut of work. Mr Yonge never asked ethers to undertake any task which he himself was not willing to share. (Applause), The REV. W. COOPER then read the knowing address from the Warrington and -District Free Church Ministerial Fraternal :—" Dear brother,—We desire to seize the occasion of ycur retirement from the more active duties of the ministry, that we may testify our united and affec:lonate appreci 'Gen of your lofty character, and reel( need aid valuable services. You have nobly sustained the traditions of the Free Church pulpit by the effectiveness of your evangelical preach- ing, the wisdom of your administration in the alleirs of your church, and the fidelity find tenderness of your pastoral visitation. You hese proclaimed the cardinal truths of the Clospel with great ability and no uncertain sound. You have not only faithfully discharged your duties as minister to Wycliffe Congregational Church, but have also found time to attend to other offices and institutions which stand for the weal of our beloved town. By taking this view of your holy vocation, you have secured the respect, confidecce and love of your fellow citizens. We rejoice that in these labours you have been ably seconded by the sympathetic co-opera- tion of your devoted wife, end we unitedly pray that in the quieter paths of service, the Divine blessing may continuo to rest unenlyou end yours. Trusting that we may continue to enjoy your presence at the meetings of our " Fret( roal," which owes so much to you, we remain with all good wishes.—Yours most sincerely, W. COOPER, SECRETARY."

PRESENTATION FROM WYCLIFFE CHURCH. Mr. P. 'P. Wl's:blau,, in the absence of Mr. Ziba Armitage, then made the presentation on behalf of Wycliffe Church. lie said he was very conscious of his own unfitness to undertake the duty and would only say, in reading the address it had been the intention of Mr. Armitage to deliver, that he fully endorsed every word it contained. Mr Yong° came to that pastorate when he (Mr Yonge) as a boy in the Sunday school and their relationship had been very happy all the way through. Mr Armitage's address was as follows :—" I vcknovvledge gratefully the honour conferred by my fellow-deacons in entrusting to me the duty of making a presentation to Mr and Mrs Yonge at the close of n pastorate which induration has far exceeded the average term of ministerial settlements. During;the past 36 years there have been several occasions which the people of Wycliffe have readily seized for marking their apprecia- tion of Mr Yonge, both as friend and past r. On any matter creating discussion, when we could not see "eye to eye," we have still agreed to differ. It will depend upon its people, far more perhaps than they may think. The success w i'l of cou-se be helped by its new Pastor, when he has been found. But meanwhile it must depend upon its people. SANGER'S CIRCUS.

FORTHCOMING VISIT TO WARRINGTON

Lord John Sanger's huge circus will make its appear- ance in Warrington, on Saturday next. It will be fixed up on the land fronting the Palatine Works,Wilderspool Causeway. Aftetnoon and evening performances have been arranged. If the weather. turns out suitable the afternoon performance viii he preceded by a gorgeous procession of what is perhaps best described as a circus, hippodrome, and menagerie. By this visit War- rington is favoured as one of the centres touched by the 1912 tour of an attractive and in many ways remarkable entertainment. The tour, which began on 18th March and will extent flyer 31 weeks, is a triumph,of organi- sation. It has occupied Lord John Sanger's attention for four months of the winter season, and he declares that the shaw eclipses anything he bas hitherto arranged. He has engaged the cream of the circus world to appear in equestrian, dramatic, comedy, acrobatic, gymnastic, zoological, and a host of other turns.

The circus has travelled throughout the British Isles all over the Continent, and in the United States and Canada. There are no fewer than 320 animals, including lions, leopards, elephants and wolves and herd John's estimate of their value may be gauged by the fact that he has refused large sums of money for some of them. They cost him a pretty penny in food alone. Each day the bigger animals devour 1001 b. of butcher's meat ; that involves an expenditure of something like 303., or about £9OO a year. On the top of this is the price of the more or less dainty morsels that have t 3 be pro- vided for the other animals. The principal fodder is hay, which is largely produced on Lord John Sanger's 400 acre farm at Honey, in Surrey. In addition to an enormous quantity of bay, fixed at 800 tons, this army of circus entertainers accounts in the course of a year for 3,650 quartem of oats and 150 tons of chaff. Nearly 5,000 square yards of canvas were required to make the immense tent that houses the circus, the material weighing 15 tons. The size of the tent is indicated by tbe fact that in addition to the big arena for the circus in the centre, there is sufficient room left to afford seating accommodation for about 6,000 persons. But the big staff of workmen is so well organized that the tent is erected and ready for use in two and a half hours. The total cost of running the show for the season is stated to be £55,000.

At the meeting of the St. Asaph Council attention was drawn to a number of automatic chocolate machines which had been erected in the district, tradesmen having complained that such machines competed with them. It was decided to Write to the County Council inquiring whether permission had been given for the ereotion of the machines, and whether rent was being paid.

HOW CARLO SAVED THE HOUSE FROM FIRE.

On two occasions lately we have read in the newspapers of an alarm of fire being given by a dog. A story has just come from America which is much more interesting; indeed it is quite an uncommon story. A gentleman named Morris Barclay taught his dog, Carlo, many tricks, one of which was to " talk " at tho telephone. When the telephone rang, Carlo would run into the hall, and if it was Mr. Barclay or any of his friends he was allowed to " speak " to them. He would bark just as if he were .trying to say, "How are you ? "

one day a call came for Central at the telephone-office and when the telephone-girl said "Hello," the only reply was the excited barking of a dog. She could not understand why no one answered her, and was so puzzled that she called Mr. Barclay at his office.

" A call came from your house," she told him, "and I can get no reply, but the dog is at the 'phone barking wildly and excitedly." "Carlo at the telephone!" Mr. Barclay exclaimed. " Why, that is strange, for there is nobody at home. I cannot understand it. Something may be wrong, so I will go right up and see. Thank you for telling me." Mr. Barclay hurried home as fast as he could, and when he opened the door a rush of smoke greeted him. Carlo was on the chair in front of the telephone scratching and bark- ing excitedly. Then he knew that the dog had realized that something was wrong, when the room filled with smoke, and he had gone to the telephone where he had "talked" with his master. In his excitement he hrd knocked the receiver down, and in that way had given the alarm which led to the dis- covery of the fire. Carlo now goes about with a silver medal hanging from his collar and anyone who bares to examine it will find an inscription which tells that it is a reward to Carlo for saving the house from fire. and he deserves it.
