LOVE THE OUTCAST.

BY ERNEST TREETON

Author of " For Love and Justice," "The Say. ing of Christian Sergison," "The Instigator," "The New Jack Sheppard," &c.

"And why won't you, Mildred?" "Because—oh, I don't know! I can't!" The questioner wee a man; the answerer was a woman. Though she had seen her twentieth summer, she was still as fair and as flowerlike tie the girlhood of sweet seven- teen. The man with whom she was standing on the garden lawn of Myrtlebury Rectory was fair too, stalwart, easy, graceful .in i poee, and handsome—a soldier, t was plain, in every air and motion. Although all Myrtlebury knew her as Mildred Rashleigh, the only daughter of the Hon. and Rev. Syd- ney Reehleigh, Rector of Myrtlebury, there was not a soul who had ever seen such a light in her eyes as shone there now. Richard Wellingham looked silently .at her for some moments before he spoke again.. "Should I be here now," he asked, "if I bad not seen the light that no man can mis- understand in your eyes? What does this change mean? You must tell me, Milly." Mildred remained silent. "I came over this afternoon, feeling as light-hearted as a boy, to ask you for an an- swer which I felt sure of getting. And now all you tell me is, 'I can't.' Yet if I had asked you a week ago you would not have said that. You would have known that you would be my wife. Why do you say 'No' now, Milly? I ought to know." "What right have you to say that?" she asked, a little desperately, at last. "The right of a man whom you have taught to love you," be replied, looking at her straightly. "Ah! I think I understand," he went on, with a touch of bitterness in his tone. "It was just the butterfly brightness of the passing hour. Your little Myrtlebury was quiet, and it made more sunshine dance in your eyes when I—some of us—came over from Norbarough with our spirits high. Yes, I see, flat's how it was. And I am not rich—and I've only a Captain's pay—" Wellingham paused. There was an expres- sion in her face which told him that, even if she had been a coquette, there had been some depth to her pastime. Ile was comparatively poor, but that was not the only thing that nr- fluenced her. Nor was it mere fickleness or caprice. There was some other force sway- ing her. Of a sudden, his thoughts &came heavy and dark. In a flash of inspiration he thought of Neil Blackmore, his command- ing officer, still single, like himself. He had seen an ominous glance askance in Black- more's eye when he and Mildred had been apart together. "Oh, it is not only that!" she murmured, constrainedly. "I did not know—l did not think so much of that—of your being only a little better off than your pay, I mean. There are other things—" Mildred stopped, almost in a breakdown, as if she had been carried away into words which would have been better left unspoken. "Give a dog a bad name—you know the rest, Milly," he said, bitterly. "Has anyone been giving me—a character?" Once more Mildred was silent. She could not answer. "I understand," he said again. "You ought to tell me, Mildred; but if you have not the courage," he added, 'chivalrously, "you need not. One of these days, perhaps, you may know what regret means. As for me—l care not what happens now—what I do or where I go. You have said `No.' Well, as you have given me the blow, I'll carry it. But I will take the last right of my love for you—" It was all the impulse of a moment. Before she knew what he intended. he had caught her in his arms, and was kissing her with his first and last kiss. So strong wee the crush of his embrace that Mildred felt weak and faint, for a moment lost. Then came the end. He had released her, and was going. She hardly realised. In the midst of the suddenness she was dazed, al- most stunned. But she loved him, and some- thing like a burst of light broke in upon her. She stretched out her arms after him. "Richard! . . . Dick!" But it was too late. Her broken, muffled call did not reach his ear. Already he was hidden by the clipped hedge of the tennis- court, and was quitting the Rectory grounds by way of the carriage-drive into the village road. "He will come back," she murmured to herself. "A drinker, a waster, and a gambler; too fond of gay life, and too free and loose in his respect for women. That's what Blackmore told father. But even if he is, I might have saved him. He must come back." Mildred said the same thing to herself the next day, and the next. Wellingham did not come. Instead, a week passed. Then she heard that he had sent his papers to the War Office, or had applied to be exchanged into another cavalry regiment—rumour woe a little uncertain as to which course he had taken. Bitter and weary though her thoughts were, they were as nothing compared with the horror of the tragedy that came a few daya later. Neil Blackmore had been found dead at the foot of the cliffs on which Shoreling Camp stood at Norborough. And Richard Wellingham was an absentee. While hie papers were still at the War Office he had quarrelled with his Colonel, and had retali- ated to a contemptuous taunt with a blow. By Blackmore he had been told to consider himself under arrest; be had given his parole, but had broken it. All that was known ,afterwards was that on the evening /A when ellingliam broke his parole by passing the bounds of the camp he wa& seen at a dis- tance, going towards the cliff-head. Black- more had passed that way a little while be- fore. On his own initiative, the second in • command sent an escort to bring the prisoner back to quarters; but the men met him re- turning, looking pale, disordered, and morose. The next morning the Colonel's body was found by the waves, and Wellingham was gone from the camp. And the suspicion was that Wellingham WAS a slayer, a fugitive from his fellow-men, with the brand of Cain on his brow. "Merciful Heaven! Have I been the cause 0 all this? A woman again!" Mildred , wondered. "This is what comes of living a reckless I life, I fear," said the rector, not unkindly. I "Now you can see how dangers may lurk where we little suspect them. I have a suspi- cion that Wellingham must have spoken to you. If he did, and you told him 'No,' you will see now that•you were wise." Then all Mildred's loyalty and devotion surged up in her. "Perhape some of the poison spoken about him has kept in your mind, father," she said, proudly. "But if you think he is guilty of that sin, I say—No!" But, all the some, she wept as if her heart would break.

A cruel wind, filled with cutting sleet, was blowing along the whole length of Rumble- street. A thoroughfar, more dismal was not to be found anywhere in the St. Giles's dis- trict of the Strand. It was a street with only One door in it; for the back of a block of dwellings occupied one side of it, and a bar- rack-like-building covered its whole length on the other. The double swing-door of the barrack was big enough. On each side of it was a name— " Lornfray House "—and any native of the metropolis would have known that the place was one of those mammoth lodging-houses which have sprung up in London within the past twenty years. A man paused a moment outside the build- ing, and then pushed his way awkwardly through the swing-door; for he was chilled with the numbing spitefulness of the night. He was tall and fair; but his shoulders drooped, his bare right hand clutched his thin overcoat close to his contracted chest, his wet boots seemed to drag with his slow feet, and his whole aspect was poor and forlorn. "One night," he mumbled. as he laid down sevenpence at the office ticket-window. " Seven—six—three." -

There NV RA hardly need for the loaves to give dee number et the cubicle tnat he tutu occupied the night before. For a long time peat he bad been a constant lodger, whenever he could raise the money for he bed, and th 3

clerk wrote his name mechanically on the counterfoil of the ticket—" Charles Bayham." "How's the wrapper-scrapping going, Bay- /lam?" he asked, familiarly. "Not very lively," the lodger replied, with a shake of the head..

"Wrapper scrapping," is envelope and wrapper addressing, precarious and poorly- paid work; and it seemed that it had been irregular with Charles Bayham lately. He passed on into the stony corridor leading to the dining-hall. His self-absorbed shuffle was not of a kind to turn the head. He still did not see the man who had followed him in from the street.

Like Bayham, the man obtained his ticket, and turned into the corridor. There he paused, dropping himself into a chair against the glazed brick wall. He was a fellow with a coarse. brutish face, plainly the offspring of some slum, and without any kind of soul. "By rip! Charles Bayham now, eh?" he muttered to himself. '"lt's him, though. He saw the whole shoot that day. What's he (loin' here? I thought he had done away with himself, but it's plain he didn't. Took a scare, that's about the size of it. . . By thunder! What would be up if he knew me again? . . . Then, them advertisements? It's a hundred pounds for information, whether he's living or dead. Either he can't know about 'em —can't know his uncle's dead, and that he's a lord—or else he don't mean to come out of his hole. Perhaps it's both. . . Smash!" he exclaimed to him- self, with a start, as an ugly inspiration came to him. "If that could be `worked ' it would he a bit of all-right. It would settle him—he couldn't put the nab on me if he was a dead 'un—and I could scoop up the hundred quid for the information. A bit risky; but I reckon the trick could be done with the beard on, and with the name I give 'em here. `Re Hankins" isn't in the same regiment with Bill "; but he pulled himself up, with a muttered, " Not so much of it." Then he lumbered out of the chair, and made a survey of the dining-hall through the windows in the corridor wall. On such e night as this a crowd of rough-looking fellows, foul alike in clothes and in manners, were loafing round the hot-plate on which lodgers, who did not patronise the refresh- ment-bar of the house, were free to cook their food. Presently Joe Hankins, as he called himself, picked out Bayham, warming himself by the smokc stack in the shadows. He was still a solitary figure in the crowd. "I ought to ha able to do something with him here," the ruffian muttered to himself again. "It's plain he's on his own," he added, as he SIW that Bayham spoke to nobody. "I've enly got to pass the word round, and raise the shout. Singing out 'Split' here would be the same as shouting 'Welsher ' on a racecourse. If he got knocked on the hot -plate long enough it would just about `do' for him." The vision of Bayham scorching on the flat top of the long cooking range seemed to give the scoundrel infinite satisfaction. " Split " —the rogues' word for "detective "—he mut- tered to himself again. It was evident that he had cause to fear recognition by the lonely figure in the unwholesome crowd. "I'll do it!" he said, finally. Then, with his hands in his pockets, after the manner of the creatures of the house, he pushed open the dining-room door and slouched into the dim place. He went and stood about a yard away from Bayham. Presently Bayham lifted his eyes from the floor; they came up to look at nothing in par- ticular, but they rested upon Hankins's face. "Hang! What's to be made of him?" the ruffian wondered, silently. An uncomfortable feeling of mistrust crept over Hankins. He could not tell whether Bayham had recognised him or not! Them he edged away, worming iu and out, and coming to a sherd now and then 86 he made his way round the length of the hot-plate. Each time that he paused he spoke to some rough idler like himself. " Split," he said, in an undertone. "Over there by the chimney stack. I'm going to work it up' for him. I'll give yer the nod when it's ready." Hankins was laying his plans well. The shock of coming into contact with the hot- plate's surface would alone be enough to kill any man. But so preoccupied had he been that he bad not noticed the time. He was again near Bayham, and his tongue was just ready to come to words with the inoffensive man against the stack, and to shout "Split!" when another voice intervened.

" Come, now; time, please " shouted the night porter of the house, as he came into the hall. The hour had come for extinguishing thP lights ; the rough crew began to crawl un- willingly towards the stairs leading to the cubicles, and for the moment Charles Bayham was saved. . . . .

"It IN ill keep till to-morrow night ! " Hankins muttered to himself; and he went to his sevenpenny bed wondering. 111. .

The mystification of Hankins was that he could not satisfy himself on any doubt or question that arose in his mind. But he was not alone in his wonder that night. Not more than two miles away, in a drawing-room in Curzon-street, Mayfair, was a woman whom all around her acknowledged to be wondrous fair. She was Mildred Rashleigh—Miss no longer, but Lady Rashleigh ; for the, rector's elder brother had died, so that Sydney Rash- leigh had become the Reverend and Right Hon. the Earl of ilildevor.

--- Though her thoughts were far away, Mildred was not alone. While her thoughts wandered, wondering whether Dick Welling - ham was living or dead, the Reverend Andrew Ilylton kept her close company. And he was doing more; he had asked her before, and was asking her again, to be his wife. " Won't you, Mildred? " Hylton pleaded once more. The question saddened Mildred; for she liked Andrew Hylton well enough, deepll enough to marry him happily; though shc had not the whole-soulcd love for him flux: the had given to Richard Wellingham. And it was that love that battled with her now. Yet, if Dick were dead, it seemed hard to deny herself all the sweetness of fulfilling a woman's destiny. But was it only that? Was her love for Dick not as great as if he were still alive? And perhaps he still lived. And to marry Andrew Hylton, when she had a stronger love for another man, would be doing him, she thought, a lifelong wrong. "Let me think a little longer, Mr. Hylton," she begged. " You are too good—" "Too good ! " he interjected. " What makes you say that? That was my feeling with regard to you. It seemed almost too much to hope that you would change your life in the world to share mine. Yet who has taken so much interest in my work as you have? "

_ What Hylton said was true. She had taken a sincere interest in his work, in strange by- places, and she had helped him in it. But she did not tell him that when she had, gone to sing at his services there had often been behind her a hope that she might come upon • Richard WeHingham_ "It is you who are too good, Lady Mildred," . Hylton went on, dropping into formality after the answer she had given him. "My only right is to respect your wish ; can only hope .on. To-morrow, Thursday evening, I am . going to preach at St. Peter's, in Tarling-street, and I hoped that you might go with me as my promised wife. Lornfray House is close by, and I thought you might like to look over it, especially as we are going to hold a service there next Sunday evening." " What is Lornfray House " Mildred asked. " A lodging-house,'' Hylton explained; " one of the big places for men which have sprung up in the past few years." " I will go with you," Mildred said, quietly. Neither she nor Hylton saw that a pair of eyes in a group only a yard away were fixed upon • them. They were not pleasing eyes, though their owner, Kenneth Harlow, was thi possesfior of a handsome face of the hard kind. And now, though Mildred little dreamt of it, there was danger. For Harlow knew of her early love for his cousin, Richard Wel- liughain. On his mother's side—she having hen Judith Wellingham—he was next-of-kin i; the inheritance of the Wellinguam estates, IN Melt, a.: the son of a younger brother, Richard Wellinglialo had nevcr hoped 10 USSeS3. A 1 thi-yugh Harlow could never wear the title of Viscount Ilindlesleigh, it was no interest of hi 3 that Richard should return to

claim the title and estates. Still leas

..... cause to welcome the reappearance of his cousin now, when, seven years being nearly past, application was about to be made to the Law Courts to assume Richard Wellingham's death. Presently the Dowager Lady Conhurst took possession of Hylton, and Harlow seized his opportunity; for the same moment left him frea to secure Mildred alone. "Good evening, Lady Mildred," he greeted her, in his pleasantest manner. "I have hoped for this moment. Am I too bold to claim you all to myself for this once? I want to tell you something that I can say only to you. . ." For a moment he paused, while he looked boldly into her face. "It is something that means everything to a man," he went on. "Who knows how it is? We can't tell, but I love you. And to-night I could not hold back any longer. I wanted to ask you whether you would be my wife? " Harlow had phrased himself well, but his skilful words had only had the effect of bear- ing clown Mildred's gaze to the floor. " Mr. Harlow," she said, " I think you must have heard that I had all your cousin's misfortunes to my fault. I cannot forget him. Don't you think it would be kinder to let me be alone? I know you have tried to press me many times. but it pains me. I could not marry you. Please do not think me unmind- ful—and I do not wish to hurt you—but that must be my answer." Although Harlow inclined his head in acquiescence, his face was dark. ' Very well, Lady Mildred," he said, sub- missively. "But while there's life there's hope," he added, in a curious, significant tone. Then he left her; and the next moment, al- though he hardly realised his object in it, a strange prompting came to him. He looked around the company for Hylton, and then made his way towards the clergyman, as if to come face to face with him casually. " Hallo! Hylton," he said, cordially, as they met; "Mill busy, I suppose? Where will you be preaching next Sunday—at your own Aurch? '

"No," Hylton replied; "I am taking a party to conduct a mission at Lornfray House." " That will be interesting," Harlow re- turned at once. "Lornfray HOMY:: is one of the new lodging-houses, isn't it? May I come?" "We're always pleased to have helpers,' assented the simple Hylton, unsuspectingly. And while all these things were passing in the rich warmth of Mayfair, the man whom Hankins had called Charles Eoyham wre walking the cheerless streets. Not once, but a number of times, Hankins had anathe- matised the porter who had shouted out: "Come, now; time, please !" For the next night had not seen Bayham an inmate of Lornfray House. Nor had several nights. Yet the wheels of fate were moving fast. Thursday night came, bringing Bayham back to Lornfray House, with a forlorn ninepence in his pocket. Again the night was cold. And it was still young; for the first call at seven o'clock, for lodgers who wished to be admitted to their beds at that hour, • had not yet been given. No danger threatened Bayham for the mo- ment, as Hankins had not arrived yet. . . But presently be came. His eyes searched at once for his quarry amongst the non- descript figures loafing _arouifd the glow- ing plate, and once more the temptation of the hundred pounds' reward, and the pressure of his own uneasy fear, was upon him. He was already contriving his evil design. Passing at that moment through the recrea- tion, the rending, and the writing-rooms, and escorted by the manager of the house, were a man and a woman. They were the Reverend Andrew Hylton and Lady Mildred Rnshleigh. "It will familiarise you with the place for next Sunday evening," Hylton said to Mil- dred, as they passed from the reading-room into the corridor opening into the dining-hall. At the sonic moment a tall, drooping figure stepped into the corridor from the lower door of the dining-place. The "seven-o'clock bed" had just been called.

For an instant the tall lodger faced towards the main entrance. With something like a mothered start, os it seemed, he turned quickly, and disoppenred round the corner of the corridor.

Of Mildred's shock there could be no doubt. Ifer face poled; a name was upon" her lips, but they trembled pitifully. Her arms half stretched out after the destitute-looking figure. By the time she reached the foot of the 6taire the lodger had passed up to the dormitorica. and that way she could not go. Once more the Fates had watched over Charles fßavliam. But there was still Sunday to come. What would it bring forth?

IV. Friday and Saturday passed, and Bayham was again a stranger to Lornfray House. Once more he woe penniless and a vagrant. But on Sunday he was fortunate; he found a dropped shilling in the street. At six o'clock, when the ticket - office ripened, he paid his sevenpence and passed Lornfray House. The place was crowded, out he sat down to a meal of pea-coup, bread, and pudding, which he purchased at the re- freshment-Lar. Then he went to the chim- ney-stack to collect all the warmth he could until half-past seven. At that hour he was going to bed, the only place in which his destitute cheerlessness could be blotted out in oblivion. Some time litter he glanced at the clock, and saw that the hour wee five minutes to seven. But he did not see Joe Hankins glanc- ing ominously at him through the corridor windows. Nor did Hankins see the well. dressed stranger who had come into the house almcst at his heels, and who was now stand- ing behind him, looking curiously through the window at the unfamiliar scene in the hall. Hankins had been drinking at a near tavern since six o'oelek, and he was now in an evil mood. He did not hear a muffled exclama- tion as the glance of the stranger fell upon the tall lodger by the smoke-stack. "You're 'ere then now, Mr. Bayham, are yer? " be snarled. " Now's my chance with you, I think." "Do you know that man, then?" asked the newcomer behind him; and Hankins turned sullenly to face Kenneth Harlow. "I ought to, seeing as how—" Then he stopped short suspiciously. "Well. I know him too," Harlow went on, speaking in a tone officially suggestive of a police agent. "Yon don't think him much good, eh? Well, he's a man I want to see cornered. Here's a sovereign," he added, slipping the coin 'into the ruffian's hand. "You can get him out of it, I darcsay." "All right ; you leave it to me! " muttered Hankins, putting upon Harlow's " out " his own interpretation. "I rather reckon I can gct him out !" The scamp slipped into the dining-hall, and Harlow watched him moving about amongst the rough crew around the plate. At that mo- ment the sound of a hymn came from the read- ing-room. It reached Bayham's ear, above the uncouth din around him, and he listened. Then he moved 'away towards the reading. room. Nothing interrupted him, and he en- tered the reading-room by its back door. In RI! instant he faced about, and retreated hurriedly. He did not know Andrew Hylton, nor the clergyman's party; but his first glance had fallen upon Mildred Rashleigh. So quick had been his movement that he hord he had not been seen. But he was mistaken. From the moment of Icn_r entry., Mildred's eyes had keen glancing everywhere. And now, quickly and unob- trusively, she slipped from her chair, and passed through the near front door into the corridor. She was just in time to see Bayham hasten into the dining-hall. " Richard ! . . Dick!" she cried out. But the wind in the draughty place slammed the door behind Bayham, and drowned her voice. Then she saw Kenneth Harlow btoseen herself and the door by which Bayham had entered, and she started. Near her was the bay of the lower door of the hall, and she stepped into it. She glanced through the door's glass patvds, looking for Bayham. but for some moments she besqated tremblingly, fearing to enter amongst the crowd of riff- : raff men. Mourning in America.—Amerioan women have for some time past leaned to white for mourning wear, and since the Titantio disaster it has become quite general—amoug those who on afford it. The fabrics employed are slft satin with a dull finish, silk cashmere, crepe de chine, hiffon, bengaline and taffeta. White hats, veils, stocking., shoes, gloves, and parasols, are part of this mourning costume, and no jewellery of any kind ,is worn. With us, white for mourning is creeping into favcur, and for country wear it is little if any more expensive than black, which so soon gets dingy in the sun and dust. Those however, who feel I that white is beyond their means have still the dis- tinctly economical greys to fall back upon.

A Pretty Af cassory.—The idea of the summer muff is quite a pr, tty ore for a garden party or other smart function. It may be characterised al useful too, for it can be made to take the place of a vanity bag, or reticule, and the wearer dispenses with gloves—always an expensive item—if she chooses. It is fabricated of lace, tulle, genze, chiffon, or any diaphanous material, and trimmed with flowers (which are generally arranged on the top) the bag shape being preferred as a rule. The summer muff gives a note of colour—always repeated in the ruffle and hat—to white lingerie dresses. The Shirt if Tulle.—Dresses of tulle have been in and out of fashion for many generations, I ut the tulle shirt, now popular with white coats and skirts, is quite a new idea. To tuck it, as is usual with lawn and linen, would be difficult, hence it is trimmed with smsll ruches. The sleeves are long, the collar high, and decorated with ruches.

London's Floral Ft"tte.—London bas never seen any- thing lile the forthcoming " Alexandra Day" fke on the 26th June, but Vienn has its Daffodil Day, and Berlin its Marguerite Day, the proceeds of which are devoted to charitable objects. It is greatly to be hoped that fine weather will favour the occasion next week, when 10,000 lady flower-sellers will brighten the streets of the metropolis with baskets of artificial wild roses. There will be many women of title among them, and they will all wear white frocks with strawheits trimmed with wild roses. lhey will work in pairs, and it is expected that each seller will average SOO roses. The dainty little floral emblems are to be sold for a penny I each, ttit it is probable that a good many purchasers will offer a silver coin and not require change. The proceeds of the fete are to ro to the hosp'tals and con- valescent homes, in which Queen Alexandra is particu- larly interested. The floral fete on this occasion will I be confined to greater London, but it is hoped next year to extend it to all the large provincial towns, and make a national observance of Alexandra Day. Co-operation in Holidays.—The vacation-holiday bureau system is not so popular here as it is in Arcerica and Australia, but it has many advantages which work- ing girls might make use of. As it is usually worked, the vacation bureau receives the girls' subscriptions.— 1 what little savings they can effect from time to time, for which they receive vouchers. When holiday time comes, the members receive cards which entitle them to be placed on a minimu u scale at the boarding house, or small hotel, at which they elect to stay. The girls are offered the choice of both a large number of places, and es'ablisbments, and the cards, one half of which is retained by the landlady, and the other by the visitor, invite any comment which either party may wi.ti to make. Both halves are returned eventually to the Bureau, provid'ng a double cheek a 3 regards treatment and behaviour, and it is obvious that the girls stand to get much better value for their money under this co-operative system than thy are likely to obtain independently, on their own resources.

CANAL BED GIVES WAY. Early on Thursday morning an alarming burst In the bed of the Great Central Railway Company's Canal at Gurnet, resr Macciefield, took place. At the point of the burst the canal is carried over an aqueduct standing above the road. The great rush of water flooded two old cottages in the vicinity. As soon as the occurrence was discovered measures were taken to dam each side of the aqueduct and then turn the remainder of the water into the River Bollin, which runs close by.

Moore.

SUNDAY SCHOOL ANNIURSARY.—On Sunday last the Suncay school anniversary services were held in con- nection with the Moore Wesleyan Church. The services wer) held at 2.30 p.m. and 6,30 p,m., the Rev. F. W. Kernick, M.A., of Blackburn, and late of the Warrington Circuit, was the preacher. Special music was rendered by the choir and scholars, under the leadership of Mr. James Autrobus, the choirmaster, Mr. John Banner presiding at the organ. Although the weather in the morning was anything but good, it ....leered up by noon for the remainder of the day, and there were very good congregations at both services.—The collections for Sunday school funds amounted to .E 5 O.

SUNDA.Y SCHOOL TEACHERS' TRIP.—The annual trip of the St. Thomas's Church Sunday school teachers took place on Saturday, when Chester was visited. The party, which included the Vicar (the Rev J Collins)and the curate (the Rev E W Clarke), numbered 29. They had a delightful river trip from the Groves to Eccleston and Eaton Hall and also inspected the various "sights " of the old city. ST. THoMAS'S CHURCH.—The Rev. E. W. ST. HELENS.

MISTAKE THAT ENDED IN DEATH.

The inquest was held at St. Helens On Tuesday on James Woke, 62, a chemical works labourer, of 75, Manor-fattest, Peasley Cross, who died in the St. Helens Hospital, on Friday, from injuries received at the Kurtz's worhs of the United Alkali Company. White was instructed to take the man lid off No. 2 Causticizer which was empty and which he wanted cleaning out, but by mistake he went to No. 3 Causticizer, which was full of liquid caustic, and was badly burned by the caustic squirting over him. Ernest White, deceased's son, said that when be saw his father in hospital he said " I made a sad mistake, I went to No 3. instead of No. 2." A statement by George Hitchin, who was working with White, was presented by Detective-Sergeant Staveley. Hitchin is confined to hospital by injuries received at the same time as White. He stated that White went up the steps to the top of the causticizer to see if the " agitater " was ready and on coming down the steps used a plank to stand on. He then commenced to take a bolt out of the mud door of N 0.3. Immediately be had got the bolt loose the boiling caustic poured on to both of them. He (Hitobin) was almost blinded but managed to walk to a water tap where he was promptly assisted by some workmen, who washed the caustic MY him, He was badly burned, and was attended by Dr E Reid and taken to hospital. The jury returned a verdict of " Accidental death," and suggested that numbers should be fixed on the causticizers to indicate when they were full. Mr ttudge, the manager, promised that the suggestion should be carried out.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

EXCITING SCENE IN THE MERSEY.

FLAT COLLIDES WITd TRANSPORTER BRIDGE There was an exciting scene in the river Mersey at Runcorn on Tuesday afternoon. There was an uausual swell on the tide, and the flat Celestial, 100 tons, belong- ing to Messrs. W. Cooper and Sons, of Widnes, laden with copper ore, attempted to drop her anchor near Runcorn Itailwsy Bridge. The anchor fai'ed to hold, and the boat, becoming unmanageable era, bed through one of the small arches of the bridge—never used by craft in the ordinary course,—and damaged her stern badly by violently colliding with the stone piers of the bridge. She was then carried rapidly on the swell in the direction ot the pier of the Transporter bridge a$ the Widnes side, which is only a few feet above high-water mark. Ciptain Hatton and the mate, O'Gara, realising the danger, jumped into the small boat. Excited speotators shouted to the passengers waiting for the Transporter oar on the pier, and there was a scamper for safety. A moment after- wards the craft struck the pier near the ticket office with great force, her masts snapping off with a crash. Fortunately the anchor chain and rigging became entangled with the pillars of the bridge and held her, land after repeated attempts her crew and others boarded I her. The tug Firefly was chartered and towed her into dock. She was badly damaged, and the bridge bore marks of the collision. The incident PM watched by a large crowd.

===== 0002601_19120622_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN AND THE BRINE RILL.

In the proceedings before the House of Lords with reference to the Cheshire Brine Bill on •fuesdny, Mr J Ashton, clerk to the Rural District Council of Runcorn, said it would be a very serious thing if anything was done which would necessitate the ultimate removal of the Castner-lielner works, which had n rateable VAllle of £4OO, and employed from 550 to 650 persons. It would also be a serious loss to the local tradesmen. Cross-examined. His council refused to meet the Cheshire County Council on this bill. While the bill proposed to leave a margin on the present quantity taken thro ugh the pipe to Weston Point, he thought it was not rig ht to makeany limit. His council had not considered the question of allowing or preventing brine going out of the country ; probably they would object to brine being taken out. Mr Robert H Posnett, managing director of a large tanning works at Rnncorn, also gave evidence against the bill, for the Urban District Council of Runcorn.

GRANTS TO POOR LAW OFFICERS' SALARIES. The Finnslet Union wrote asking the Board to support a resolution expressing the opinion of this Board that the limitation of the amount of Exchequer contribution in respect of the salaries of Poor Law officers and the cost of drugs and medical appliances to the expenditure actually incurred during the financial year 1887-8 operates unfairly in the case of the many Unions where vast improvements in administration have been made during the past 24 years, especially in the case of the sink and children, by casting the whole burden of such improvement upon the local ratepayers. The Clerk pointed out that there was no telling how their Board would fare in the matter, if it were re-adjusted. Mr Morris moved that the matter be left with the Parliamentary Committee. A Voice : Will that ba this year ? Mr Linaker, jun: Some timethis century. (Laughter) On the proposition of Mr Davies, the matter as tabled.

===== 0002601_19120622_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
When the United Alkali Company removed their works from Runcorn to Fleetwood the district lost 6,000 of their inhabitants, 600 houses became empty, and for years the town was on the verge of the utmost poverty With the development of the Salt Union's works at Weston Point, and with the advent of other industries they had got back about 4,000 of the population they had lost. On Thursday the Lords' Committee decided not te allow the Bill to proceed. Upon the news of the defeat of the Bill being received there was great jubilation at , Runcorn, which town more than any other stood to lose severely by any restrictions on brine. Flags were hoisted on the Salt Union offices and the public build- ings, and the buzzers at the new salt works and at the Castner-Kellner works were sounded continuously for a long period in the evening. The Salt Work's brass band paraded the town, playing triumphal airs, and the officials of the Salt Union were heartily congratulated on all bands.

===== 0002601_19120622_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Connell as held at Dutton on Tuesday, Mr Antwiss (vice-chairman) presiding, with Mr G Piling In the vice-chair. Letters were read from the Local Government Board enclosing order re clerks' extra remuneration and also orders TO extra remuneration to surveyors. A letter was also read from the Town Clerk of Warrington enclosing agreement for the supply of water to Dingle Farm, Appleton, and it was decided that the seal of the Council he affixed to the agreement

THE MEDICAL OFFICER. Dr Adams wrote to the effect that be was slightly Improved in health and hoped to be in attendance at the next meeting of the Council. RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday Mr Spruce presiding.

TM': VACCINATION ACT.

The Leicester Board of Guardians wrote asking the Board to support the following resolution passed by them in regard to the Vaccination Act :—" That the Leicester Board of Guardians, believing that the present state of law in regard to vaccination is moat unsatis- factory and having regard to the enormous increase in the numbers of exemption certificates granted, is of the opinion that the time is now opportune for the repeal of the Vaccination Acts and therefore views with gratification the introduction into the House of Commons of a Bill to achieve that object and trusts that the Government well afford facilities for its speedy passage into law. Mr Morris said he was of the opinion that the 'Board ought to support the resolution with all their power. He was a firm believer in vaccination himself and he thought it would be for the best if the present law was repealed and made more stringent. A Voice : They do not say that ; they want to abolish vaccination altogether. Mr Linaker was of the opinion that it would not be advisable for them to do anything to aboli:h the exist- ing law without knowing what they were going to have substituted. The Leicester Board did not state any— thing about what would be done in the event of the Act being repealed. He proposed that the letter be left on the table. This course was agreed to.

===== 0002601_19120622_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
THE KHEDIVE OF EGYPT. Abbas Pasha Hilmi, Khedive of Egypt, sovereign of Nubia, of the Soudan, of Kor- dofan, and of Darfur—to give him his full title—is the latest Royal visitor to our shores, and is expected to reach Windsor on Friday to spend the week-end at Windsor Castle as the guest of the King and Queen. He is a handsome man of thirty-eight, all accomplished musician, a skilled engineer and scientist, a keen business man, a clever farmer and horse-breeder, and a daring motorist. In addition, he is reported to be a fluent speaker in seven languages.

===== 0002601_19120622_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
HOLIDAY ALLOWANCE FOR OFFICERS.

The Chairman moved that the holiday allowance to Workhouse officers be Bs. 6d. per week in lieu of rations. In doing so he said the matter bad been dealt with by the House Committee and they had passed a resolution to the effect that the officers be made the allowance stated.

Mr. Morris seconded and the motion was carried unanimously.

-THE MENTAL DEFICIENCY BILL.

The West Bromwich Union forwarded a circular dealing with the Mental Deficiency Bill now before Parliament pointing out that the Bill seemed to pro- vide that the authority to deal with the question of mentally deficient people was the County Council or County Borough. They pointed out that the best thing to do would be to leave the matter with the Board of Guardians who were the most competent authority and protested very strongly against the proposals of the Bill. They asked the Board to support the following resolution "That the powers which the Guardians possessed be extended and that it be their duty to supervise all cases and when necessary and expedient to protect the patients under guardianship," and also that the question of disfran- chisement be left with Boards of Guardians.

---- Mr. Davies moved that the Guardians support the resolution.

Mr. Piling said it was the right thing to do in their district where arrangements were being made for the treatment of patients. Mr. Davies : It should rest with the Guardians because they know more about these cases than anyone Mr. Hayes thought the right course had been taken. They were quite right in maintaining their dignity as a Board of Guardians and also in maintaining their rights. They should keep the powers and rights they bad at present. (Hear, hear). It was decided that the resolution have the unanimous support of the Board. SIR VICTOR HORSLEY'S FRE.

The Clerk said that Sir Victor Horsley's fee for treat- ing a patient suffering from mental trouble was £3 33. The House Committee recommended that the patient be sent to be treated and that the master take charge. The Board signified their approval of the committee's action.

COAL SUPPLIES. The tenders for the supply of 120 tons of coal and 150 tons of slack were considered and it was decided that the tenders of Messrs The Wigan Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., be accepted for coal, and that Messrs Taylor, Frith and Co. supply the quantity of slack. NURSE'S RESIGNATION. The resignation of Miss A Lievesey, assistant nurse at the institution was accepted.

===== 0002601_19120622_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FREEDOM OF THE TEACHER.

EDUCATION COMMITTEE AND PLACE OF RESIDENCE.

PROPOSED NEW RULE REJECTED.

The Warrington Education Committee spent some time on Monday evening in discussing the advisability of laying down a regulation as to the place of residence of the teachers who may in future come into their service. The School Management Committee had resolved That all trachers hereafter appointed to schools maintained by the committee be required to give an undertaking that they will be preparea to reside within a reasonable distance of the school, and that they will, if required by the managers of the school and the committees, remain .at the school for a period of not less than one year. At a meeting on Saturday the War ington and Dis- trict Teachers' Association placed on record their alarm at the possibility of such a resolution as the above being passea as, in their view, it interfered with personal liberty. The association was of opinion that, provided teachers attended punctually and regularly and did their work in the school to the satisfaction of the committee, the place of residence should be left to the choice of the individual teacher. This resolu- tion was read to the Education Committee. As the Rev. Canon Stevenson had not arrived at the 1 meeting, Mr. Ursorl moved the adoption of the minutes of the School Management Committee. Tie did so, he said, with some reluctance, if not with diffidence. He believed there was some considerable fear on the part of the teachers lest the resolution with regard to the place of residence should operate retrospectively and mean a great deal more than it definitely expressed. The terms of the resolution were somewhat vogue and needed definition. The phrase " reasonable distance" seemed to have aroused considerable disturbance in the minds of the teachers, especially of those who were living just outside the borough. Many of them had undertaken the purchase of their houses and made arrangements which could not legitimately be disturbed without incurring great financial loss. There was, however, no desire on the part of the committee to compel teachers to live within the borough or within a specified radius of their schools—in fact it conduced to the health and efficiency of the teachers that they should have to walk or cycle some distance each day. What it was sought to prevent was teachers I raking up oppointments in Warrington and continuing to live in places like Liverpool where, haying missed one train they could not arrive at their schools until a considerable portion of the morning session had passed away. The committee had found that teaohers living in Liverpool had applied for places in Warrington because there were no vacancies in their Own city, but had remained in residence in Liverpool, kept their names before the Liver- pool committee and after serving a few months in the Warrington schools, seized the earliest oppor- tunity of returning to Liverpool, much to the detri- ment of the work in the schools and to the hindrance of true educational progress. It was a disastrous thing that children of one class should come under the control of two or three teachers in one year. A similar clause to the one proposed had governed this appoint- ment of teachers iu the Secondary school during the past five years. I Councillor JENKINS seconded. ,1 Councillor BIRTLES pointed out that the resolution I would only apply to future appointments, and the teachers already in the employ of the authority had no cause for alarm.

—...,........ ......—. Councillor 13ELLIAN said some very good reason must be given before be could support the proposition, because it seemed to deprive the teachers of freedom of choice. .

The CHAIRMAN (Mr A .1 Jolley) called attention to the provision that, if the committee desired it, anyone receiving an appointment should undertake to stay for at least one year. That condition was made by many authorities and, in his view, was quite reasonable. As to the place of residence, the interests of teachers at present in the employ of the committee were not affeoted, and the resolution distinctly said "All teachers hereafter appointed." Alderman BENN= said he did not like the resolution at all. It seemed to inflict on a very deserving body of public servants a sort of indignity. It put them in a class by themselves and to seme extent restricted other freedom of choice. If teachers adopted a course which prevented them discharging their duties satisfactorily the committee had the right to dismiss them, and that should be sufficient without any new regulation. Councillor JENKINS considered the resolution was in keeping with what many of the members of the com- mittee had been trying to do—encourage the develop- ment of tilt ir own teiicaers in their own district. Councillor 13F.tmaN moved an amendment that the minute be referred back. The provision as to length of service was, he admitted, perfectly reasonable but he did not see any reason for the regulation of the place oflresidence. Alderman BENNETT seconded. Mr R T FAIRCLOUGH said the resolution would be useful as it would prevent Liverpool teachers making useless applications for posts in Warrington. The CHAIRMAN said the resolution was passed to secure greater efficiency in the working of the schools, and for no other purpose. Mr. C. E. PARKER said he recognized the object for which the resolution was passed but, on the whole, he had come to the conclusion that it was an unwise pro- position and he should support the amendment. It would be better to leave it to the managers of the schools to exercise their discretion in the matter. The very vagueness of the term " within reasonable dis- tance" was liable to abuse. Anybody who thought that Warrington appointments should be conferred to Warrington lads and lasses would possibly use that proposition to exclude anyone from the village which he bailed. (Laughter.) If the committee was going to lay down any resolution for the guidance of the authority and the managers, it should be so drafted that there could be no doubt about its meaning. Mr. IiJP:,ON said he adraittad that the resolution was open to a varied interpretation, which would depend on the constitution of the committee. . .

------ The amendment referring the resolution back was carried by a large majority.

EVENING SPORTS.

About 800 spectators thoroughly enjoyed the pro- gratatne of six events decided on the ground of the Warrington Football Club on Thursday with the follow- ing results:—

The final was a grand race, inches separated the lot. Spencer looked a winner 20 yards from the tape, but Grounds finished splendidly and the well-earned verdict was S Grounds first, J Spencer second, 29 competed out of an entry of 35. SCHOOLBOYS RELAY RACE.—Twenty-five teams of three with each boy to ru'n 100 yards had entered and all competed in five heats, each ot which provided good sport into which the youngsters entered with a keenness I which was grand to see. The heats were won after rousing contests by St. Mary's A Team, Heathside A Team National B Team, Arpley-street A Team, Christ Church A Team. Siddall. just recovering from an influenza attack, at scratch. With so many young athletes in the district, these sports provided a much-needed opportunity for com- petition, for those whose pocket money is unequal to the expense attached to open competition. The venture was deserving of better support, but there is no reason why such local competitions should not be more frequent. Messrs. H. Ashton and E. Osborne were judges, and Messrs. ANGLING.

WARRIN GTON WORKINGMEN'S ASSOCIATION

(N.A. CENTRE).

• The season for course fish opened with very unsatis- factory weather which must have kept a great number away, Norton being the most favoured place. Chester, Hartford, Norton and Long Barn were visited and we noticed several Northern Anglers from other districts were paying Hartford a visit. The rain kept oil and they were well pleated with the outing, although the train service is not very good for anglers visiting Hartford.

CHESTER. The Dee and canal were tried here for coarse fish, and fair sport was had, but none of any great size we:6a taken. The fish from the river were cleaner than those of the canal.

HARTFORD. Fair sport was had here by a party of Northern Anglers who visited the Weaver for a trial for this week's match. Most of them got amongst the small fry to get their balance and were satisfied with their visit alter spending a couple of hours. a NORTON. The canal here was in good condition and most of the locals made their first turn-out. Several good roach, perch and eels were taken and remarks were passed on!..thei roach being clean. Messrs Hardman Potter, Singleton and Hewitt took the best specimens, T Singleton weighing in a gudgeon. The specials will not go a-begging for this water by appearances. Hardman's perch was almost Soz, and several roach from 6oz up to Boz were taken. W Hewitt's couple of eels was well over the Jlbs and a nice eel was taken well over lib by a memner early on in the morning'

THE GOWY. There was a falling off of members here,the unsettled conditions accounting for it. Sport was not what it should have been. There can be no cry for rain, for this stream must have been well flushed. One member returned with one trout and about lA dozen eels and one with a jack about lib from the buttons end, others had several eels, but we have heard of no coarse fish being taken. MISCELLANEOUS. The draw for the benefit of F Singleton will take place on Monday evening next. Please send in money and duplicates to the secretary as early as possible. The society is offering three medals this season in addition to their usual amount of prizes, one for the opening match at Billing's Green in July, one for the officials' match at Vale Royal in August and one for the annual match at Newbr‘dge. They have been kindly given by patrons of the society. A.S.

UNITED TEMPERANCE CAMPAIGN.

The second meeting in continuation of the open-air campaign in Warrington vias held at Bridge Foot on Thursday last week. Mr T Dale presided over a large audience. A contingent trom the Warrington Band of Hope Union Choir very beautifully rendered several part songs and glees. The Chairman said that the drink traffic was the greatest moral, physical, and political enemy in Warrington and in the country. Their work was to educate the people to realise the peril that was threatening. It was necessary that the children should be educated in the principles of total abstinence. In their Band of Hope Union they had 3,000 children. If in the past we had had the same kind of teaching in our day schools and in the Bands of Hope as was now given the Temperance Cause to-day would be in a far more advanced state than it is. Warrington was in an unenviaale position with regard to statistics for drunkenness but they were out to try and make their town purer and better for their work. Mr. William Canning F.M.S.A. (Manchester), delivered an interesting and (convincing address. He said that temperance advocates were often abused for being men of one idea. For his part he thought it was a thing to be proud of always provided the idea was a good one. If they had one idea and it was their own, it was one more than the average man had for ideas were largely adopted, ready made. It was their work to get this idea that alcohol was the great enemy of our country into the heads aud hearts of the people. The speaker went on to show the injurious action of alcohol upon the blood corpuscles—the effect spreading from them to every part of the body.

HOPE COLLECTING SOCIETY AND THE INSURANCE ACT.

Now the time is close at hand when this Act will come into operation it behoves all persons who are to become members (and it is row fairly well understood who have to become insured) to carefully select which society they will join for the several purposes of the Act. It is of the utmost importance that each intend- ing member should join a society that is well managed and one where they can lbe in close touch with its officers and committee of management, so that they may have a voice in its working without unnecessary trouble. The Hope Collecting Society, Warrington, has decided to become approved. It is a local and old established society, and has made rapid strides during recent years. The committee is composed entirely of Warrington men who have had a vast experience of Friendly Society work, such as will be necessary in the administration of this Act in all its dealings, Such a society should commend itself to our readers and those who have not already joined an approved society would do well to consider the Hope Collecting Society. It will be seen from our advertising columns that information will gladly be given by any of their agents or the secretary.

LANDLORD AND TENANT. THE ALLEGED ASSAULT AT GREAT SANKEY. Singular Story at tile County Court.

I

~ i,

I

The hearing was resumed at the Warrington County Court, on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Stanger, K.C., of the action in which Arthurina Lmse, wife of Frank R Lowe, Mount Pleasant, Liverpool- road, Great Sankey, claimed .4.:30 from Charles H. Johnstone, builder, Great Saukey. as damages for an assault alleged to have been committed by the defendant on February 15th, while Mr and Mrs Lowe were leaving a house in Bighfield-avenue, Great Sankey, of which defendant was the owner. Mr Browne appeared for the plaintiff and Mr Burgis, barrister (instructed by Mr T E Knowles) represented the defendant. The case for the plaintiff was that, after haviog repeatedly complained to the defendant of the uninhabitable condition of a house called Melrose, Highfield Avenue, Mr Lowe decided to leave it. On the date of the alleged assault the removal was taking place when the defendant, after having sent some workmen to inspect the premises, himself entered the house. The plaintiff took exception to his having done so without permission and refused to allow urn to go upstairs. She stood on the stairs in front of him, whereupon the defendant knocked her down and pushed her, step by step, on her back up the stairs. Eventually, while sha was lying across the stairs, defendant trampled on her left leg and got past her It was alleged that plaintiff suffered severe injuries and intense pain through the assault. At the resumed hearing, Dr. McKenzie, of Widnes, continued his evidence as to the injuries alleged to have been caused by the assault. In answer to Mr. Burgis, he stated that as a result of an examination which Dr. For and he made, they were satisfied that there were no permanent internal injuries. Witness agreed that many women developed bruises with the least possible pressure but he did not consider that Mrs Lowe was exceptional in that respect. Her skin was not more likely to bruise than that of other ladies. She was a lady of somewhat excitable character. .

Mr. Burgis : Suppose she has been concerned in what I may call a row and a certain amount of violence has been used, it might very easily upset her ?—No doubt. Suppose the is a woman who has been having trouble with her husband and has been turned out of her house, that might easily upset Ler ? Quite so. Would you agree with me that vomiting and fainting fits are common symptoms where there is hysteria ? Quite so. lam going to suggest that this lady is of what you might call rather hysterical character ?—No, I don't think I should go as far as that. But you would go a little way with me ?—I have gone a little way. Suppose she was a lady who was slightly inclined to hysteria. I suppose one of the effects you would not be surprised to find would be that there might be slight exaggeration ?—That is possible. If a person was suffering from shock, you would not recommend stimulants?— Stimulants are sometimes useful, but I very- seldom advise them. I have allowed Mrs. Lowe to take a little as I thought at the time it was worth while her doing so. John Southern Patten, retired stationmaster, High- field-avenue, (7 reat Sankey, said he lived next door to the house in which Mr and Mrs Lowe formerly resided. On the date iu question he was having dinner when Mrs Lowe ran up to his front door. The thumb of her left band was bleeding and there was a mark, as if made by a boot, on the left side of her dress. There was dirt on the back of her dress as if she had been lying on the stairs. In consequence of what Mrs. Lowe said, witness went into her house and saw the dt fendant standing at the top of the stairs Witness said : "Did you consider it gentlemanly to assault a lady? No man would do it. Nobody but a cur would.' Defendant made no reply. Later defendant said he was bound to be in the house because he had been told that Mr. Lowe intended to leave without paying the rent. Mrs. Lowe returned to the house and in witness's presence, took oil defendant's cap and threw it on the floor. Witness put his arm in front of her and told her to be quiet as there was another remedy in such a case as that. On May Stb, the defendant, conversing with witness about the action entered against him, said be went to the house to fetch his workmen away as their time had to be paid for and it might become costly. Witness replied : "But that is a different tale from what you told me when you were in Mr. Lowe's house. Why didn't you whistle or shout to them to come out?'

Mr. Burgis: Mr Johnstooe was a courteous man, as far as you know, apart from this occasion? -Mr. Johnstone and I are friendly enough. So far as you know, you always found him a perfect gentleman ?—I don't know so much about gentleman. I suggest you have never known him to to other than courteous.?—Well if I haven't, others have. You have always found him truthful ?—I expect so, same as myself. (Laughter). Did ycu hear Mrs Lowe say anything about the defendant having been sacked for dishonesty ?—She told him, when he charged her with attempting to get out of the house without paying the rent, not to put her husband on the low level that Mr. Johnstone bad been, that she had been given to understand that be was dismissed from the works at a moment's notice. There was no mention of dishonesty. She looked to be very excited ?—the was excited, quite naturally. I take it that under similar circumstances, you your- self might not have kept your temper ?—Berhaps you are measuring a peck cut of your own sack. (Laughter). Should you have felt like striking her, if she had taken your cap off?- No. I should not. I should havelaughed and put the cap on again. Mrs. Caroline Jane Prescot, 121, Dixon-street, Widnes, who assists the plaintiff in her house- work, said that prior to the assault Mrs. Lowe was notl subject to fainting fits and she had always been able to attend to her home duties. Since the assault she bad not been able to do so. Her leg was black as the result of the encounter. Emily Hawthorne, manageress of the confectionery department of the Warrington Co-operative Stores, spoke to Mrs. Lowe fainting in the shop on March 27th. Frank R. Lowe, husband of the plaintiff, said be was a chemist at the United Alkali Works, Widnes, and a lecturer in chemistry at the Warrington Technical Institute. Up to the time of the assault be did not remember his wife ever having fainted, and she was always well enough to do her housework. Mr. Browns: Have you ever turned her out cf the •!'• house ?—Never. It is a pure invention. , It has also been suggested that your wife is intemperate. Is there a shadow of truth in that? - Mr. Burgis: It has not been suggested. Mr. Browne : Mrs. Lowe was hiahly indignant at , the suggestion made at, the last hearing as to her taking stimulants. His Honour: If the language in which the question was put conveyed that, Mr. Burgis says he did not - intend it to do so and therefore he makes no sugges- tion whatever of that character. Continuing his evidence, Mr. Lowe stated that on returning home after the alleged assault, he found his wife was very much upset. She was crying and was highly nervous, and could not settle down. She cu - ' plained bitterly of the way in capleh she had been treated. Ever since that day she had been very nervous and hardly able to sleep at night. Witness . had often asked the defendant to inspect the house, but he never came. The place was not habitable and ;. at one time rain was coming through like a fountain. ; Witness had no intention whatever of avoiding payment of rent. By Mr. Burgis : He was surprised to hear now that the house had been re-let. Have your relations with ycur wife been happy relations ?—Always. Can you explain how it came about that when your wife was asked whether you turned her out at night, she refused to answer the question ?—Yes. She wanted to know what that had to do with an assault case. Mr. Browne : I advised her to refuse to answer. Is it not a fact that you have done so ?—Certainly not. I suggest that you turned her out and she went to your mother-in-law's house at midnight and was refused admission? - She would have had to walk about 200 miles to get to my mother-in•law's house. (Laughter.) To your mother's house? - No. Your mother lives at Widnes ?—Yes. Is it not a fact that she refused to have her ?—lt is not a fact. You think not ?—I am certain of it, absolutely. Are you responsible for the costs in this action?-- Mr. Browne: Don't answer the question. I suggest that you refused to have anything

: to do

===== 0002601_19120622_art0152.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
:

===== 0002601_19120622_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
with the costs of the action, or to be made a party in the case?

Mr Browne : You have got the fact that he is not a party, and you may comment upon that fact. Further questioned witness denied that for a long time his wife had been excitable and nervous. The defendant, Charles Henry Johnstone, builder and contractor, was then called. Be stated that he inspected the house on February 15th. for the first time since the previous July. He denied that there had been constant complaints and that be had refused to pay attention to them. His reason for visiting the house on February lsth was to safeguard his puretion as owner because, as Mr. Lowe held a lease, the house might have been lccked up until the lease expired on July 25th. He gave mitten authority to the foreman joiner to take with him a painter and a bricksetter and to report es to tie state et the hou.ie. When the men had been inside avant half an hour he thought he had better see what they were &Mg, as their time had to be paid for. The front door was open and he walked straight into the kitchen. He had purposely waited until all the furniture had been removed from the house before he took steps to ascertain its condi- tion. Plainitiff was in the kitchen along with the workmen. On looking at the report which the fore- man had made, he saw mention of a broken leaded light and he intended to go upstairs to look at it. As they turned out of the kitchen the foreman was immediately behind him and Mrs. Lowe followed. He (defendant) had got up three or four stabs when the defendant threw herself at his legs alter the fashion of a Rugby footballer tackling a man. (Laughter). He was nearly thrown off his balance and took hold of the handrail with both hands. As soon as he freed one foot from plaintiff's clutches, she grabbed it again, but he gradually mounted the stairs. In the kitchen the plaintiff had aderessed him in tones of abuse and he had found from past experience that the only way to treat her was to ignore her. It was absolutely untrue that he said " it is a loog time since I touched a woman, but I will do so now." He never deliberately kicked her, but during the struggle his feet might have come into contact with some part of tier body. He was in front of the plaintiff the whole of the time they were going upstairs and she continued to grab his legs. He did not use any more violence than was absolutely neces- sary. At the top of the stairs, plaintiff struck him across the bead and knocked his cap off. Be held Ler wrists to prevent her striking him again. When Mr Patten came in, all he said was: "Mr Johnstone, what have you been doing to this woman? " and he (defendant) replied "I have done ni thing." Plaintiff flew at him with the intention of triking• him again, but Mr Patten stepped between them and pushed her in a coiner. Plaintiff shouted cut: "You have b,eu backed from your last place through dishonesty." Mr. Burgis : Is there any truth whatever in that suggestion ?—None at all. How did you come to leave that place ? - The con- cern was in financial difficulties and the managers, of w ham I was one, were paid out. The managing director himself was paid out shortly afterwards and I believe the concern has since been wound up. Mr. Burgis put in a testimonial to the defendant from the secretary of the company. Mr. Browne : We have not said anything about dishonesty. Mr. Burgis : During the whole of the time when this incident occurred, did you lose your temper ? Not a bit.

Cross-examined by Mr. Browne, defendant said that prior to a few years ago he had not had expo; i- ence of building houses, hut he had had building experience in connection with a works. Asked %%hv he visited the house on February I iith, defendant said he had heard rumours that Mr. Lowe was not going to pay any more rent and, according to his agreement, he had a right to enter the house. Mr. Browne: Da you mean that you, a strong man, were on the stairs, that she got Ii Id of your les and you did not rebel at all ?—All I wanted to do was to get my feet free. . .

Why couldn't you come downstairs instead of going up ?—lf I had come downstairs, I shoutd have had to trample upon a woman. If you bad wanted to get rid of her and she was pulling you back, all you had to do was to come back with her ?—Not a bit of it. We should both have tumbled downstairs.

Why do you suggest shethrew herself upon youlike a Rugby footballer? You don't suggest be Rayed football ?—Not a bit of it. I don't care whether she played football or not. It is the nearest desctiptiou I could give of what took place. Questioned as to what he meant in referring to his "past expetience " of the plaintiff, the defendant recalled an occasion when she ran alter him shoutirg excitedly about a leak in the back window. He could not give other particular instances. Mr. Browne: That amounts to absolutely nothing. Asked by his Honour why the plaintiff's thumb bled, defendant said it was conceivable that the skin might have been broken through grabbing a boot lace or the tab of the boot.

James Ifelsby, joiner employed by the defendant, gave a similar version of the incident and said the , plaintiff abused the defendant. There was no truth in the statement that the defendant pushed the plaintiff down on the stairs and trampled upon her. Mrs. Lowe stiuck the defendant twice and made several other attempts to strike him. Walter Unsworth, painter employed by the defend- ant, partially corroborated this statement. Dr. E. Fox said he made an examination of the plaintiff on May 15th. Her general condition was not such as could have been ascribed to shock, but was due to natural causes. He found no evidence of extreme nervousness. He had not sufficient opportunity to express an opinion as to whether plaintiff was a highly-strung Woman. There were traces of a bruise on the thigh. Mr. Burgis intimated that he would like to call two witnesses to speak as to the defendants' character, but His Honour field that there was no necessity to do so when defendant's character was not directly impugned. Mr. Browne: Men with the best of characters sometimes lose their temper. Mr. Burgis, addressing his Honour for the defendant, said the case was of far more importance to him than the mere money involved. The defendant's character for manliness and chivalry was called into question and he keenly felt the nature of the attack. His Honour had to decide between two absolutely conflicting stories Defendant did not deny that a certain amount of , physical force had been used, but he submitted that it was not more than than the circumstances required and that it was employed purely in a defensive way. Plaintiff's story was not one which would commend itself to the credence of any ordinary individual. If defendant had wanted to get upstairs and plaintiff had blocked his path in the way described, surely the easiest thing would have been for him to have stepped over her at once. There was no need to push her up step by step, and such procedure would have needed a man of Het culean strength. If plaintiff had been throviu backwards on the stairs, there must have been inquiries to the back or shoulders. It was also peculiar that if plaintiff was being so violently assaulted, she did not shout for help. There was, moreover, no direct corroboration ot her story. The footprint on the dress showed that defendant's foot came into contact with plaintiff's dress, but he submitted that it did 80 involuntarily. If the defendant had not been con- vinced of the justice of his cause, it was only reasonable to assume that he would have made efforts to settle the case. He further submitted that the plaintiff's injuries were only of a very nominal character. Mr. Browne submitted that the plaintiff bad told the more probable and straightforward story, and the fact that she immediately complained of the assault to Mr. Patten and that he called the defendant a cur was corroboration to some extent. The plaintiff's case had been amply made out, and she was entitled to substan- tial damages for an outrageous assault. His Honour reserved his judgment until July 4tll.

"I AM VERY GRATEFUL FOR THE FREE PRESCRIPTION."

THERE IS NOTHING LIKE IT FOR PAIN

" It wal a lucky day for me when I saw in your columns the free prescription that cured Mr. Williams. At the time I was down with influenza, and aching in every bone in my body. One doctor said the pains were Neuralgia, while at the hospital they told me they were Neuritis. Taking medicine was agony to me as it so affected my heart. I had the prescription filled the same night, and in the morning I was up. my cold was gone, my heart normal, and though I had a little pain during the day, I have not had it since. I gave it to my mate, and he tells rue that he has lost his Itheumatistn and Lumbago- " The prescripti, n was this : Go to the nearest chemist's shop and get 60 grains of Kephaldol in tablet form. Take two tablets at once, and then one every hour until the pain is gone. I took three when I went to bed, and broke up my cold."—Mr. Johnson Hicks, Newcastle:

===== 0002601_19120622_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
[Photo Burrell.

A SNAPSHOT AT OULTON. The Lodge-keeper's children being interviewed by cripples. The one in the crate has a tendency to ramble.

How monotonous and how dreary would be the lives of the poor crippled children of Warrington if it were not tor such societies as the Bold-street Cripples' Guild, which have for their primary object the lessening of the burden which these unfortu- nates have to bear. Debarred through their afflictions from entering into the games and pastimes of their able-bodied brothers and sisters, these children of misfortune eagerly look f rward to anything in the way of a treat arranged for their edification, and what could suit their childish hearts more than a icing motor drive in the country, crowned with a capital tea and entertainments ? The third motor trip promoted by the Guild took place on Thurs- day, the venue being Oulton Park, the beautiful seat of Sir Philip Grey Egerton,and the outing was a highly delightful one. Local motorists are ever willing to render their assistance, and evidence in support of this statement was seen outside the Bold-street schools on Thursday noun when 35 handsome automobiles and a large cha-a-banc were placed at the disposal of the children. The cars were soon filled and a start was made at 1.30 and the long procession headed by the pilot car under the direction of Mr J Hamblett proceeded via Acton Bridge, Weaverham Gate, and Cwidington,—places all noted for their picturesque scenery—to the beautiful park at Oulton which was thrown open entirely to the visitors. Sir Philip's agent, Mr. Newton, kindly allowed the party to go over and inspect the fine old house and the visitors thoroughly appreciated the privilege. The trees in the park are among the finest in Cheshire and looked absolutely at their best, the recent rains having brought them out in all their glory and magnificence. To see the deer darting from glade to glade with all the activity of their kind, and to hear the birds warbling their song of happiness amidst such beautiful surroundings was

indeed entrancing to the young folks whose daily surroundings are so drab and monotonous. After having viewed as many interesting sights as possible the children were entertained with a Punch and Judy show. Mr. Sam Aukland caused much merriment with his humour, and his con- certina performances were, as usual, first class. Mr. Alfred Dutton also added to the enjoyment of the afternoon with his humorous recitals. By this time the children were quite ready to do justice to the excellent tea which was laid out for them in front of the hall, the provisions having been borne from Warrington in a van kindly lent by the Alliance Box Co. It was a pleasant sight to see the youngsters assembled on the lawn enjoying themselves to the full, and the officials who had the arrangements in hand and whose names are appended must have been highly satisfied. _

The members- of the Executive Committee who had charge of the outing were: Messrs. Williamson (president), Hamblett (vice- president), Harris, Whitmore, Hickman, Twiss, Bennett, A. Saunders, and Misses Weaver and Dring. Mr. Twiss proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. 13ellian who had arranged for the motor cars. Mr. Hickman proposed a vote of thanks to Sir Philip Grey Egerton, and his agent, and these were accorded in a hearty manner as was also the one to the motor car owners, proposed by Mr. Williamson. The executive wish to thank all friends who had helped to make the affair a success by gifts of money and goods. The return journey was commenced at 6.50 and Warrington was reached about 8 o'clock, a halt having been made at Hill Cliffe, and the children, though somewhat tired; hid that which they alone could give to the officials, the smile of gratitude and thankfulness for such a pleasant and memorable outing.

THE CYCLIST'S NOTE-BOOK.

[By " Silver Street."]

" Perplexed " writes : "I visit your town on occasion, end geuetally a-wheel. As I approacherl the Mersey Bridge I espied a policeman standing at the point where two roads converge, in the most awkward spot that he could possibly have chosen, As I got nearer he pointed to some place on the ground, and otherwise paid no attention to my ring. I proceeded over the network of tram lines and boles, only to perceive another man in blue, who in response to my ring also pointed at the ground ; getting round him almost caused me to skid on the exposed rail and try conclusions with a lorry and a motor car both squeezing with me through the gorge. I asked a pedestrian what these men were for and was told they are on "point duty" and gazing round I was horrified to see a bronze statue with sword and book ,1 imitating the policemen. I fled, sir, at the nightmare What meaneth all this pointing out, or is it the officers' conception of their duty at a dangerous cross road ? Point-duty is surely not to obstruct the traffic but to regulate it. Please solve this mystery. I pass these three gentlemen four times a day and and although cyclists are not obstructed now so much as they were suon after the hoardings were put up, yet the roadfarers are greatly bothered especially by the officer on the Cheshire side, who might place himself where he could direct the traffic instead of helping still more to block up the way.

===== 0002601_19120622_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
A HEALTHY CLUB.

A turn out of 31 for a Saturday afternoon ri,le of thirty miles cannot be considered bad in these degenerate days for cycling clubs. Such was the strength of the Wesleyans last Saturday, to Pine Tree Cottage, Lower Peover, and four more turned in later having come by motor. Mrs. Hoyle's excellent rest- house ought to be far better known to Warrington wheelers, for everything has been provided for the convenience of them and other tourists, either for the day or for longer. Following the main road from Knutsford to Middle- wich, Pine Tree Cottage is on the left just a mile past Peover Brock, and simply bears the notice, "Teas." Not a single mishap marred either journey and the weather suited almost to perfection. After tea a social was held in the large room, and Miss Hipkiss, Miss Kershaw, Miss Ward, and Mr S Winatanley sang, recitations and readings being given by Mr A Dutton, Mr S Johnson and the Captain who also took the chair. Mr. Dutton was recalled for his rendering of Kipling's " Gunga Deep," and gave an impersonation of Wilkins Micawber as encore. , Miss (Jr. Lawton made au excellent accompanist. The return through Lostock and Budworth was commenced at .7.45. The only shower occurred at Hulme Mill, where shelter was taken and relieved by a sing-song, and Stockton Heath was reached at 9.45.
