A TOUR, IN CORK AND KERRY.

It took three days to accomplish the round which shall form the subject of my penultimate article. For five miles the road was almost as bad as at Killarney, but for the next three we rode through the grounds of Dromore Castle, then undergoing renovation. It was here we bad our first experience of fuchsia hedges; they were being trimmed and the beautiful purple bells were lying in masses on the ground, where, in England we should have been obligod to walk for fear of punctures. We rejoined the main road at Blackwater Bridge. Seventy feet below is a foaming torrent and after a hundred yards course it is the salt sea. All along these 41) miles the Kenmare Bay is studded with purple islands and the rocks are thickly hung with seaweed that is taken by hundreds of tons on to the fields for manure. At Parknasilla we turned inland for a few miles, but at Castle Cove we were down at the edge again with splendid views of the rocky headlands that make all that coast so formidable.

The road surface now for miles is splendid. It ,dodges in and out round round the coastline, and your outlook is the open sea with hundreds of islands, and the great Atlantic, next stop America. At Cahetdaniel, a little postal village at the head of Darrynane Bay we found accommodation of a sort for two nights, and visited Staigue Fort a pre-historic fortress, Darrynane, the home of Daniel O'Connell (we had tea at the pretty hotel that " Knklos " of the " Daily News " goes wild over)l land St. Finan's ruined Abbey on an island in the bay. The third day commenced with riding the Coomakista Pus 7001 t., the gradient perfect with a splendid run. down to Waterville where many of the 'newer i Americas cables come ashore n Ballinskelligs Bay. That was our "farthest west." We had a westerly for 16 miles with gale at our back up the Inny valley

===== 0002601_19120622_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
bogs on all sides. The road was kill excellent till it jumped at the Macgillycuddy Reeks. It clears the Ballaghisheen Pass at one leap, but we didn't, it was a painful push to the top 800 ft„ with many a backward view over the 16 miles to the sea through a crystal atmosphere, one that is so far from enervating that you can do twice as much exertion as at Warrington. At the top comes the look forward across the Carah Valley with Carantual, Ireland's monarch, always cloud-coverd, blocking the way towards Killarney. Another nine miles and we struck our third pa e-; that day, the Ballaghbeama, KOft„ and reached Kenniare, GI miles, through a procession of market carts which were all over the road, as almost every driver was drunk. Before you call them for it, please remember what I said about public-houses in Kenmare. British rule in Ireland is absolutely impossible, as I shall show in my next and last article. Lamp time to-night, 9.42 ; next Saturday, 9.37.

THE OLD ENGLISH FAIR.

With happy reccollections of the Old English Fair held in Victoria Park in 1910, Warringtoniaus are looking forward with considerable pleasure to the second venture of the kind, in aid of the Infirmary Million Penny Fund, which will take place in the same park on July 11th, 12th, and 13th. It is expected that, given real summer weather, the net profit of 1,550 in 1910, when 45,650 persons paid for admission, will be exceeded. A multitude of attrac.. tions has been secured. In addition to all the tradi- tional amusements of the fair-ground, there will be entertainments by the Park Pierrots, with Mr. Alf Grounds as general manager and Mr. W, W. Whittle in charge of the stage. Madame Lily Fairhurst, Mr, J E Hannan and many other well-known artistes have offered their services. "Dancing on the green " will he indulged in to the strains of Messrs Crosfield's Prize Band on the first evening and the Pioneer Prize Band on Friday and Saturday evenings. Aviation will be a unique attraction at the fair, the " Daily Mail" airman having arranged to alight in the park on the Friday and to give exhibitions. An aerial post will be established—that is customers will be able to purchase postcards and post them on the ground, and the aviator will convey them to his next stopping place where they will be posted. The rifle range will again afford would-be "crack shots " an opportunity to test their marksmanship, and under the superintendence of the L. & N.-W. Railway Institute Rifle Club, competitions for ladies, the Church Lads' Brigade, Boy Scouts, civilians, the M.P.P. Committee and works teams have been arranged, the winners of which will receive medals and prizes. Inquiries should be addressed to Mr J Davenport, Mr A W Allman, or Mr W G Lunt, at the L & N.- N. Railway Institute, Mr J J A Parfitt, whose motor boat on the river was very popular at the last fair, has again promised to place it at the disposal of the promoters, who will &range fzequent trips on the Mersey. Several interesting competitions have been arranged. Ten valuable prizes are offered for the nearest estimates of the number of persons who will pay for admission to the fair, and each intending competitor should at once purchase the requisite penny ticket and make his or her "guess." Mr. Henry Hope of 68, Hood-lane, has the competition in hand. Another feature which promises to he popular is the doll show. There are six classes, and in each of these prizes are offered for the best-dressed doll, and the I most original style of costume. The arrangements for the fair are in the hands of an able committee, with Mr J II Smethurst as chair- man and Mr H Collin as hon. secretary.

Kissing and Disease.—Following the lead of Sir William Broadbent, the doctors have been condemning the habit of indiscriminate kissing. Tuberculosis they say, is nearly always caused by expectoration or by kissing, and the presence of a You,ng person suffering from consumption at a children party is a serious danger. No doubt such diseases as influenza, measles and phthisis would be restricted, if kissing were abolished, hut no one can be sanguine of seeing this reform an accomplished fact. In Elizabethan days any man who laid claim to good breeding, greeted a lady by a kiss on the mouth. In time a re-action against indiscriminate kissing set in, but the sTstdm, according to medical ideas, the pulling down of insanitary areas, the proper ventilation of offices, .shops, public buildings, and pnvate houses and other !sanitary measures, such as the avoidance of overcrowding, are comparatively easy matters.

Stockton Heath Walking Day.

Sir,—l would esteem it a favour if you would grant me a small space in your valuable paper on behalf of the above. lam pleased to coincide with" Father of One" and I think it ig about time we haa one big affair on Walking Day, there are many children in the village who have to no satisfied with this one day. I think it is every parent's duty to uphold this Sunday School demonstration, and not to let it be such a half- hearted affair as we see it. Now I would like to ask " Father of One " how this could be brought about? Could it be brought about by a meeting of the parents, or does he think the parent church ought to open the ball or the smaller churches approach the parent church. For myself, I think the parent Church ought to be the parent, and step out in a way which will bring about a very pleasant day for the children. "Father of One" will have the thanks of a good many for having the courage to come out with a suggestion in the way he has, as I have of ten heard it talked about. I hope these few lines will bring matters a step nearer. I would be pleased to meet "Father of One" and help to give the children a united day which they would have occasion to remember.—Yours,

===== 0002601_19120622_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
Do Strikes Benefit the Workers ?

Sir,—Whilst anyone who is interested in the uplift- ing of the working class may be pleased that they are making it evident that they are not satisfied with the existing order of things, I think we cannot afford to let their methode pass without comment, averse or otherwise. Th most popular method at present used we all know to be the strike and for myself, as a workingman, after experiencing the tremendous strikes of the past twelve months and weighing up the effect of them from the point of view of bettering the workers' condition, I feel that the strike is far from the right way to give us a better existence. Without entering iuto details, I say strikes to raise wages at all are futile even if the worker can win every time. I believe that the capitalist class can by the manipulation of prices or the adulteration of commodities or by more efficient organisation always neutralise any benefits the worker may receive from strikes To better their conditions the workers must in my belief adopt constructive and fundamental methods. It is no USA npprOaChli g these ptoblems from the rurface. Cer- tainly on the face of it it appears that all that is wanted is more wages, but in reality what we as workers want is more and better food, more and better houses, more and better clothes. more leisure for the enjoyment of art, literature, music, and not the worse than animal existence that we have now. I make this last contention because I really believe that animals are better treated than the workingclass. Certainly their market value is greater. The root cause of the poverty of the workers is the fact that we have allowed anybody to organise the supply of our material needs, and those needs are only supplied by the people who do it in order to obtain that margin known as profit. And I ask anyone, do they really think that so long as we have the supply of our clothes, our food, our houses manipulated for private profit, we shall have the whole of the people properly housed and properly cared for generally ? The iaising of wages does not come near touching the question. The railway men ask and strike for a rise in their wages They get it Immediately after the railway companies ask and get sanction to raise the price of travelling to the general public. In a certain district, before the general miners' strike some colliers came out on strike and were successful in getting a 2s. advance Immediately the price of bread went up a halfpenny a loaf in that particular district." Well" our striking friend will ask, "If striking to get better wages will not help us, what will ? " That is a perfectly legitimate question and ought to be answered: Well. as I said before, the root of evil is in the means of livelihood being owned by private individuals for their enrich- ment and not for the general good, and the workers must adopt a policy that will bring the sources of wealth to their hands. Some of us believe that if the people would send men to Parliament and to the local Councils who believe in this policy, it would soon be done. But that seems a weed way off. The workers are divided into Liberals and Tories as yet and send the owners of property to these places so that we may rely upon it that nothing will be done through that source as yet. This is where I think trade unions would come in useful. I have come to the belief that when the people want a thing they can have it. The workers want better houses and better food, and it has become my belief that if trade unions would adopt a constructive policy by, in the first place initiating a scheme for the housing of the I workers upon a decent scale, a commencement would be really made towards the betterment of the worker's condition. What is there to stop them from say, housing about one-thousand of the working class for a start. Money could be easily borrowed to start the scheme of buying land and material &c, which could soon be paid back. Once you have them housed what is there to atop them from having their own communal milk supply and their bread supply and their own coal supply? With the supplies owned and controlled and organised on a proper basis they could eliminate all the waste labour which only runs up the cost of foodstuffs &c. They could rzet thews at the natural cost of prodaction without all the charges of parasitic interests. There would be no profits to pay to the idle shareholders, the middlemen or the cost of advertisements. I offer this as a serious suggestion to the local Trade Unionists, Friendly Societies and also the Co-operative Society. They do invest money in productive enterprises, why not Invest in a scheme of this kind? With better houses and surroundings, with better food and clothing, we should soon have a better people to understand the great national ques- tions needed to complete the scheme already begun. At any rate I have come to the conclusion that we need not wait for Parliament or Town Councils. And I have certainly come to the conclusion that if Trade Unions would use their energies in this direction rather than in the present way, the result in the long run would be far better.—Yours truly, LABOR 01INIA. VINCIT.

International Competition Abroad. Sir,—For inaby years past we have heard—from our fathers, and other sources—how strenuously trade in foreign lands must be competed for and how narrow is Ow margin by which we maintain our hold upon oversea business. So far as I am aware, no serious attempt has ever been made to measure these generalisations against specific records of fact and to note the results. Such a comparison indeed is by. no means easy. To say nothing of language difficulties, it is well-nigh impossible to obtain, for all couutries, exact data in sufficient detail. Periods of record differ in different lands. Fiscal years in most coun- tries coincide with the calendar. In other instances March :31st is the closing day, and for a further series, June 30th. Again, goods fall under groupings in countries whither they go, differing widely from those used in the lands whence they come. To compare, therefore, British recorded ea2Ports with the official import lists of other countries is both very difficult and unavoidably inexact, even if we disregard the fact that nearly always values of goods are given when received as c.i.f. and when despatched as f.o.b. Nevertheless, a,painstaking student of these matters may find justly comparable data which cover a large slice of British Commerce. Two Government publica- tions (Cd 5808 for British India and Cd 5906 for other parts of the Empire) give us detailed lists of imports into the various British possessions. Particulars regarding sources of supply are also furnished. From a careful study of these details we may learn how different countries shared the business among them. Taking British India for example, we find that, in the last year given (March 31st, 1910,) India imported goods worth £81,766,000. Great Britain supplied 4:52,353,000 worth or 64 per cent., Germany, Holland Belgium, France, Austria, Italy and the United States

HOPE COLLECTING SOCIETY Established 1868. Registered Office, 13 ARPLEY ST.4 WARRINGTON. The Hope Collecting society, one of the oldest collecting societies in Warrington, has decided to become an APPROVED SOCIETY for Males and Females. Its members have full control of the management. The Committee is composed of local men who have had a long experience of Friendly Society Work and its management. Agents Wanted, liberal terms to capable men and women. Further information and application forms may be obtained from any of the Society's agents, or om Was. 1311313 Y, Secretary. If you desire to join a Society and be able to approach as Officials and Committee with the least possible trouble JOIN THE HOPE coLLEeriNG SOCIETY

-WARRINGTON UNION.—SUPERINTENDENT SCHOOLMASTER AND MATRON of COTTAGE HOMES AND SCHOOLS. The Guardians of the Warrington Union require at their Cottage Homes and Schools a HEAD SCHOOL- MASTER, who will undertake also the duties of SUPERINTENDENT, and a MATRON. The persons appointed to be husband and wife. The Salary as Superintendendent will be £4O per annum and as Schoolmaster £6O per annum. The Salary of the Matron will be £4O per annum. in both cases with apartments, rations and washing. The appointments will be made subject to the sanction of the Local Govetoment Board and the provisions of the Poor Law Officers Superannuation Act, 1896 (for which purpose the emoluments are valued at £5O per annum in each case). Application forms can be obtained from the under- signed upon forwarding stamped addressed envelope (Foolscap size), and the same must be returned to me duly completed and endorsed " Application for Superintendent, &o." not later thanlo a.m. on Tuesday, the 2nd July, 1912. Canvassing the Guardians, directly or indirectly, will be deemed a disqualification. By Order, Union Offices, ARTHUR BOTTOMLEY, Be weey Chambers, (Clerk). Warrington, 19th June, 1912.

National Insurance Pict. In order to secure the full benefits of the National Insurance act you must join an Approved Society. Have you applied for membership ? If not, apply at once to the nearest PRUDENTIAL ASSURANCE AGENT for a proposal for member- ship of The Prudential Approved Society No difficulties in regard to change of residence will arise for members of this Society, for it will have representatives In every part of the United Kingdom. All the profits of the Society go to the Members There is no other Society that can give better bene- fits under the Act, or offer greater advantages to its members than the Prudential Approved Society. COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON. PROCESSION OF SUNDAY SCHOLARS, . ON FRIDAY, 28TH JUNE, 1912. By virtue of the powers given in that behalf by the Towns Police Clauses Act, 1847, and by order of the Town Council. I DO HEREBY GIVE NOTICE that in order to I prevent accidents on the above occasion no CART, CARRIAGE. TRAMCAR, MOTOR CAR, LURRY, or other VEHICLE (excepting vehicles taking part in the IProcession and Mailcarts), and no HORSE or other lanimal will be allowed to remain in or travel along or over any of the undermentioned Streets between the hours of 9 a.m. and 12 30 p.m.' viz. : Arpley Street Kendrick Street Barbauld Street Legh Street Rath Street Museum Street Bewsey Street Palmyra Square Bold Street Queen Street Bridge Street Rolleston Street Butterrnarket Street Rylanda Street Cairo Street Sankey Street Church Street Scotland Road Edgeworth Street Springfield Street Friars' Gate Sues Street Froghall Lane Tanners Lane Golborne Street Winmarleigh Street Hanover Street Winvrick Street Horsemarket Street BICYCLES and TRICYCLES are Included in tlie term "vehicle," and must not be RIDDEN through any of the above-named streets during the hours speci- fied. Any persons wilfully acting in contravention of the above Order will be liable to a PENALTY not exceed- ing 40s. Dated this Fifth day of June, 1912. GEORGE WILLIAM JOSEPH, Town Hall, Warrington. Mayor, N.B.—The above Order will cease to apply to any of the above-mentioned streets as soon as the procession In its proper order has paeqe4 along any such streets.

Warrington has now lost all chances of being repre- sented in the final stages of tha Lancashire Amateur Ass-ciation's Championship and the Crovfield Cup, for on Saturday Warrington Cemetery team were defeated by Leigh Railway at the Angel Hotel, Sleet- ford, and Whiteen ss lost to Irlam-olh-'lleightl at Kersal Moor. Both the local teams were well represented, and put up a good fight with their opponents. -- CEMETERY HOTEL V LEIGH RAILWAY. The local trundlers had exceptionally hard luck, for despite the fact that they won equally as many individual games as their opponents—six—their total score was four points behind that of the Railwaymen. T Wood (Leigh) opened well for his aide. He defeated 11 Antrobus by 21-6 in 21 ends after having led from the third end. T Bolton (Cemetery) was in fine form and disposed of A Newton by 21-16 after having been 2-5, 6-8, 6-10 and 9-10 in arrears. Bolton gained his le ad at the 20th end and maintained the advantage to the finish. W J Woods (Cemetery) ran a tight game with J Rothwell and after having peeled %jai 14 each at the 20th end, the Cemetery man hid a good run to the 26th end when he won by 21-15. ---- W Barton has shown good form lately and much was expected of him rn Saturday when he met .3 G undy (Leigh). The players kept together all the way but Barton bad a slight advantage in the 18h to 32nd ends. when the game was 20-19 in his favone. Grundy peeled on the next length and there was is great amount of excitement when the list jack was thrown. Barton found himself on a good length and got home by a single point. After several games had been decided in favour of each team in turn E Cleave (Cemetery) took op the jack against J Ken 3 on. Cleave had a good run from the beginning and at the 18th end he led by 15-9. Ken3on, however, gradually drew tearer until at the 29th end the pla3ers players peeled with 18 each. This proved to be Gleaye's last score and with three singles Kenyon returned the winning card. -- Although A Ford (Cemetery) wan over S Hall he had DO easy thing on and he W.IS only the victor by two points in 31 ends. The Church-street trundler lei up to the 26th end, when Hall got in a good wood and g deed a lead of 18-17' Ford equalised on the next length and at the 29th end the game was 19 each. Ford, however, got two good tingles on the next two ends and won 21-19. The last game was keen- ly watched for A F, e'er (Core etery) was up against J Keeley and at this juncture the Cemetery team had a lead of two points on the match. Keeley played good bowls and try as he would Fo-ster could not hold him. At the 28th end the game was only 17-15 in favour of the railway men but Forster failed to get in another chalk and with a double and two singles Keeley got home on the 311.5 t end and thus gave his side a majority on the whole match of bur. -- WIIITECROSS V. irmAat-o'nt-itemiers. T 1 e captain of the Whitecross team soon set a good example by disposing of J Clayton by 21-lb after 26 (suds. Appleton got a good single on the next length and a neat double on the 29th end gave him the victory by 21-19. S Massey (Whitecross) only lost to A Bennett by the odd one after the game tad extended over 33 ends. The players peeled with 8, 10, 12, 16, 17, and at the 32nd end the game was 20 each. Bennett got the einnining wood in by inches. WARRINGTON CEMETERY 2ND V ROEBUCK 2ND. The Warrington Cemetery 2nd team gave a very creditable performance against Roebeck 2nd at Leigh in the third round of the Association Competition second teams championship for they came home with a 39 point victory to their credit. --- There wore only four of the Roebuck team who won and the Cemetery men were undoubtedly the superior expenents of the game. W Swanton started well by disposing of E Steer (Roebuck) by 21-16 in 25 ends, ,N Fielding (Cemetery) and J Melbourne kept this up by defeating B Dickinson and J Parker by 21-14 and 21-18 respectively. -- F Yarwood (Cemetery) had hard lines in his game with G Archer for he only lost by three points after a very hard game. Archer led by 16-8 at the 17th end but Yarwood played well and at the 29th end Archer only led by 19-18 but he got his necessary two on the nest length and won by 3 points. C Smith (Cemetery) gained a line victory over J Fletcher by 21-10 but R Knight had not such an easy thing on with N Rainford for he only won by the odd point. The score was even up to the last end when after having peeled with 20 in the previous end Knight got in the decisive wood and won by the odd chalk. The Cemetery men did exceedingly well and entered the 4th round with plenty of confidence. Warrington and District Association. As we prophesied last week, Fearnhead gave the Boll's Head men a hot time on the Farmer's Arms green on Saturday. They bad a heavy score to wipe out, and they had set their mind on doing it. -- J Cleave (Bull's Head) was one of the four men to win for his side and he did so over H Ratcliffe by 21-15 after 28 ends. J Hewitt (Fearnhehd) soon made amends when he finished off B Regan by 21-9 after 22 ends had been contested. Roberts was in his usual good form and On his own land he takes some shifting, and this J Hands soon found out. Tha other games with the exception of three were good wins for the Fearnhead team who only lost on the aggregate by eight points,they having won on Saturday by 50 points. -- CONSOLATION COMPETITION. SENIOR CONSERVATIVES V. PENKETII. In the consolation competition Senior Conservatives beat Penketh by 56 points on the Sankey-street green and thus reduced the aggregate score of the villagers to one of a six point win. The villagers only won on four individual games but on most of the others they ran very close. The Conservatives were bound to give a better dis- play on their own green than; they did the previous Saturday at Penketh and their win of 56 points was expected by the Sankey-street supporters. On the aggregate Penketh won by points.

MEDICAL TREATMENT IN THE SCHOOLS. The Government now promises to give grants for the medical treatment of school children, and accordingly' (a correspondent writes) the London County Council Education Committee proposes to adopt a complete scheme by which a quarter of a million scholars will receive treatment every year. Accordingly the present series of' schemes are to he re-organised and co-ordinated, and 21 full-time doctors are to be appointed, with four divisional officers to take charge. The doctors are to be paid £4OO a year, rising to £:t00. Grants will be made to schools for tubercular children and others needing the open-air treatment ; and though no actual promise is made, consideration vrill be given to application for grants for the provision of spectacles and surgical appliances, and also for treatmect by private practitioners. The scheme is to be so complete that the medical treatment will necessarily have to he followed up in the homes. The scheme, in brief, means that the State and municipality make themselves responsible for the health of the childien who go to school.
